\begin{proposition}[Layer cake formula]\label{measure-theory:proposition-layer-cake}
Let $\mu$ be a $\sigma$-finite measure on $X$ and $f \colon X \to [0, \infty]$ measurable. Then
\[
\int_Xf\,d\mu = \int_0^\infty\mu(\{f > t\})\,dt.
\]
More generally, if $\psi \colon [0, \infty) \to [0, \infty)$ is Borel measurable and $\phi(s) = \int_0^s\psi(t)\,dt$ (with $\phi(\infty) = \int_0^\infty\psi$), then
$\int_X\phi \circ f\,d\mu = \int_0^\infty\psi(t)\,\mu(\{f > t\})\,dt$; in particular $\int_Xf^p\,d\mu = p\int_0^\infty t^{p-1}\mu(\{f > t\})\,dt$ for $p > 0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The set $E = \{(x, t) \in X \times [0, \infty) : t < f(x)\}$ is measurable for the product $\sigma$-algebra, because $t < f(x)$ holds exactly when $t < q < f(x)$ for some rational
$q$, so $E = \bigcup_{q \in \QQ_{>0}}\{f > q\} \times [0, q)$. Apply Tonelli's theorem (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-fubini}(1)) to the non-negative measurable function
$(x, t) \mapsto \psi(t)\mathbf{1}_E(x, t)$ on $X \times [0, \infty)$ with $\mu \times \lambda$. Integrating first in $t$ gives $\int_0^{f(x)}\psi(t)\,dt = \phi(f(x))$, integrating first in
$x$ gives $\psi(t)\mu(\{f > t\})$. The first formula is the case $\psi = 1$, and the last one the case $\psi(t) = pt^{p-1}$.
\end{proof}

The name describes the picture: the region under the graph of $f$ is cut into horizontal layers $\{f > t\}$ of thickness $dt$. As an application we show that the maximal
function of an $L^p$ function is in $L^p$ for $p > 1$, although it is never in $L^1$ (Exercise~\ref{measure-theory:exercise-maximal-not-integrable}).

\begin{corollary}[Hardy--Littlewood maximal theorem]\label{measure-theory:corollary-maximal-lp}
For $1 < p \leq \infty$ and $f \in L^p(\RR^n)$, $\|Mf\|_p \leq C_{n,p}\|f\|_p$, with $C_{n,\infty} = 1$ and $C_{n,p}^p = 2^p3^np/(p - 1)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For $p = \infty$, every average of $|f|$ is at most $\|f\|_\infty$. Let $p < \infty$ and $t > 0$, and split $f = f_1 + f_2$ with $f_1 = f\mathbf{1}_{\{|f| > t/2\}}$. Then $f_1 \in L^1$,
since $|f_1| \leq (2/t)^{p-1}|f|^p$, and $Mf \leq Mf_1 + Mf_2 \leq Mf_1 + t/2$, so $\{Mf > t\} \subseteq \{Mf_1 > t/2\}$, and by
Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-maximal-inequality}, $\lambda(\{Mf > t\}) \leq \frac{2 \cdot 3^n}{t}\int_{\{|f| > t/2\}}|f|$. By
Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-layer-cake} and Tonelli's theorem,
\[
\int(Mf)^p = p\int_0^\infty t^{p-1}\lambda(\{Mf > t\})\,dt \leq 2 \cdot 3^np\int|f(x)|\int_0^{2|f(x)|}t^{p-2}\,dt\,d\lambda(x) = \frac{2 \cdot 3^np}{p - 1}\int|f|(2|f|)^{p-1},
\]
which is $\frac{2^p3^np}{p - 1}\|f\|_p^p$.
\end{proof}

\section{Change of variables}\label{measure-theory:section-change-of-variables}

\begin{lemma}\label{measure-theory:lemma-linear-change}
For $T \in \GL_n(\RR)$ and Borel $E \subseteq \RR^n$, $\lambda(T(E)) = |\det T|\,\lambda(E)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Both sides are multiplicative in $T$, and every invertible matrix is a product of \emph{elementary} matrices (by Gaussian elimination): permutations of coordinates, scalings of one coordinate by
$c \neq 0$, and shears $x_i \mapsto x_i + cx_j$ ($i \neq j$). Permutations and scalings map boxes to boxes, multiplying volumes by $|\det T|$, so they multiply outer measure by $|\det T|$. A shear preserves each
line parallel to the $i$-th axis and acts on it by a translation; by Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-fubini} and Remark~\ref{measure-theory:remark-lebesgue-product}, integrating the one-dimensional
measures of these sections shows $\lambda(T(E)) = \lambda(E)$, and $\det T = 1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Change of variables]\label{measure-theory:theorem-change-of-variables}
Let $U, V \subseteq \RR^n$ be open and $\phi \colon U \to V$ a $C^1$-diffeomorphism. For every measurable $f \colon V \to [0, \infty]$, and every $f \in L^1(V)$,
\[
\int_Vf\,d\lambda = \int_Uf(\phi(x))\,|\det D\phi(x)|\,d\lambda(x).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We use the norm $\|x\|_\infty$ and the corresponding operator norm; the mean value inequality (Proposition~\ref{real-analysis:proposition-derivative-basic}(4)) holds for it with the same proof. Cubes are balls
$Q = \{x : \|x - a\|_\infty \leq h\}$.

\emph{Step 1.} Let $Q \subseteq U$ be a cube with centre $a$ and $T \in \GL_n(\RR)$, and let $M = \sup_{y \in Q}\|T^{-1}D\phi(y)\|$. By the mean value inequality applied to $T^{-1}\phi$,
$\phi(Q) \subseteq \phi(a) + T(\{\|z\|_\infty \leq Mh\})$, so $\lambda(\phi(Q)) \leq |\det T|M^n\lambda(Q)$ by Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-linear-change}.

\emph{Step 2: $\lambda(\phi(Q)) \leq \int_Q|\det D\phi|$ for cubes $Q \subseteq U$.} Let $\varepsilon > 0$. Since $D\phi$ is uniformly continuous on $Q$ and $\|D\phi^{-1}\|$ is bounded there, we can divide $Q$ into
subcubes $Q_j$ with centres $x_j$ such that $\|D\phi(x_j)^{-1}D\phi(y)\| \leq 1 + \varepsilon$ for $y \in Q_j$, and $|\det D\phi|$ varies by less than $\varepsilon$ on each $Q_j$. Step 1 with $T = D\phi(x_j)$ gives
$\lambda(\phi(Q)) \leq \sum_j\lambda(\phi(Q_j)) \leq (1 + \varepsilon)^n\sum_j|\det D\phi(x_j)|\lambda(Q_j) \leq (1 + \varepsilon)^n(\int_Q|\det D\phi| + \varepsilon\lambda(Q))$.

\emph{Step 3: $\lambda(\phi(E)) \leq \int_E|\det D\phi|$ for measurable $E \subseteq U$.} An open $W \subseteq U$ is a countable union of cubes with disjoint interiors (dyadic cubes), whose boundaries are null, so the
inequality holds for $W$. Let $E \subseteq K$ with $K \subseteq U$ compact, and $C = \sup$ of $|\det D\phi|$ on a compact neighbourhood $N \subseteq U$ of $K$. By regularity choose open $W$ with $E \subseteq W \subseteq N$ and
$\lambda(W \setminus E) < \delta$; then $\lambda(\phi(E)) \leq \lambda(\phi(W)) \leq \int_E|\det D\phi| + C\delta$. This shows that $\phi$ maps null sets to null sets, hence Lebesgue measurable sets to
Lebesgue measurable sets (a measurable set is a Borel set up to a null set), and gives the inequality for $E$ in a compact subset; exhaust $U$ by compact sets for general $E$.

\emph{Step 4.} By Step 3, $\int_V\mathbf{1}_{\phi(E)} \leq \int_U\mathbf{1}_{\phi(E)} \circ \phi\,|\det D\phi|$; by linearity and monotone convergence, $\int_Vf \leq \int_U(f \circ \phi)|\det D\phi|$ for all measurable
$f \geq 0$. Applying this to $\phi^{-1}$ and the function $(f \circ \phi)|\det D\phi|$ on $U$, and using $D\phi(\phi^{-1}(y))D(\phi^{-1})(y) = 1$, gives the reverse inequality. The $L^1$ case follows by linearity.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}\label{measure-theory:example-gaussian}
With polar coordinates $(r, \theta) \mapsto (r\cos\theta, r\sin\theta)$, a diffeomorphism $(0, \infty) \times (0, 2\pi) \to \RR^2$ minus a closed half-line (a null set), with Jacobian determinant $r$,
Tonelli's theorem gives $(\int_\RR e^{-x^2}dx)^2 = \int_{\RR^2}e^{-\|x\|^2} = \int_0^{2\pi}\int_0^\infty e^{-r^2}r\,dr\,d\theta = \pi$. So $\int_\RR e^{-x^2}\,dx = \sqrt{\pi}$.
\end{example}

Fubini's theorem reduces integration by parts in several variables to one variable. When one of the functions has compact support there is no boundary term.

\begin{proposition}[Integration by parts in $\RR^n$]\label{measure-theory:proposition-integration-by-parts}
Let $f, g \colon \RR^n \to \CC$ be continuously differentiable, and suppose that one of them has compact support. Then for each $i$
\[
\int_{\RR^n}(\partial_if)\,g\,d\lambda = -\int_{\RR^n}f\,\partial_ig\,d\lambda .
\]
Consequently, for a continuously differentiable vector field $F$ and a continuously differentiable function $g$, one of them compactly supported,
$\int\operatorname{div}F\,g\,d\lambda = -\int\langle F, \nabla g\rangle\,d\lambda$, where $\operatorname{div}F = \sum_i\partial_iF^i$ and $\nabla g = (\partial_1g, \ldots, \partial_ng)$; and for $f$ of class $C^2$,
$\int(\sum_i\partial_i^2f)\,g\,d\lambda = -\int\langle\nabla f, \nabla g\rangle\,d\lambda$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The product $h = fg$ is continuously differentiable with compact support, contained in a cube $[-R, R]^n$, and $\partial_ih = (\partial_if)g + f\,\partial_ig$ by the product rule; all these functions are
continuous with compact support, hence integrable. By Fubini's theorem (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-fubini}) we may integrate first in the variable $x_i$, the others being fixed. For fixed
values of the other variables, $t \mapsto h(\ldots, t, \ldots)$ is continuously differentiable and vanishes for $|t| \geq R$, so by the fundamental theorem of calculus
(Theorem~\ref{real-analysis:theorem-ftc}) $\int_{-R}^R\partial_ih\,dx_i = h(\ldots, R, \ldots) - h(\ldots, -R, \ldots) = 0$. Hence $\int\partial_ih\,d\lambda = 0$, which is the formula. For the vector field,
apply it to $F^i$ and $g$ and sum over $i$; for the last formula take $F = \nabla f$.
\end{proof}

The formula says that the operator $\partial_i$ is \emph{anti-self-adjoint} for the $L^2$ inner product on compactly supported functions: $\langle\partial_if, \bar g\rangle = -\langle f, \partial_i\bar g\rangle$. It is the
reason why the derivative of a distribution is defined by $(\partial_iT)(\varphi) = -T(\partial_i\varphi)$ (Chapter~\ref{distributions}), and, applied twice, it shows that $-\sum_i\partial_i^2$ is a
non-negative operator: $\int(-\sum_i\partial_i^2f)\bar f\,d\lambda = \int|\nabla f|^2\,d\lambda \geq 0$ for compactly supported $f$ of class $C^2$.

\subsection{Volumes: Cavalieri's principle and solids of revolution}\label{measure-theory:subsection-volumes}

Fubini's theorem and the change of variables formula contain the classical recipes for computing volumes. We write $\lambda^n$ for Lebesgue measure on $\RR^n$ and call $\lambda^3(E)$ the \emph{volume}
and $\lambda^2(E)$ the \emph{area} of a set. The first recipe slices a solid by parallel planes.

\begin{proposition}[Cavalieri's principle]\label{measure-theory:proposition-cavalieri}
Let $E \subseteq \RR^n = \RR^{n-1} \times \RR$ be a Borel set, and for $t \in \RR$ let $E_t = \{y \in \RR^{n-1} : (y, t) \in E\}$ be its slice at height $t$. Then $t \mapsto \lambda^{n-1}(E_t)$ is measurable and
\[
\lambda^n(E) = \int_\RR\lambda^{n-1}(E_t)\,dt .
\]
In particular, two Borel sets whose slices at every height have the same $(n-1)$-dimensional measure have the same volume.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Apply Tonelli's theorem (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-fubini}(1)) to the indicator function $\mathbf{1}_E$ on $\RR^{n-1} \times \RR$, with $\lambda^n = \lambda^{n-1} \times \lambda^1$ on Borel sets
(Remark~\ref{measure-theory:remark-lebesgue-product}), integrating first over $y$: $\int\mathbf{1}_E(y, t)\,d\lambda^{n-1}(y) = \lambda^{n-1}(E_t)$.
\end{proof}

The principle is named after Bonaventura Cavalieri (1635), who stated it for plane figures and solids, but Archimedes had already used it, and Zu Chongzhi and his son Zu Geng used it in China in the
fifth century to find the volume of the ball.

\begin{example}\label{measure-theory:example-cavalieri}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Cones.} Let $B \subseteq \RR^2$ be a Borel set of finite area $A$ and $h > 0$, and let $C$ be the cone over $B$ with apex $(0, 0, h)$, the union of the segments from the points of $B \times \{0\}$ to the apex.
Its slice at height $t \in [0, h)$ is the scaled copy $(1 - t/h)B$, of area $(1 - t/h)^2A$ by Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-linear-change}. So $\lambda^3(C) = A\int_0^h(1 - t/h)^2\,dt = \frac13Ah$, whatever the shape of the
base: a third of the prism with the same base and height.
\item \emph{Archimedes' ball.} The slice of the half-ball $\{|x| \leq R, x_3 \geq 0\}$ at height $t \in [0, R]$ is a disc of area $\pi(R^2 - t^2)$. The slice of the cylinder $\{x_1^2 + x_2^2 \leq R^2, 0 \leq x_3 \leq R\}$ minus
the cone $\{\sqrt{x_1^2 + x_2^2} < x_3\}$ is an annulus of area $\pi R^2 - \pi t^2$. The slices have equal areas, so the half-ball has the volume of the cylinder minus the cone, $\pi R^3 - \frac13\pi R^3 = \frac23\pi R^3$, and
the ball has volume $\frac43\pi R^3$. This is Archimedes' argument, which he regarded as his finest: the ball is two thirds of the circumscribed cylinder.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

A \emph{solid of revolution} is obtained by rotating a plane region around an axis. We take the $z$-axis in $\RR^3$ as axis, and describe the region in the half-plane $H = \{(r, z) : r > 0\}$ of distances
from the axis and heights. For a Borel set $R \subseteq H$ let
\[
\operatorname{Rev}(R) = \bigl\{(x, y, z) \in \RR^3 : \bigl(\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}, z\bigr) \in R\bigr\}
\]
be the solid swept out by $R$, a Borel set since it is the preimage of $R$ under a continuous map on $\RR^3$ minus the axis.

\begin{proposition}[Volumes of solids of revolution]\label{measure-theory:proposition-revolution-volume}
For every Borel set $R \subseteq H$,
\[
\lambda^3(\operatorname{Rev}(R)) = 2\pi\int_Rr\,d\lambda^2(r, z) .
\]
In particular:
\begin{enumerate}
\item (\emph{Discs}) if $R = \{(r, z) : a \leq z \leq b,\ 0 < r \leq f(z)\}$ for a continuous $f \geq 0$ on $[a, b]$, then $\lambda^3(\operatorname{Rev}(R)) = \pi\int_a^bf(z)^2\,dz$;
\item (\emph{Shells}) if $R = \{(r, z) : a \leq r \leq b,\ 0 \leq z \leq g(r)\}$ for $0 < a < b$ and a continuous $g \geq 0$ on $[a, b]$, then $\lambda^3(\operatorname{Rev}(R)) = 2\pi\int_a^brg(r)\,dr$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Use \emph{cylindrical coordinates} $\phi(r, \theta, z) = (r\cos\theta, r\sin\theta, z)$, a $C^1$-diffeomorphism from $U = (0, \infty) \times (0, 2\pi) \times \RR$ onto $\RR^3$ minus the closed half-plane $\{y = 0, x \geq 0\}$,
which is a null set. Its Jacobian determinant is
\[
\det\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta & -r\sin\theta & 0\\ \sin\theta & r\cos\theta & 0\\ 0 & 0 & 1\end{pmatrix} = r .
\]
Since $\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{Rev}(R)}(\phi(r, \theta, z)) = \mathbf{1}_R(r, z)$, the change of variables formula (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-change-of-variables}) and Tonelli's theorem give
$\lambda^3(\operatorname{Rev}(R)) = \int_U\mathbf{1}_R(r, z)\,r\,d\lambda^3 = \int_0^{2\pi}d\theta\int_Rr\,d\lambda^2 = 2\pi\int_Rr\,d\lambda^2$. For (1), integrate first over $r$: $\int_0^{f(z)}r\,dr = \frac12f(z)^2$. For
(2), integrate first over $z$: $\int_0^{g(r)}dz = g(r)$.
\end{proof}

The two special cases are the ``disc method'' and the ``shell method'' of calculus courses: (1) cuts the solid by planes perpendicular to the axis, which meet it in discs of area $\pi f(z)^2$, and is
also a case of Cavalieri's principle; (2) cuts it into thin cylindrical shells of radius $r$, height $g(r)$ and thickness $dr$, of volume $2\pi rg(r)\,dr$. For the ball, (1) with $f(z) = \sqrt{R^2 - z^2}$
gives $\pi\int_{-R}^R(R^2 - z^2)\,dz = \frac43\pi R^3$ once more.

The integral $\int_Rr\,d\lambda^2$ has a mechanical meaning. The \emph{centroid} of a Borel set $R \subseteq \RR^2$ of finite positive area is the point $(\bar r, \bar z)$ with
$\bar r = \lambda^2(R)^{-1}\int_Rr\,d\lambda^2$ and $\bar z = \lambda^2(R)^{-1}\int_Rz\,d\lambda^2$, its centre of mass for a uniform density. So the proposition can be read as follows.

\begin{corollary}[Pappus--Guldin theorem for volumes]\label{measure-theory:corollary-pappus-volume}
If $R \subseteq H$ has finite positive area and centroid at distance $\bar r$ from the axis, then
\[
\lambda^3(\operatorname{Rev}(R)) = 2\pi\bar r \cdot \lambda^2(R):
\]
the volume of a solid of revolution is the area of the region times the length of the circle described by its centroid.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
$2\pi\int_Rr\,d\lambda^2 = 2\pi\bar r\lambda^2(R)$ by the definition of $\bar r$.
\end{proof}

The theorem was stated by Pappus of Alexandria around 320 and rediscovered by Paul Guldin in 1640; in Italian it is the \emph{teorema di Pappo--Guldino}. It turns volume computations into centroid
computations, and conversely.

\begin{example}\label{measure-theory:example-pappus-volume}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The torus.} Rotating the disc of radius $a$ centred at $(c, 0)$, with $0 < a < c$, gives a solid torus. The centroid is the centre, by symmetry, so the volume is $2\pi c \cdot \pi a^2 = 2\pi^2ca^2$: the
volume of a cylinder of radius $a$ whose length is the central circle of the torus. Computing it with discs requires the integral $\int_{-a}^a\pi\bigl((c + \sqrt{a^2 - z^2})^2 - (c - \sqrt{a^2 - z^2})^2\bigr)dz =
4\pi c\int_{-a}^a\sqrt{a^2 - z^2}\,dz$, which is the same thing, $4\pi c \cdot \frac12\pi a^2$.
\item \emph{The centroid of a half-disc.} Conversely, rotating the half-disc $\{r^2 + z^2 \leq a^2, r > 0\}$ around the $z$-axis gives the ball of radius $a$, so $\frac43\pi a^3 = 2\pi\bar r \cdot \frac12\pi a^2$, and the
centroid of a half-disc lies at distance $\bar r = \frac{4a}{3\pi}$ from its diameter.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\section{The Radon--Nikodym and Riesz representation theorems}\label{measure-theory:section-radon-nikodym}

\begin{lemma}\label{measure-theory:lemma-hilbert-riesz}
Let $H$ be a complex inner product space that is complete (a \emph{Hilbert space}), for instance $L^2(\mu)$. Every continuous linear functional $\Lambda \colon H \to \CC$ is of the form $\Lambda(f) = \langle f, g \rangle$
for a unique $g \in H$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $\Lambda = 0$ take $g = 0$. Otherwise $N = \ker\Lambda$ is a closed proper subspace. Choose $u \notin N$ and let $d = \inf_{v \in N}\|u - v\| > 0$. For $v_k \in N$ with $\|u - v_k\| \to d$, the parallelogram law
$\|v_k - v_l\|^2 = 2\|u - v_k\|^2 + 2\|u - v_l\|^2 - 4\|u - \frac{v_k + v_l}{2}\|^2$ shows that $(v_k)$ is Cauchy; its limit $v$ satisfies $\|u - v\| = d$. For $w \in N$ and $t \in \CC$,
$\|u - v - tw\|^2 \geq d^2$ forces $\langle u - v, w \rangle = 0$. So $z = u - v \neq 0$ is orthogonal to $N$. For $f \in H$, $\Lambda(f)z - \Lambda(z)f \in N$, so $\Lambda(f)\|z\|^2 = \Lambda(z)\langle f, z \rangle$, and
$g = \overline{\Lambda(z)}z/\|z\|^2$ works. Uniqueness: if $\langle f, g - g' \rangle = 0$ for all $f$, take $f = g - g'$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Radon--Nikodym]\label{measure-theory:theorem-radon-nikodym}
Let $\mu$ and $\nu$ be $\sigma$-finite measures on $(X, \mathcal{A})$ with $\nu \ll \mu$, meaning $\mu(E) = 0 \Rightarrow \nu(E) = 0$. There is a measurable $h \geq 0$, unique a.e., with
$\nu(E) = \int_Eh\,d\mu$ for all $E$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Assume $\mu$ and $\nu$ finite; the $\sigma$-finite case follows by decomposing $X$. Let $\rho = \mu + \nu$. On $L^2(\rho)$, $f \mapsto \int f\,d\nu$ is continuous, since
$|\int f\,d\nu| \leq \int|f|\,d\rho \leq \rho(X)^{1/2}\|f\|_2$. By Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-hilbert-riesz} there is $g$ with $\int f\,d\nu = \int fg\,d\rho$ for all $f \in L^2(\rho)$, and $g$ may be taken real. Taking
$f = \mathbf{1}_E$ gives $\nu(E) = \int_Eg\,d\rho$, so $g \geq 0$ $\rho$-a.e.; and $\int_E(1 - g)\,d\nu = \int_Eg\,d\mu$ (for bounded $f$, $\int f(1 - g)\,d\nu = \int fg\,d\mu$). For $E = \{g \geq 1\}$ the left side
is $\leq 0$ and the right side is $\geq \mu(E)$, so $\mu(E) = 0$ and $\nu(E) = 0$. Applying the identity to $f = \mathbf{1}_E(1 + g + \cdots + g^k)$ gives $\int_E(1 - g^{k+1})\,d\nu = \int_E(g + \cdots + g^{k+1})\,d\mu$. As
$k \to \infty$, monotone convergence gives $\nu(E) = \int_Eh\,d\mu$ with $h = g/(1 - g)$. If $h'$ also works, $\int_E(h - h')\,d\mu = 0$ for $E = \{h > h'\}$ and $\{h < h'\}$ (intersected with sets of finite measure), so
$h = h'$ a.e.
\end{proof}

Two measures $\mu$, $\nu$ on $(X, \mathcal{A})$ are \emph{mutually singular}, $\nu \perp \mu$, if there is $S \in \mathcal{A}$ with $\mu(S) = 0$ and $\nu(X \setminus S) = 0$: they live on disjoint sets.

\begin{theorem}[Lebesgue decomposition]\label{measure-theory:theorem-lebesgue-decomposition}
Let $\mu$, $\nu$ be $\sigma$-finite measures on $(X, \mathcal{A})$. There are unique measures $\nu_a \ll \mu$ and $\nu_s \perp \mu$ with $\nu = \nu_a + \nu_s$; and $\nu_a = h\,\mu$ for a measurable $h \geq 0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
As in Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-radon-nikodym} we may assume $\mu$, $\nu$ finite. Let $\rho = \mu + \nu$. Since $\nu \ll \rho$, Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-radon-nikodym} gives $g \geq 0$ with
$\nu(E) = \int_Eg\,d\rho$; from $\nu(E) \leq \rho(E)$ for all $E$, $g \leq 1$ $\rho$-almost everywhere, and we may take $0 \leq g \leq 1$. Writing $\int_Eg\,d\rho = \int_Eg\,d\mu + \int_Eg\,d\nu$ gives
\[
\int_E(1 - g)\,d\nu = \int_Eg\,d\mu \qquad (E \in \mathcal{A}).
\]
Let $S = \{g = 1\}$. For $E = S$ the left side vanishes, so $\int_Sg\,d\mu = \mu(S) = 0$. Put $\nu_s(E) = \nu(E \cap S)$ and $\nu_a(E) = \nu(E \setminus S)$; then $\nu_s \perp \mu$. If $\mu(E) = 0$, the identity gives
$\int_E(1 - g)\,d\nu = 0$, so $1 - g = 0$ $\nu$-almost everywhere on $E$, that is, $\nu(E \setminus S) = 0$: $\nu_a \ll \mu$, and $\nu_a = h\,\mu$ by Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-radon-nikodym}. For
uniqueness, let $\nu = \nu_a^{\prime} + \nu_s^{\prime}$ be another decomposition, with $\nu_s^{\prime}$ living on a $\mu$-null set $S^{\prime}$. On subsets of $X \setminus (S \cup S^{\prime})$ both singular parts vanish,
so $\nu_a = \nu = \nu_a^{\prime}$; on subsets of the $\mu$-null set $S \cup S^{\prime}$ both absolutely continuous parts vanish. Hence $\nu_a = \nu_a^{\prime}$, and $\nu_s = \nu - \nu_a = \nu_s^{\prime}$ ($\nu$ being finite).
\end{proof}

\begin{example}\label{measure-theory:example-lebesgue-decomposition}
With respect to Lebesgue measure $\lambda$ on $\RR$: $\nu = \delta_0 + \mathbf{1}_{[0,1]}\lambda$ has $\nu_a = \mathbf{1}_{[0,1]}\lambda$ and $\nu_s = \delta_0$, living on $\{0\}$. The measure $\mu_F$ of the
Cantor function (Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-stieltjes}) is singular, living on the Cantor set, but has no atoms. In general the singular part splits further into a \emph{discrete} part, a countable sum of
point masses, and a \emph{singular continuous} part without atoms, like $\mu_F$: the atoms of a $\sigma$-finite measure form a countable set, and the discrete part is the restriction to it.
\end{example}

\section{Absolutely continuous functions}\label{measure-theory:section-absolutely-continuous}

By Corollary~\ref{measure-theory:corollary-ftc-lebesgue}, an indefinite integral $F(x) = F(a) + \int_a^xf$ of an integrable function satisfies $F' = f$ almost everywhere.
Conversely, when is a function the integral of its derivative? Continuity and differentiability almost everywhere are not enough, by the Cantor function
(Counterexample~\ref{measure-theory:counterexample-cantor-function}). The right condition is absolute continuity.

\begin{definition}\label{measure-theory:definition-absolutely-continuous}
A function $F \colon [a, b] \to \CC$ is \emph{absolutely continuous} if for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is $\delta > 0$ such that $\sum_k|F(b_k) - F(a_k)| < \varepsilon$ for every finite
family of pairwise disjoint intervals $(a_k, b_k) \subseteq [a, b]$ with $\sum_k(b_k - a_k) < \delta$. For real $F$, the \emph{total variation} of $F$ on $[a, x]$ is
$V_F(x) = \sup\sum_i|F(t_i) - F(t_{i-1})|$, the supremum over the partitions $a = t_0 < \cdots < t_m = x$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}\label{measure-theory:lemma-absolutely-continuous}
\begin{enumerate}
\item For $f \in L^1([a, b])$, $F(x) = \int_{[a,x]}f\,d\lambda$ is absolutely continuous; more precisely, for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is $\delta > 0$ with $\int_E|f| < \varepsilon$
whenever $\lambda(E) < \delta$.
\item If $F$ is real and absolutely continuous, then $V_F$ is finite, nondecreasing and absolutely continuous, and so is $V_F - F$. So $F = V_F - (V_F - F)$ is a difference of
nondecreasing absolutely continuous functions.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(1) By monotone convergence $\int_{\{|f| > N\}}|f| \to 0$ as $N \to \infty$; choose $N$ with $\int_{\{|f| > N\}}|f| < \varepsilon/2$ and $\delta = \varepsilon/(2N)$. Then
$\int_E|f| \leq \int_{\{|f| > N\}}|f| + N\lambda(E) < \varepsilon$. For disjoint intervals of total length $< \delta$, $\sum_k|F(b_k) - F(a_k)| \leq \int_{\bigcup_k(a_k, b_k)}|f| < \varepsilon$.

(2) Take $\delta$ for $\varepsilon = 1$ and cut $[a, b]$ into $N$ intervals of length $< \delta$; refining a partition by these cut points does not decrease the sum, and the sum
over each of the $N$ intervals is $< 1$, so $V_F(b) \leq N$. For $x < y$, $V_F(y) - V_F(x)$ is the total variation of $F$ on $[x, y]$ (a partition of $[a, y]$ refined by $x$ splits
into partitions of $[a, x]$ and $[x, y]$), so $V_F$ is nondecreasing and $V_F(y) - V_F(x) \geq |F(y) - F(x)| \geq F(y) - F(x)$, which makes $V_F - F$ nondecreasing. If
$(a_k, b_k)$ are disjoint with $\sum_k(b_k - a_k) < \delta$ for the $\delta$ of $\varepsilon$, then any partitions of the intervals $[a_k, b_k]$ together give disjoint intervals of total
length $< \delta$, so $\sum_k(V_F(b_k) - V_F(a_k)) \leq \varepsilon$: $V_F$ is absolutely continuous, and so is $V_F - F$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{measure-theory:lemma-stieltjes}
Let $F \colon [a, b] \to \RR$ be continuous and nondecreasing. There is a finite Borel measure $\mu_F$ on $[a, b]$ with $\mu_F([a, x]) = F(x) - F(a)$ for all $x$; so
$\mu_F((x, y]) = F(y) - F(x)$ and points have measure $0$. If $F$ is absolutely continuous, then $\mu_F$ is absolutely continuous with respect to $\lambda$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $c = F(a)$ and $d = F(b)$. For $t \in [c, d]$ the set $\{x : F(x) = t\}$ is nonempty by the intermediate value theorem and closed, so it has a least element $G(t)$. The map
$G \colon [c, d] \to [a, b]$ is nondecreasing, hence Borel measurable (preimages of intervals are intervals), and $G(t) \leq x$ if and only if $t \leq F(x)$: if $t \leq F(x)$ some
$y \leq x$ has $F(y) = t$, and if $G(t) \leq x$ then $t = F(G(t)) \leq F(x)$. Let $\mu_F(E) = \lambda(\{t \in [c, d] : G(t) \in E\})$ for Borel $E \subseteq [a, b]$, the image of
Lebesgue measure under $G$. Then $\mu_F([a, x]) = \lambda([c, F(x)]) = F(x) - c$.

Let $F$ be absolutely continuous and $E$ a Borel set with $\lambda(E) = 0$. Given $\varepsilon > 0$, take $\delta$ from the definition and an open $U \supseteq E$ with $\lambda(U) < \delta$
(Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-lebesgue-measure}). The open set $U \cap (a, b)$ is the disjoint union of countably many open intervals $(a_k, b_k)$, its connected components,
with $\sum_k(b_k - a_k) < \delta$. For every $N$, $\sum_{k \leq N}\mu_F((a_k, b_k)) = \sum_{k \leq N}(F(b_k) - F(a_k)) < \varepsilon$, so $\mu_F(U \cap (a, b)) \leq \varepsilon$ and
$\mu_F(E) \leq \varepsilon$, the endpoints $a$, $b$ having measure $0$. As $\varepsilon$ is arbitrary, $\mu_F(E) = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Fundamental theorem of calculus for absolutely continuous functions]\label{measure-theory:theorem-absolutely-continuous}
Let $F \colon [a, b] \to \CC$ be absolutely continuous. Then $F$ is differentiable almost everywhere, $F' \in L^1([a, b])$, and
\[
F(x) = F(a) + \int_a^xF'(t)\,dt \qquad \text{for all } x \in [a, b].
\]
Conversely, every function of the form $F(a) + \int_a^xf$ with $f \in L^1([a, b])$ is absolutely continuous. So the absolutely continuous functions are exactly the indefinite
Lebesgue integrals.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The converse is Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-absolutely-continuous}(1). For the direct statement, treat real and imaginary parts separately, and by
Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-absolutely-continuous}(2) assume that $F$ is nondecreasing, the statement being linear in $F$. By
Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-stieltjes}, $\mu_F$ is a finite measure with $\mu_F \ll \lambda$ on the Borel sets of $[a, b]$, so by the Radon--Nikodym theorem
(Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-radon-nikodym}) there is an integrable $h \geq 0$ with $\mu_F(E) = \int_Eh\,d\lambda$. Hence $F(x) - F(a) = \mu_F([a, x]) = \int_a^xh$, and by
Corollary~\ref{measure-theory:corollary-ftc-lebesgue}, $F$ is differentiable almost everywhere with $F' = h$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}\label{measure-theory:example-absolutely-continuous}
A Lipschitz function, $|F(x) - F(y)| \leq L|x - y|$, is absolutely continuous (take $\delta = \varepsilon/L$), so it is differentiable almost everywhere and is the integral of its
derivative; for instance $|x| = \int_0^x\operatorname{sgn}$. The Cantor function is continuous and nondecreasing but not absolutely continuous: it maps the Cantor set, of measure
$0$, onto $[0, 1]$, whereas the proof of Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-stieltjes} shows that an absolutely continuous nondecreasing function gives measure $0$ to null sets, and
$\mu_F(C) = F(1) - F(0) = 1$ for the Cantor function because $F$ is constant on the complement of $C$. The function $x\sin(1/x)$ (and $0$ at $0$) on $[0, 1]$ is continuous but not
of bounded variation, hence not absolutely continuous.
\end{example}


\section{Radon measures and the Riesz representation theorem}\label{measure-theory:section-radon}

A measure assigns numbers to sets; integration against it assigns numbers to functions, $f \mapsto \int f\,d\mu$, and this assignment is linear and positive. On $\RR^n$ the
Riemann integral of continuous functions with compact support is such an assignment, and Lebesgue measure can be recovered from it: $\lambda([0, 1]^n)$ is the infimum of the integrals of the
continuous functions that are at least $1$ on the cube. The Riesz representation theorem says that this always works: every positive linear functional on continuous functions with compact support
is integration against exactly one measure of a reasonable kind. This point of view, \emph{measures as linear functionals}, is basic in geometric measure theory, in probability and in the theory
of partial differential equations. It allows one to take limits of measures (Section~\ref{measure-theory:section-weak-convergence}), and it is the model for distributions, which are linear functionals on
smooth functions (Chapter~\ref{distributions}).

Throughout this section $X$ is a locally compact Hausdorff space with a countable basis; for instance $\RR^n$, an open or a closed subset of $\RR^n$, or a manifold. Such a space is metrisable: it is
regular, because for a point $x$ outside a closed set $F$, Lemma~\ref{general-topology:lemma-lch-shrinking} applied to $\{x\} \subseteq X \setminus F$ gives an open $V \ni x$ with
$\overline{V} \cap F = \emptyset$, and a regular space with a countable basis is metrisable (Theorem~\ref{general-topology:theorem-urysohn-metrisation}). It has an exhaustion by compact sets
$K_1 \subseteq K_2 \subseteq \cdots$ with $K_m \subseteq \operatorname{int}K_{m+1}$ (Proposition~\ref{general-topology:proposition-exhaustion}); every compact $K \subseteq X$ lies in some $K_m$, since the
increasing open sets $\operatorname{int}K_m$ cover $K$.

We write $C_c(X)$ for the real vector space of continuous functions $f \colon X \to \RR$ with compact support, and, for an open $V \subseteq X$,
\[
f \prec V \quad \text{if} \quad f \in C_c(X), \quad 0 \leq f \leq 1 \quad \text{and} \quad \operatorname{supp}f \subseteq V.
\]
Continuous functions are Borel measurable. If $\mu$ is a Borel measure that is finite on compact sets, every $f \in C_c(X)$ is integrable, with $|\int f\,d\mu| \leq \|f\|_\infty\,\mu(\operatorname{supp}f)$.

\begin{lemma}\label{measure-theory:lemma-closed-approximation}
Let $\nu$ be a finite Borel measure on a metrisable space $X$. For every Borel set $E$ and every $\varepsilon > 0$ there are a closed set $F$ and an open set $U$ with $F \subseteq E \subseteq U$ and
$\nu(U \setminus F) < \varepsilon$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Fix a metric $d$ inducing the topology, and let $\mathcal{G}$ be the family of Borel sets $E$ with the stated property. A closed set $E$ belongs to $\mathcal{G}$: take $F = E$ and
$U_k = \{x : d(x, E) < 1/k\}$, which are open, decrease, and have intersection $E$ because $E$ is closed; as $\nu$ is finite, $\nu(U_k \setminus E) \to 0$
(Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-measure-properties}). The family $\mathcal{G}$ is closed under complements, since $F \subseteq E \subseteq U$ gives
$X \setminus U \subseteq X \setminus E \subseteq X \setminus F$ with the same difference $U \setminus F$. It is closed under countable unions: if $F_k \subseteq E_k \subseteq U_k$ with
$\nu(U_k \setminus F_k) < \varepsilon 2^{-k-1}$, then $U = \bigcup_kU_k$ is open and $F^{\prime} = \bigcup_kF_k$ satisfies $\nu(U \setminus F^{\prime}) < \varepsilon/2$; the closed sets
$F_1 \cup \cdots \cup F_N$ increase to $F^{\prime}$, so one of them, $F$, has $\nu(F^{\prime} \setminus F) < \varepsilon/2$, and then $F \subseteq \bigcup_kE_k \subseteq U$ and $\nu(U \setminus F) < \varepsilon$.
So $\mathcal{G}$ is a $\sigma$-algebra containing the closed sets, hence all Borel sets.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}\label{measure-theory:proposition-radon-automatic}
Let $X$ be a locally compact Hausdorff space with a countable basis and $\mu$ a Borel measure on $X$ that is finite on compact sets. Then $\mu$ is $\sigma$-finite and regular, hence a Radon measure
(Definition~\ref{measure-theory:definition-regular}): for every Borel set $E$,
\[
\mu(E) = \inf\{\mu(U) : U \supseteq E \text{ open}\} = \sup\{\mu(K) : K \subseteq E \text{ compact}\}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
$X$ is the union of the compact sets $K_m$ of an exhaustion, which have finite measure, so $\mu$ is $\sigma$-finite; and every point has a neighbourhood $\operatorname{int}K_{m+1} \subseteq K_{m+1}$ of finite
measure. The measures $\nu_m(A) = \mu(A \cap \operatorname{int}K_m)$ are finite, and Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-closed-approximation} applies to them, $X$ being metrisable.

\emph{Outer regularity.} Let $E$ be a Borel set with $\mu(E) < \infty$ (otherwise there is nothing to prove) and $\varepsilon > 0$. Choose open sets $U_m \supseteq E$ with
$\nu_m(U_m \setminus E) < \varepsilon2^{-m}$, and let $U = \bigcup_m(U_m \cap \operatorname{int}K_m)$. It is open and contains $E = \bigcup_m(E \cap \operatorname{int}K_m)$, and
$\mu(U \setminus E) \leq \sum_m\mu\bigl((U_m \cap \operatorname{int}K_m) \setminus E\bigr) = \sum_m\nu_m(U_m \setminus E) < \varepsilon$. So $\mu(U) < \mu(E) + \varepsilon$.

\emph{Inner regularity.} Let $E$ be a Borel set and $t < \mu(E)$. By continuity from below, $\mu(E \cap \operatorname{int}K_m) > t$ for some $m$. Choose a closed $F \subseteq E$ with
$\nu_m(E \setminus F) < \mu(E \cap \operatorname{int}K_m) - t$. Then $K = F \cap K_m$ is compact, contained in $E$, and
$\mu(K) \geq \mu(F \cap \operatorname{int}K_m) = \nu_m(E) - \nu_m(E \setminus F) > t$.
\end{proof}

So on $\RR^n$ and on manifolds, \emph{Radon measure} simply means \emph{Borel measure finite on compact sets}. For general locally compact Hausdorff spaces this is false, and the theory below has to
be set up with more care \cite[Chapter 2]{rudin-rca}; we shall not need that generality.

\begin{lemma}\label{measure-theory:lemma-partition-compact}
Let $X$ be a locally compact Hausdorff space, $K \subseteq X$ compact and $V_1, \ldots, V_n$ open sets covering $K$. There are $h_i \prec V_i$ with $\sum_ih_i \leq 1$ on $X$ and $\sum_ih_i = 1$ on $K$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Each $x \in K$ lies in some $V_i$, and Lemma~\ref{general-topology:lemma-lch-shrinking} gives an open $W_x \ni x$ with compact closure $\overline{W_x} \subseteq V_i$. Finitely many $W_x$ cover $K$; let $H_i$ be
the union of the closures of those among them for which the chosen $i$ was $i$. Then $H_i \subseteq V_i$ is compact and $K \subseteq H_1 \cup \cdots \cup H_n$. Lemma~\ref{general-topology:lemma-lch-shrinking}
gives $g_i \prec V_i$ with $g_i = 1$ on $H_i$. Put $h_1 = g_1$ and $h_k = (1 - g_1)\cdots(1 - g_{k-1})g_k$. Then $0 \leq h_k \leq g_k$, so $h_k \prec V_k$, and by induction on $m$,
\[
h_1 + \cdots + h_m = 1 - (1 - g_1)(1 - g_2)\cdots(1 - g_m),
\]
since $1 - \prod_{k \leq m}(1 - g_k) + h_{m+1} = 1 - \prod_{k \leq m+1}(1 - g_k)$. For $m = n$ the right side lies in $[0, 1]$, and it equals $1$ on each $H_i$, where the factor $1 - g_i$ vanishes.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}\label{measure-theory:definition-positive-functional}
A linear map $\Lambda \colon C_c(X) \to \RR$ is \emph{positive} if $\Lambda(f) \geq 0$ whenever $f \geq 0$.
\end{definition}

A positive functional is monotone: $f \leq g$ implies $\Lambda f \leq \Lambda g$. It is also automatically continuous in the following sense. If $K$ is compact and $\phi_K \in C_c(X)$ satisfies
$0 \leq \phi_K \leq 1$ and $\phi_K = 1$ on $K$ (Lemma~\ref{general-topology:lemma-lch-shrinking}), then every $f$ with $\operatorname{supp}f \subseteq K$ satisfies
$-\|f\|_\infty\phi_K \leq f \leq \|f\|_\infty\phi_K$, hence
\[
|\Lambda f| \leq \Lambda(\phi_K)\,\|f\|_\infty .
\]
So uniform convergence of functions supported in a fixed compact set implies convergence of the values of $\Lambda$.

\begin{example}\label{measure-theory:example-positive-functionals}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Point masses.} $\Lambda f = f(x_0)$ is positive, and $\Lambda f = \int f\,d\delta_{x_0}$. More generally, for points $x_1, x_2, \ldots$ of $X$ with no accumulation point and numbers
$c_k \geq 0$, the finite sum $\Lambda f = \sum_kc_kf(x_k)$ is integration against $\sum_kc_k\delta_{x_k}$.
\item \emph{Densities.} For $g \in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\RR^n)$ with $g \geq 0$, $\Lambda f = \int fg\,d\lambda$ is positive; it is integration against the measure $g\lambda$, $E \mapsto \int_Eg\,d\lambda$. For
$g = 1$ this is the Riemann integral of $f$ (Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-riemann-lebesgue}, together with Fubini's theorem for the iterated integrals).
\item \emph{Arc length on a circle.} On $X = \RR^2$, $\Lambda f = \int_0^{2\pi}f(\cos t, \sin t)\,dt$ is positive. The corresponding measure $\sigma$ (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-riesz-markov}
below) lives on the unit circle $S^1$: if $K$ is compact and disjoint from $S^1$, a function $f \prec \RR^2 \setminus S^1$ with $f = 1$ on $K$ gives $\sigma(K) \leq \int f\,d\sigma = \Lambda f = 0$, and
by inner regularity $\sigma(\RR^2 \setminus S^1) = 0$. Since $\lambda^2(S^1) = 0$, the measures $\sigma$ and $\lambda^2$ are mutually singular. The measure of an arc is its length
(Exercise~\ref{measure-theory:exercise-riesz-circle}), and $\sigma$ is the restriction to $S^1$ of the one-dimensional Hausdorff measure of Section~\ref{measure-theory:section-hausdorff}.
\item \emph{Push-forward.} More generally, for a continuous map $\varphi \colon [a, b] \to X$, the functional $\Lambda f = \int_a^bf(\varphi(t))\,dt$ is integration against the push-forward
$\varphi_*\lambda$, $E \mapsto \lambda(\{t \in [a, b] : \varphi(t) \in E\})$, which is a Borel measure, finite because $\lambda([a, b]) < \infty$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}[Riesz--Markov]\label{measure-theory:theorem-riesz-markov}
Let $X$ be a locally compact Hausdorff space with a countable basis, and $\Lambda \colon C_c(X) \to \RR$ a positive linear functional. There is a unique Borel measure $\mu$ on $X$, finite on compact
sets, with
\[
\Lambda f = \int_Xf\,d\mu \qquad \text{for all } f \in C_c(X).
\]
It is a Radon measure, and for open $V$ and compact $K$,
\[
\mu(V) = \sup\{\Lambda f : f \prec V\}, \qquad \mu(K) = \inf\{\Lambda f : f \in C_c(X),\ f \geq \mathbf{1}_K\}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The proof follows \cite[Theorem 2.14]{rudin-rca}. Motivated by the formulas to be proved, define $\mu(V) = \sup\{\Lambda f : f \prec V\}$ for open $V$, and for an arbitrary $E \subseteq X$
\[
\mu^*(E) = \inf\{\mu(V) : V \supseteq E \text{ open}\}.
\]
If $V \subseteq W$ are open, $f \prec V$ implies $f \prec W$, so $\mu(V) \leq \mu(W)$; hence $\mu^*(V) = \mu(V)$ for open $V$, and $\mu^*$ is monotone.

\emph{Step 1: $\mu^*$ is an outer measure.} First let $V = \bigcup_iV_i$ be a countable union of open sets, and $f \prec V$. The compact set $\operatorname{supp}f$ is covered by finitely many
$V_1, \ldots, V_n$, and Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-partition-compact} gives $h_i \prec V_i$ with $\sum_ih_i = 1$ on $\operatorname{supp}f$. Then $f = \sum_ih_if$ with $h_if \prec V_i$, so
$\Lambda f = \sum_i\Lambda(h_if) \leq \sum_i\mu(V_i)$; taking the supremum over $f$, $\mu(V) \leq \sum_i\mu(V_i)$. Now let $E = \bigcup_iE_i$ be arbitrary, with $\sum_i\mu^*(E_i) < \infty$, and
$\varepsilon > 0$. Choose open $V_i \supseteq E_i$ with $\mu(V_i) \leq \mu^*(E_i) + \varepsilon2^{-i}$; then
$\mu^*(E) \leq \mu(\bigcup_iV_i) \leq \sum_i\mu^*(E_i) + \varepsilon$. Finally $\mu^*(\emptyset) = 0$, as $f \prec \emptyset$ forces $f = 0$.

\emph{Step 2: compact sets.} Let $K$ be compact. If $f \in C_c(X)$ and $f \geq \mathbf{1}_K$, and $0 < \alpha < 1$, then $V_\alpha = \{f > \alpha\}$ is an open set containing $K$, and every $g \prec V_\alpha$
satisfies $\alpha g \leq f$; so $\mu^*(K) \leq \mu(V_\alpha) \leq \Lambda f/\alpha$, and letting $\alpha \to 1$, $\mu^*(K) \leq \Lambda f$. Conversely, for an open $V \supseteq K$,
Lemma~\ref{general-topology:lemma-lch-shrinking} gives $f \prec V$ with $f = 1$ on $K$, and $\Lambda f \leq \mu(V)$. So $\mu^*(K) = \inf\{\Lambda f : f \geq \mathbf{1}_K\}$, which is finite.

\emph{Step 3: Borel sets are $\mu^*$-measurable.} By Carath\'eodory's theorem (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-caratheodory}) the $\mu^*$-measurable sets form a $\sigma$-algebra on which $\mu^*$ is a
measure, so it suffices to show that every open $U$ is $\mu^*$-measurable: $\mu^*(E \cap U) + \mu^*(E \setminus U) \leq \mu^*(E)$ for every $E$ with $\mu^*(E) < \infty$. Let $\varepsilon > 0$ and
choose an open $V \supseteq E$ with $\mu(V) \leq \mu^*(E) + \varepsilon$. Choose $f \prec V \cap U$ with $\Lambda f > \mu(V \cap U) - \varepsilon$; its support $K$ is a compact subset of $U$, so
$V \setminus K$ is open and we may choose $g \prec V \setminus K$ with $\Lambda g > \mu(V \setminus K) - \varepsilon$. The functions $f$ and $g$ have disjoint supports, so $f + g \prec V$, and
\[
\mu^*(E) + \varepsilon \geq \mu(V) \geq \Lambda f + \Lambda g > \mu(V \cap U) + \mu(V \setminus K) - 2\varepsilon \geq \mu^*(E \cap U) + \mu^*(E \setminus U) - 2\varepsilon,
\]
because $E \cap U \subseteq V \cap U$ and $E \setminus U \subseteq V \setminus K$. Let $\varepsilon \to 0$.

So the restriction of $\mu^*$ to the Borel sets is a Borel measure $\mu$, finite on compact sets by Step 2, and by construction it satisfies the two formulas of the statement.

\emph{Step 4: $\Lambda f = \int f\,d\mu$.} Both sides are linear in $f$, so it suffices to show $\Lambda f \leq \int f\,d\mu$ for every $f \in C_c(X)$: applied to $-f$ it gives the reverse inequality.
Let $K = \operatorname{supp}f$, choose $a < b$ with $f(X) \subseteq [a, b]$, let $\varepsilon > 0$, and choose $y_0 < a < y_1 < \cdots < y_n = b$ with $y_i - y_{i-1} < \varepsilon$. The Borel sets
$E_i = \{y_{i-1} < f \leq y_i\} \cap K$ are disjoint with union $K$. By the definition of $\mu^*$ there are open sets $V_i \supseteq E_i$ with $\mu(V_i) < \mu(E_i) + \varepsilon/n$; replacing $V_i$ by
$V_i \cap \{f < y_i + \varepsilon\}$ we may assume $f < y_i + \varepsilon$ on $V_i$. Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-partition-compact} gives $h_i \prec V_i$ with $\sum_ih_i = 1$ on $K$. Then
$f = \sum_ih_if$, and $h_if \leq (y_i + \varepsilon)h_i$. By Step 2, $\sum_i\Lambda h_i = \Lambda(\sum_ih_i) \geq \mu(K)$, and $\Lambda h_i \leq \mu(V_i)$. As $|a| + y_i + \varepsilon > 0$,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\Lambda f = \sum_i\Lambda(h_if) &\leq \sum_i(y_i + \varepsilon)\Lambda h_i = \sum_i(|a| + y_i + \varepsilon)\Lambda h_i - |a|\sum_i\Lambda h_i\\
&\leq \sum_i(|a| + y_i + \varepsilon)\Bigl(\mu(E_i) + \frac{\varepsilon}{n}\Bigr) - |a|\mu(K)\\
&\leq \sum_i(y_i - \varepsilon)\mu(E_i) + 2\varepsilon\mu(K) + \varepsilon(|a| + b + \varepsilon),
\end{aligned}
\]
using $\sum_i\mu(E_i) = \mu(K)$ and $y_i \leq b$. On $E_i$ we have $f > y_{i-1} > y_i - \varepsilon$, so $\sum_i(y_i - \varepsilon)\mu(E_i) \leq \int f\,d\mu$. Letting $\varepsilon \to 0$ gives
$\Lambda f \leq \int f\,d\mu$.

\emph{Step 5: regularity and uniqueness.} $\mu$ is a Radon measure by Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-radon-automatic}. Let $\mu^{\prime}$ be another Borel measure, finite on compact sets,
representing $\Lambda$, and $K$ compact. If $f \geq \mathbf{1}_K$, then $\mu^{\prime}(K) \leq \int f\,d\mu^{\prime} = \Lambda f$, so $\mu^{\prime}(K) \leq \mu(K)$ by Step 2. If $V \supseteq K$ is open and
$f \prec V$ with $f = 1$ on $K$, then $\mu(K) \leq \Lambda f = \int f\,d\mu^{\prime} \leq \mu^{\prime}(V)$, and by outer regularity of $\mu^{\prime}$
(Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-radon-automatic}) $\mu(K) \leq \mu^{\prime}(K)$. So $\mu$ and $\mu^{\prime}$ agree on compact sets, hence, by inner regularity, on all Borel sets.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{measure-theory:remark-riesz-markov}
\begin{enumerate}
\item For $X = \RR^n$ and $\Lambda$ the Riemann integral, the theorem produces Lebesgue measure on the Borel sets; the uniqueness statement is a convenient way to identify measures, as in
Exercise~\ref{measure-theory:exercise-riesz-circle}. On a smooth manifold, an orientation and a volume form, or more generally a density, define a positive functional by integration, and hence a
Radon measure (Chapter~\ref{differential-forms}).
\item The theorem holds for every locally compact Hausdorff space, but then the measure is only outer regular and inner regular on open sets and on sets of finite measure
\cite[Theorem 2.14 and Theorem 2.18]{rudin-rca}.
\item If $X$ is compact, $1 \in C_c(X)$, and $|\Lambda f| \leq \Lambda(1)\|f\|_\infty = \mu(X)\|f\|_\infty$: a positive functional on $C(X)$ is bounded with norm $\mu(X)$. Every bounded linear functional on
$C(X)$ is integration against a \emph{signed} Radon measure, a difference of two finite Radon measures \cite[Theorem 6.19]{rudin-rca} (for the spaces of this section, see Corollary~\ref{measure-theory:corollary-signed-riesz}
below); this identifies the dual space of $C(X)$, a theme of
Chapter~\ref{functional-analysis}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\subsection{Signed and vector-valued functionals}\label{measure-theory:subsection-riesz-vector}

Many natural linear functionals are not positive. The functional $\varphi \mapsto \varphi(0) - \varphi(1)$ on $C_c(\RR)$ is the difference of two Dirac measures; $\varphi \mapsto \int\varphi h\,d\lambda$ with $h$
changing sign is the difference of the measures $h^+\lambda$ and $h^-\lambda$. In geometric measure theory one also meets functionals on \emph{vector fields}: for the unit circle $S^1 \subseteq \RR^2$ with
outward normal $n(x) = x$, the \emph{flux} $F \mapsto \int_0^{2\pi}\langle F(\cos t, \sin t), (\cos t, \sin t)\rangle\,dt$ through the circle is a linear functional on continuous vector fields $F \colon \RR^2 \to \RR^2$ with compact support. The
Riesz representation theorem extends to all these cases: a functional that is merely bounded on functions of bounded size supported in a fixed compact set is integration against a measure, after pairing with
a field of unit vectors.

Let $H$ be a finite-dimensional real vector space with an inner product $\langle\,,\rangle$ and norm $|\ |$; for instance $H = \RR^m$ with the standard inner product. Let $C_c(X, H)$ be the vector space of
continuous maps $f \colon X \to H$ with compact support. For $f \in C_c(X, H)$ the function $|f|$ belongs to $C_c(X)$. The map $r \colon H \to H$, $r(v) = v$ for $|v| \leq 1$ and $r(v) = v/|v|$ for $|v| > 1$, the
nearest point projection onto the closed unit ball, is continuous, takes values in the ball, and moves no point farther from the ball: $|r(u) - w| \leq |u - w|$ whenever $|w| \leq 1$. To see this,
we may assume $|u| > 1$; then
$|u - w|^2 - |r(u) - w|^2 = |u|^2 - 1 - 2(|u| - 1)\langle u/|u|, w\rangle \geq |u|^2 - 1 - 2(|u| - 1) = (|u| - 1)^2 \geq 0$.

\begin{lemma}\label{measure-theory:lemma-radon-approximation}
Let $\mu$ be a Radon measure on $X$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item For $1 \leq p < \infty$, $C_c(X)$ is dense in $L^p(\mu)$.
\item Let $w \colon X \to H$ be Borel with $|w| \leq 1$, $E$ a Borel set with $\mu(E) < \infty$, and $V \supseteq E$ open. For every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is $g \in C_c(X, H)$ with $|g| \leq 1$,
$\operatorname{supp}g \subseteq V$ and $\int|g - \mathbf{1}_Ew|\,d\mu < \varepsilon$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(1) As in Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-density}, simple functions $\sum a_i\mathbf{1}_{E_i}$ with $\mu(E_i) < \infty$ are dense in $L^p(\mu)$, by
Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-measurable-functions}(4) and dominated convergence. Given $E$ with $\mu(E) < \infty$ and $\varepsilon > 0$, regularity of $\mu$ gives a compact $K$ and an
open $U$ with $K \subseteq E \subseteq U$ and $\mu(U \setminus K) < \varepsilon$, and Lemma~\ref{general-topology:lemma-lch-shrinking} gives $\phi \in C_c(X)$ with $0 \leq \phi \leq 1$, $\phi = 1$ on $K$ and
$\operatorname{supp}\phi \subseteq U$. Then $\int|\mathbf{1}_E - \phi|^p\,d\mu \leq \mu(U \setminus K) < \varepsilon$.

(2) Fix an orthonormal basis $e_1, \ldots, e_m$ of $H$ and write $w = \sum_jw_je_j$. By (1) choose $\phi_j \in C_c(X)$ with $\int|\phi_j - \mathbf{1}_Ew_j|\,d\mu < \varepsilon/(2m)$, and put
$g_0 = \sum_j\phi_je_j$, so $\int|g_0 - \mathbf{1}_Ew|\,d\mu < \varepsilon/2$. Then $g_1 = r \circ g_0$ is continuous with compact support, $|g_1| \leq 1$, and $|g_1 - \mathbf{1}_Ew| \leq |g_0 - \mathbf{1}_Ew|$
pointwise, as $|\mathbf{1}_Ew| \leq 1$. Choose a compact $K \subseteq E$ with $\mu(E \setminus K) < \varepsilon/4$ and $\psi \in C_c(X)$ with $0 \leq \psi \leq 1$, $\psi = 1$ on $K$ and
$\operatorname{supp}\psi \subseteq V$, and let $g = \psi g_1$. On $K$, $g = g_1$; outside $E$, $|g - \mathbf{1}_Ew| = |g| \leq |g_1| = |g_1 - \mathbf{1}_Ew|$; on $E \setminus K$, $|g - w| \leq 2$. Hence
$\int|g - \mathbf{1}_Ew|\,d\mu \leq \int|g_1 - \mathbf{1}_Ew|\,d\mu + 2\mu(E \setminus K) < \varepsilon$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Riesz representation theorem for vector-valued functions]\label{measure-theory:theorem-riesz-vector}
Let $L \colon C_c(X, H) \to \RR$ be linear and suppose that for every compact $K \subseteq X$
\[
M_K = \sup\bigl\{L(f) : f \in C_c(X, H),\ |f| \leq 1,\ \operatorname{supp}f \subseteq K\bigr\} < \infty .
\]
Then there are a Radon measure $\mu$ on $X$ and a Borel map $\nu \colon X \to H$ with $|\nu| = 1$ $\mu$-almost everywhere such that
\[
L(f) = \int_X\langle f, \nu\rangle\,d\mu \qquad\text{for all } f \in C_c(X, H).
\]
Moreover, for every open $V \subseteq X$,
\begin{equation}\label{measure-theory:equation-total-variation}
\mu(V) = \sup\bigl\{L(f) : f \in C_c(X, H),\ |f| \leq 1,\ \operatorname{supp}f \subseteq V\bigr\},
\end{equation}
and the pair $(\mu, \nu)$ is unique: $\mu$ is unique, and $\nu$ is unique up to $\mu$-null sets. The measure $\mu$ is the \emph{total variation measure} of $L$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The idea is to manufacture a positive functional out of $L$, namely the largest value of $L$ on fields dominated by a given function, and to apply the Riesz--Markov theorem to it.

\emph{Step 1: a positive functional on nonnegative functions.} For $\varphi \in C_c(X)$ with $\varphi \geq 0$ let
\[
\lambda(\varphi) = \sup\bigl\{L(g) : g \in C_c(X, H),\ |g| \leq \varphi\bigr\}.
\]
Taking $g = 0$ shows $\lambda(\varphi) \geq 0$. If $|g| \leq \varphi$, then $\operatorname{supp}g \subseteq K = \operatorname{supp}\varphi$, and if $\varphi \neq 0$, $g/\|\varphi\|_\infty$ is admissible in the
definition of $M_K$; so $L(g) \leq \|\varphi\|_\infty M_K$ and $\lambda(\varphi) < \infty$. Clearly $\lambda(c\varphi) = c\lambda(\varphi)$ for $c > 0$, as $g \mapsto cg$ matches the admissible fields. We
show $\lambda(\varphi_1 + \varphi_2) = \lambda(\varphi_1) + \lambda(\varphi_2)$ for $\varphi_1, \varphi_2 \geq 0$. If $|g_i| \leq \varphi_i$, then $|g_1 + g_2| \leq \varphi_1 + \varphi_2$, so
$L(g_1) + L(g_2) \leq \lambda(\varphi_1 + \varphi_2)$; taking suprema gives ``$\geq$''. Conversely let $|g| \leq \varphi_1 + \varphi_2$, and define $g_i = \varphi_ig/(\varphi_1 + \varphi_2)$ where
$\varphi_1 + \varphi_2 > 0$ and $g_i = 0$ elsewhere. Then $|g_i| \leq \varphi_i$, and $g_i$ is continuous: this is clear where $\varphi_1 + \varphi_2 > 0$, and at a point $x_0$ where
$\varphi_1 + \varphi_2 = 0$ we have $|g_i| \leq \varphi_i$ everywhere and $\varphi_i(x_0) = 0$, so $g_i(x) \to 0 = g_i(x_0)$ as $x \to x_0$. Since $g = 0$ where $\varphi_1 + \varphi_2 = 0$, $g = g_1 + g_2$, and
$L(g) = L(g_1) + L(g_2) \leq \lambda(\varphi_1) + \lambda(\varphi_2)$.

\emph{Step 2: extension and the measure $\mu$.} For $\varphi \in C_c(X)$ put $\lambda(\varphi) = \lambda(\varphi^+) - \lambda(\varphi^-)$, where $\varphi^\pm = \max(\pm\varphi, 0) \in C_c(X)$. If
$\varphi = a - b$ with $a, b \geq 0$ in $C_c(X)$, then $\varphi^+ + b = \varphi^- + a$, and Step 1 gives $\lambda(\varphi) = \lambda(a) - \lambda(b)$. Writing
$\varphi + \psi = (\varphi^+ + \psi^+) - (\varphi^- + \psi^-)$ shows that $\lambda$ is additive; it is homogeneous for $c \geq 0$ by Step 1, and $\lambda(-\varphi) = -\lambda(\varphi)$ since
$(-\varphi)^\pm = \varphi^\mp$. So $\lambda$ is a positive linear functional on $C_c(X)$, and the Riesz--Markov theorem (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-riesz-markov}) gives a Radon measure $\mu$ with
$\lambda(\varphi) = \int\varphi\,d\mu$.

\emph{Step 3: $|L(g)| \leq \int|g|\,d\mu$.} For $g \in C_c(X, H)$, $g$ is admissible for $\lambda(|g|)$, so $L(g) \leq \lambda(|g|) = \int|g|\,d\mu$; apply this also to $-g$.

\emph{Step 4: construction of $\nu$.} Fix an orthonormal basis $e_1, \ldots, e_m$ of $H$ and let $L_j(\varphi) = L(\varphi e_j)$ for $\varphi \in C_c(X)$. By Step 3, $|L_j(\varphi)| \leq \int|\varphi|\,d\mu$,
so $\Lambda_j(\varphi) = \int\varphi\,d\mu + L_j(\varphi)$ is a positive linear functional on $C_c(X)$. By the Riesz--Markov theorem there is a Radon measure $\sigma_j$ with $\Lambda_j(\varphi) = \int\varphi\,d\sigma_j$.
For open $V$ and $\varphi \prec V$, $\Lambda_j(\varphi) \leq 2\int\varphi\,d\mu \leq 2\mu(V)$, so $\sigma_j(V) \leq 2\mu(V)$ by the formula for $\sigma_j(V)$ in Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-riesz-markov}; by
outer regularity of $\mu$, $\sigma_j(E) \leq 2\mu(E)$ for all Borel $E$. Both measures are $\sigma$-finite (Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-radon-automatic}) and $\sigma_j \ll \mu$, so by the
Radon--Nikodym theorem (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-radon-nikodym}) $\sigma_j(E) = \int_Eh_j\,d\mu$ for a Borel function $h_j \geq 0$. Since $\int_Eh_j\,d\mu \leq 2\mu(E)$ for all $E$, applied to
$E = \{h_j > 2\} \cap K_m$ for the compact exhaustion $(K_m)$, $h_j \leq 2$ almost everywhere, and we may change $h_j$ on a null set so that $0 \leq h_j \leq 2$ everywhere. By the usual extension from
indicator functions to simple functions and limits, $\int\varphi\,d\sigma_j = \int\varphi h_j\,d\mu$ for $\varphi \in C_c(X)$. Put $\nu_j = h_j - 1 \in [-1, 1]$ and $\nu = \sum_j\nu_je_j$, a Borel map with
$|\nu| \leq \sqrt m$. Then $L_j(\varphi) = \int\varphi\,\nu_j\,d\mu$, and for $f \in C_c(X, H)$, writing $f = \sum_j\langle f, e_j\rangle e_j$ with $\langle f, e_j\rangle \in C_c(X)$,
\[
L(f) = \sum_jL_j\bigl(\langle f, e_j\rangle\bigr) = \int\sum_j\langle f, e_j\rangle\nu_j\,d\mu = \int\langle f, \nu\rangle\,d\mu .
\]

\emph{Step 5: $|\nu| \leq 1$ almost everywhere.} Let $E = \{|\nu| > 1\} \cap K_m$ and $w = \nu/|\nu|$ on $E$, $w = 0$ elsewhere. For $\varepsilon > 0$, Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-radon-approximation}(2) gives
$g \in C_c(X, H)$ with $|g| \leq 1$ and $\int|g - \mathbf{1}_Ew|\,d\mu < \varepsilon$. Then, by Step 3,
\[
\int_E|\nu|\,d\mu = \int\langle\mathbf{1}_Ew, \nu\rangle\,d\mu \leq L(g) + \sqrt m\,\varepsilon \leq \int|g|\,d\mu + \sqrt m\,\varepsilon \leq \mu(E) + (1 + \sqrt m)\varepsilon .
\]
So $\int_E(|\nu| - 1)\,d\mu \leq 0$, while $|\nu| - 1 > 0$ on $E$; hence $\mu(E) = 0$, for every $m$.

\emph{Step 6: $|\nu| \geq 1$ almost everywhere.} For $\varphi \geq 0$ in $C_c(X)$ and $|g| \leq \varphi$, $L(g) = \int\langle g, \nu\rangle\,d\mu \leq \int\varphi|\nu|\,d\mu$; taking the supremum,
$\int\varphi\,d\mu = \lambda(\varphi) \leq \int\varphi|\nu|\,d\mu$, that is, $\int\varphi(1 - |\nu|)\,d\mu \leq 0$. Given a compact $K$, choose such $\varphi$ with $\varphi = 1$ on $K$ and $0 \leq \varphi \leq 1$
(Lemma~\ref{general-topology:lemma-lch-shrinking}); as $1 - |\nu| \geq 0$ almost everywhere by Step 5, $\int_K(1 - |\nu|)\,d\mu \leq \int\varphi(1 - |\nu|)\,d\mu \leq 0$, so $|\nu| = 1$ almost
everywhere on $K$, and on $X = \bigcup_mK_m$.

\emph{Step 7: the formula \eqref{measure-theory:equation-total-variation} and uniqueness.} Let $(\mu^{\prime}, \nu^{\prime})$ be any pair with the properties of the theorem; we show that $\mu^{\prime}(V)$ equals
the right side $s(V)$ of \eqref{measure-theory:equation-total-variation}. If $|f| \leq 1$, then $L(f) = \int\langle f, \nu^{\prime}\rangle\,d\mu^{\prime} \leq \int_V|f|\,d\mu^{\prime} \leq \mu^{\prime}(V)$ for
$\operatorname{supp}f \subseteq V$, so $s(V) \leq \mu^{\prime}(V)$. Conversely, let $K \subseteq V$ be compact and $\varepsilon > 0$. Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-radon-approximation}(2), applied to $\mu^{\prime}$,
$w = \nu^{\prime}$ and $E = K$, gives $g$ with $|g| \leq 1$, $\operatorname{supp}g \subseteq V$ and $\int|g - \mathbf{1}_K\nu^{\prime}|\,d\mu^{\prime} < \varepsilon$, so
$L(g) \geq \int_K|\nu^{\prime}|^2\,d\mu^{\prime} - \varepsilon = \mu^{\prime}(K) - \varepsilon$. By inner regularity $s(V) \geq \mu^{\prime}(V)$. Hence $\mu^{\prime}(V) = s(V)$ for all open $V$; this proves
\eqref{measure-theory:equation-total-variation} for $\mu$, and shows that $\mu^{\prime}$ and $\mu$ agree on open sets, hence on all Borel sets by outer regularity. Finally
$\int\langle f, \nu - \nu^{\prime}\rangle\,d\mu = 0$ for all $f \in C_c(X, H)$. Let $E = \{\nu \neq \nu^{\prime}\} \cap K_m$ and $w = (\nu - \nu^{\prime})/|\nu - \nu^{\prime}|$ on $E$, $0$ elsewhere; approximating
$\mathbf{1}_Ew$ by $g$ as in Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-radon-approximation}(2) and using $|\nu - \nu^{\prime}| \leq 2$ almost everywhere gives $\int_E|\nu - \nu^{\prime}|\,d\mu \leq 2\varepsilon$ for every
$\varepsilon$, so $\nu = \nu^{\prime}$ almost everywhere on $E$, and on $X$.
\end{proof}

For a positive functional on $C_c(X) = C_c(X, \RR)$ the theorem gives back the Riesz--Markov measure with $\nu = 1$. For general functionals on $C_c(X)$ it gives the decomposition into a positive and a negative
part.

\begin{corollary}\label{measure-theory:corollary-signed-riesz}
Let $L \colon C_c(X) \to \RR$ be linear with $\sup\{|L(\varphi)| : |\varphi| \leq 1, \operatorname{supp}\varphi \subseteq K\} < \infty$ for every compact $K$. Then there are Radon measures $\mu^+$ and
$\mu^-$, concentrated on disjoint Borel sets $X^+$ and $X^-$, with
\[
L(\varphi) = \int\varphi\,d\mu^+ - \int\varphi\,d\mu^- \qquad (\varphi \in C_c(X)).
\]
If $X$ is compact, every bounded linear functional $L$ on $C(X)$ (with the norm $\|\ \|_\infty$) has this form with $\mu^\pm$ finite, and
\[
\|L\| = \|\mu^+ - \mu^-\| = \mu^+(X) + \mu^-(X),
\]
the \emph{total variation norm} of the signed measure $\mu^+ - \mu^-$, $E \mapsto \mu^+(E) - \mu^-(E)$: the norm of the functional is the norm of the measure.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-riesz-vector} with $H = \RR$. Then $\nu = \pm1$ almost everywhere; let $X^\pm = \{\nu = \pm1\}$ and $\mu^\pm(E) = \mu(E \cap X^\pm)$, Borel measures finite on compact
sets, hence Radon by Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-radon-automatic}. Then $\int\varphi\nu\,d\mu = \int\varphi\,d\mu^+ - \int\varphi\,d\mu^-$. If $X$ is compact and $L$ is bounded, the hypothesis
holds with the constant $\|L\|$, $\mu^\pm(X) \leq \mu(X) < \infty$, and \eqref{measure-theory:equation-total-variation} with $V = X$ gives $\mu(X) = \sup\{L(\varphi) : \|\varphi\|_\infty \leq 1\} = \|L\|$.
\end{proof}

For a signed measure $\mu = \mu^+ - \mu^-$ as in the corollary, with $\|\mu\| = \mu^+(X) + \mu^-(X)$, the total variation norm is intrinsic to the set function $\mu$:
$\|\mu\| = \sup\sum_i|\mu(E_i)|$ over all finite partitions of $X$ into Borel sets $E_i$. Indeed $\sum_i|\mu(E_i)| \leq \sum_i(\mu^+(E_i) + \mu^-(E_i)) = \|\mu\|$, with equality for the partition
$\{X^+, X \setminus X^+\}$. For example $\|\delta_0 - \delta_1\| = 2$, and $\|h\lambda\| = \int|h|\,d\lambda$ for the measure $E \mapsto \int_Eh\,d\lambda$ with $h \in L^1$.
So the dual space of $C(X)$, for a compact metrisable $X$, is the space of signed Radon measures with the total variation norm; this proves the statement quoted in
Remark~\ref{measure-theory:remark-riesz-markov}(3) for the spaces of this section. The decomposition $X = X^+ \cup X^-$ is the \emph{Hahn decomposition} and $\mu^\pm$ the \emph{Jordan decomposition} of the
signed measure $\mu^+ - \mu^-$.

For a noncompact $X$ the natural Banach space is $C_0(X)$, the space of continuous $f \colon X \to \RR$ \emph{vanishing at infinity}: for every $\varepsilon > 0$ the set $\{|f| \geq \varepsilon\}$ is compact.
With the norm $\|f\|_\infty$ it is a closed subspace of the bounded continuous functions, hence complete (a uniform limit of functions vanishing at infinity vanishes at infinity), and it contains
$C_c(X)$ as a dense subspace: for $f \in C_0(X)$ and $\varepsilon > 0$, Lemma~\ref{general-topology:lemma-lch-shrinking} gives $\psi \prec X$ with $\psi = 1$ on the compact set $\{|f| \geq \varepsilon\}$, and
then $\psi f \in C_c(X)$ and $\|f - \psi f\|_\infty \leq \varepsilon$. For $X = \RR^n$, $e^{-|x|^2}$ and $1/(1 + |x|^2)$ lie in $C_0$, while the constant $1$ does not. As in Corollary~\ref{measure-theory:corollary-signed-riesz}, a
\emph{finite signed Radon measure} is a difference $\mu = \mu^+ - \mu^-$ of finite Radon measures concentrated on disjoint Borel sets, with total variation norm $\|\mu\| = \mu^+(X) + \mu^-(X)$ and
$\int f\,d\mu = \int f\,d\mu^+ - \int f\,d\mu^-$.

\begin{corollary}[The dual of $C_0(X)$]\label{measure-theory:corollary-riesz-c0}
For every bounded linear functional $L$ on $C_0(X)$ there is a unique finite signed Radon measure $\mu$ with
\[
L(f) = \int f\,d\mu \quad (f \in C_0(X)), \qquad \|L\| = \|\mu\|.
\]
Conversely every finite signed Radon measure defines in this way a bounded functional of norm $\|\mu\|$. So $C_0(X)^*$ is isometrically isomorphic to the space of finite signed Radon measures with the
total variation norm. More generally, with $H$ as above, every bounded linear functional on $C_0(X, H)$ (with the norm $\sup_x|f(x)|$) is $f \mapsto \int\langle f, \nu\rangle\,d\mu$ with $\mu$ a finite Radon
measure of mass $\mu(X) = \|L\|$ and $|\nu| = 1$ $\mu$-almost everywhere.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
We prove the vector-valued statement; the scalar one is the case $H = \RR$, with $\mu^\pm$ as in the proof of Corollary~\ref{measure-theory:corollary-signed-riesz}. The restriction of $L$ to $C_c(X, H)$
satisfies the hypothesis of Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-riesz-vector} with $M_K \leq \|L\|$, which gives $\mu$ and $\nu$. By \eqref{measure-theory:equation-total-variation} with $V = X$,
$\mu(X) = \sup\{L(f) : f \in C_c(X, H), |f| \leq 1\} \leq \|L\|$; in particular $\mu$ is finite. The functional $f \mapsto \int\langle f, \nu\rangle\,d\mu$ is defined on $C_0(X, H)$, with
$|\int\langle f, \nu\rangle\,d\mu| \leq \|f\|_\infty\mu(X)$, and it agrees with $L$ on $C_c(X, H)$. The density argument above, applied componentwise (or with $\psi f$ for $f \in C_0(X, H)$), shows that
$C_c(X, H)$ is dense in $C_0(X, H)$; two bounded functionals agreeing on a dense subspace are equal, so $L(f) = \int\langle f, \nu\rangle\,d\mu$ on $C_0(X, H)$. This also gives $\|L\| \leq \mu(X)$, so
$\|L\| = \mu(X)$. Uniqueness follows from the uniqueness in Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-riesz-vector}, applied to the restriction to $C_c(X, H)$. For the converse, let $\mu = \mu^+ - \mu^-$ with $\mu^\pm$ concentrated on disjoint Borel sets
$X^\pm$. The functional $L(f) = \int f\,d\mu$ satisfies $|L(f)| \leq \|f\|_\infty\|\mu\|$, and it is represented as in Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-riesz-vector} by the pair
$(\mu^+ + \mu^-, \mathbf{1}_{X^+} - \mathbf{1}_{X^-})$ (a finite Borel measure, hence Radon by Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-radon-automatic}). By uniqueness and the first part,
$\|L\| = (\mu^+ + \mu^-)(X) = \|\mu\|$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{measure-theory:remark-dual-bounded-continuous}
For a noncompact $X$ the corollary does not extend from $C_0(X)$ to the space $C_b(X)$ of all bounded continuous functions. For $X = \NN$ with the discrete topology, $C_0(\NN) = c_0$ is the space of
sequences tending to $0$, its dual is the space of finite signed measures on $\NN$, that is, of summable sequences $\ell^1$, and $C_b(\NN) = \ell^\infty$. A bounded linear functional $L$ on $\ell^\infty$ that
vanishes on $c_0$ and has $L(\mathbf{1}) = 1$ cannot be integration against a countably additive measure: such a measure would give mass $L(\mathbf{1}_{\{n\}}) = 0$ to each point, hence mass $0$ to $\NN$.
Such functionals exist, by the Hahn--Banach theorem (Chapter~\ref{functional-analysis}); they correspond to finitely additive measures, or to measures on a large compactification of $X$. This is why the
compactness of $X$ matters in Corollary~\ref{measure-theory:corollary-signed-riesz}, and why weak convergence of measures, in the next section, is defined by testing against $C_c(X)$; testing
against $C_b(X)$ is a stronger condition, which also requires that no mass escapes to infinity.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}\label{measure-theory:example-riesz-vector}
\begin{enumerate}
\item For $L(\varphi) = \varphi(0) - \varphi(1)$ on $C_c(\RR)$, $\mu = \delta_0 + \delta_1$, $\nu(0) = 1$ and $\nu(1) = -1$. Indeed, for open $V$, the supremum in
\eqref{measure-theory:equation-total-variation} is the number of the points $0, 1$ lying in $V$, attained by functions equal to $1$ near $0$ and $-1$ near $1$.
\item For $h \in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\RR^n)$ and $L(\varphi) = \int\varphi h\,d\lambda$, $\mu = |h|\lambda$ and $\nu = h/|h|$ where $h \neq 0$ (and $\nu = 1$ elsewhere, a $\mu$-null set). The total variation of
$L$ on $V$ is $\int_V|h|\,d\lambda$, the $L^1$ norm.
\item For the flux $L(F) = \int_0^{2\pi}\langle F(\cos t, \sin t), (\cos t, \sin t)\rangle\,dt = \int\langle F, n\rangle\,d\sigma$, with $\sigma$ the arc length measure of
Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-positive-functionals}(3), $\mu = \sigma$ and $\nu = n$ on $S^1$: the theorem recovers the curve and its unit normal from the functional.
For a $C^1$ field $F$, the divergence theorem in the plane gives $L(F) = \int_{|x| < 1}\operatorname{div}F\,d\lambda^2$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{remark}\label{measure-theory:remark-perimeter}
\emph{Sets of finite perimeter.} Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-riesz-vector}(3) suggests a definition of boundary measure for arbitrary sets. For a Lebesgue measurable $E \subseteq \RR^n$ consider
$L_E(F) = \int_E\operatorname{div}F\,d\lambda^n$ for $C^1$ vector fields $F$ with compact support. If $\sup\{L_E(F) : |F| \leq 1, \operatorname{supp}F \subseteq K\} < \infty$ for every compact $K$, then $L_E$ extends
by uniform approximation to $C_c(\RR^n, \RR^n)$, and Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-riesz-vector} gives a Radon measure $\mu_E$, the \emph{perimeter measure}, and a unit vector field $\nu_E$ with
$\int_E\operatorname{div}F = \int\langle F, \nu_E\rangle\,d\mu_E$: a divergence theorem for $E$, valid with no regularity of $\partial E$ assumed. For a bounded open set with smooth boundary, $\mu_E$ is the
$(n-1)$-dimensional Hausdorff measure $\mathcal{H}^{n-1}$ (Section~\ref{measure-theory:section-hausdorff}) on $\partial E$ and $\nu_E$ is the outer normal. Such sets $E$ are the sets of \emph{finite perimeter} of Caccioppoli and De Giorgi, and the same construction with $\int u\operatorname{div}F$
defines functions of \emph{bounded variation}; De Giorgi's structure theorem shows that $\mu_E$ is $\mathcal{H}^{n-1}$ restricted to a countably $(n-1)$-rectifiable ``reduced boundary''
\cite[Chapter 5]{evans-gariepy}. This is the setting in which the isoperimetric problem and minimal surfaces are solved in geometric measure theory.
\end{remark}

\section{Weak convergence of measures}\label{measure-theory:section-weak-convergence}

Sequences of measures appear whenever something discrete approximates something continuous, or something smooth approximates something singular. Riemann sums are integrals against finitely many
point masses that approximate Lebesgue measure; in probability the distributions of normalised sums of random variables approximate the Gaussian distribution; smooth bumps of total mass $1$
concentrating at a point approximate the Dirac measure; and in geometric measure theory one studies limits of the area measures of a sequence of surfaces, for instance of a minimising sequence for
the area. Convergence $\mu_k(A) \to \mu(A)$ for all Borel sets $A$ is far too strong for these purposes (Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-weak-convergence}(1)). The useful notion tests the measures
against continuous functions. As in Section~\ref{measure-theory:section-radon}, $X$ is a locally compact Hausdorff space with a countable basis.

\begin{definition}\label{measure-theory:definition-weak-convergence}
A sequence of Radon measures $\mu_k$ on $X$ \emph{converges weakly} to a Radon measure $\mu$, written $\mu_k \rightharpoonup \mu$, if
\[
\int_Xf\,d\mu_k \to \int_Xf\,d\mu \qquad \text{for every } f \in C_c(X).
\]
\end{definition}

The limit is unique, by the uniqueness in Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-riesz-markov}. In probability theory this is called \emph{vague} convergence, and \emph{weak} convergence of probability
measures means testing against all bounded continuous functions; the two agree when no mass is lost (Exercise~\ref{measure-theory:exercise-weak-mass}). In functional analysis it is the weak-$*$
convergence of functionals on $C_c(X)$.

\begin{example}\label{measure-theory:example-weak-convergence}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Moving point masses.} If $x_k \to x$ in $X$, then $\delta_{x_k} \rightharpoonup \delta_x$, since $f(x_k) \to f(x)$ for continuous $f$. On $\RR$, $\delta_{1/k} \rightharpoonup \delta_0$, but
$\delta_{1/k}(\{0\}) = 0$ does not converge to $\delta_0(\{0\}) = 1$, and $\delta_{1/k}((0, 1)) = 1$ does not converge to $\delta_0((0, 1)) = 0$. So weak convergence does not imply convergence on
compact sets, nor on open sets; Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-weak-convergence-sets} gives the inequalities that do hold.
\item \emph{Riemann sums.} On $\RR$ let $\mu_k = \frac1k\sum_{j=1}^k\delta_{j/k}$. Then $\mu_k \rightharpoonup \mathbf{1}_{[0, 1]}\lambda$. Indeed $f \in C_c(\RR)$ is uniformly continuous, being
continuous with compact support; with $\omega(\delta) = \sup\{|f(s) - f(t)| : |s - t| \leq \delta\}$, which tends to $0$ with $\delta$,
\[
\Bigl|\int f\,d\mu_k - \int_0^1f\,d\lambda\Bigr| = \Bigl|\sum_{j=1}^k\int_{(j-1)/k}^{j/k}\bigl(f(j/k) - f(t)\bigr)\,dt\Bigr| \leq \omega(1/k) \to 0.
\]
Each $\mu_k$ lives on a finite set, of Lebesgue measure $0$, while the limit is absolutely continuous with respect to $\lambda$.
\item \emph{Approximate identities.} Let $\phi \geq 0$ be continuous on $\RR$ with support in $[-1, 1]$ and $\int\phi\,d\lambda = 1$, and $\phi_k(x) = k\phi(kx)$. Then $\int\phi_k\,d\lambda = 1$
(Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-change-of-variables}), $\phi_k$ vanishes outside $[-1/k, 1/k]$, and for $f \in C_c(\RR)$
\[
\Bigl|\int f\phi_k\,d\lambda - f(0)\Bigr| = \Bigl|\int\bigl(f(x) - f(0)\bigr)\phi_k(x)\,dx\Bigr| \leq \omega(1/k),
\]
so $\phi_k\lambda \rightharpoonup \delta_0$: absolutely continuous measures converge to a singular one.
\item \emph{Loss of mass.} On $\RR$, $\delta_k \rightharpoonup 0$, since every $f \in C_c(\RR)$ vanishes at $k$ for $k$ large; and $\frac1k\mathbf{1}_{[0, k]}\lambda \rightharpoonup 0$, since
$|\int_0^kf\,d\lambda|/k \leq \|f\|_1/k$. All these measures have total mass $1$, the limit has mass $0$: mass can escape to infinity, by moving away or by spreading out.
\item \emph{Oscillation.} Let $g \geq 0$ be continuous on $\RR$ and periodic of period $2\pi$, with mean value $\bar g = \frac1{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}g\,d\lambda$. Then $g(kx)\lambda \rightharpoonup \bar g\lambda$; for
instance $(1 + \sin kx)\lambda \rightharpoonup \lambda$, although the densities $1 + \sin kx$ do not converge pointwise (at $x = \pi/2$ they run through $2, 1, 0, 1, 2, \ldots$). To see this, let
$f \in C_c(\RR)$ with support in $[a, b]$ and $\varepsilon > 0$, and choose $a = a_0 < \cdots < a_N = b$ with $|f(s) - f(a_j)| \leq \varepsilon$ for $s \in [a_{j-1}, a_j]$. An interval
$[k\alpha, k\beta]$ consists of $\lfloor k(\beta - \alpha)/2\pi\rfloor$ full periods and a remainder shorter than $2\pi$, so
$\int_\alpha^\beta g(kx)\,dx = \frac1k\int_{k\alpha}^{k\beta}g(t)\,dt = (\beta - \alpha)\bar g + r$ with $|r| \leq 4\pi\|g\|_\infty/k$. Replacing $f$ by $f(a_j)$ on $[a_{j-1}, a_j]$ costs at most
$\varepsilon(b - a)\|g\|_\infty$ in $\int fg(kx)\,dx$ and in $\bar g\int f\,dx$, so
\[
\Bigl|\int f(x)g(kx)\,dx - \bar g\int f(x)\,dx\Bigr| \leq 2\varepsilon(b - a)\|g\|_\infty + \frac{4\pi N\|f\|_\infty\|g\|_\infty}{k},
\]
whose $\limsup$ as $k \to \infty$ is at most $2\varepsilon(b - a)\|g\|_\infty$. Fast oscillations average out; this is the measure-theoretic content of the Riemann--Lebesgue lemma.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{proposition}\label{measure-theory:proposition-weak-convergence-sets}
Let $\mu_k \rightharpoonup \mu$ be Radon measures on $X$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\mu(U) \leq \liminf_k\mu_k(U)$ for every open $U$.
\item $\mu(K) \geq \limsup_k\mu_k(K)$ for every compact $K$.
\item $\mu_k(A) \to \mu(A)$ for every Borel set $A$ with compact closure and $\mu(\partial A) = 0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) By inner regularity, $\mu(U)$ is the supremum of $\mu(K)$ over compact $K \subseteq U$; for such $K$, Lemma~\ref{general-topology:lemma-lch-shrinking} gives $f \prec U$ with $f = 1$ on $K$, and
$\mu(K) \leq \int f\,d\mu \leq \mu(U)$. So $\mu(U) = \sup\{\int f\,d\mu : f \prec U\}$. For $f \prec U$, $\int f\,d\mu = \lim_k\int f\,d\mu_k \leq \liminf_k\mu_k(U)$.

(2) Let $V \supseteq K$ be open. By Lemma~\ref{general-topology:lemma-lch-shrinking} there is $f \prec V$ with $f = 1$ on $K$, and $\limsup_k\mu_k(K) \leq \lim_k\int f\,d\mu_k = \int f\,d\mu \leq \mu(V)$.
By outer regularity, the infimum of $\mu(V)$ over such $V$ is $\mu(K)$.

(3) The interior $\operatorname{int}A$ and the closure $\overline{A}$ differ by $\partial A$, so $\mu(\operatorname{int}A) = \mu(A) = \mu(\overline{A})$. By (1) and (2),
$\mu(A) = \mu(\operatorname{int}A) \leq \liminf_k\mu_k(\operatorname{int}A) \leq \liminf_k\mu_k(A) \leq \limsup_k\mu_k(A) \leq \limsup_k\mu_k(\overline{A}) \leq \mu(\overline{A}) = \mu(A)$.
\end{proof}

In Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-weak-convergence}(1) the inequalities are strict: $\delta_0((0, 1)) = 0 < 1$ and $\delta_0(\{0\}) = 1 > 0$; the sets $(0, 1)$ and $\{0\}$ have the point $0$, of
$\delta_0$-measure $1$, in their boundary. In (2) of the same example, (3) says that the proportion of the points $j/k$ lying in an interval $[\alpha, \beta] \subseteq [0, 1]$ tends to $\beta - \alpha$.

The main theorem about weak convergence is a compactness theorem: bounded mass on compact sets suffices for a convergent subsequence. Its proof needs a countable supply of test functions.

\begin{lemma}\label{measure-theory:lemma-cc-separable}
Let $K_1 \subseteq K_2 \subseteq \cdots$ be an exhaustion of $X$ by compact sets with $K_m \subseteq \operatorname{int}K_{m+1}$. There is a countable set $\mathcal{D} \subseteq C_c(X)$ such that for every
$m$, every $f \in C_c(X)$ with $\operatorname{supp}f \subseteq K_m$ and every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is $g \in \mathcal{D}$ with $\operatorname{supp}g \subseteq K_{m+1}$ and $\|f - g\|_\infty < \varepsilon$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
First, $C(L, \RR)$ has a countable dense subset for every compact metric space $(L, d)$. Indeed $L$ has a countable dense subset $\{x_1, x_2, \ldots\}$ (for each $j$, finitely many balls of radius $1/j$
cover $L$; take their centres). The functions $u_i(y) = d(x_i, y)$ separate points: if $y \neq z$ and $d(x_i, y) < d(y, z)/2$, then $d(x_i, z) > d(y, z)/2 > d(x_i, y)$. By the Stone--Weierstrass theorem
(Theorem~\ref{real-analysis:theorem-stone-weierstrass}) the polynomials in finitely many of the $u_i$ are dense in $C(L, \RR)$, and so are those with rational coefficients, which form a countable set, since
the $u_i$ are bounded and real coefficients can be approximated by rational ones.

Each $K_{m+1}$ is a compact metric space, $X$ being metrisable; let $\mathcal{D}_m \subseteq C(K_{m+1}, \RR)$ be countable and dense. By Lemma~\ref{general-topology:lemma-lch-shrinking} choose
$\chi_m \in C_c(X)$ with values in $[0, 1]$, $\chi_m = 1$ on $K_m$ and $\operatorname{supp}\chi_m \subseteq \operatorname{int}K_{m+1}$. For $u \in \mathcal{D}_m$ the function $\chi_mu$, extended by $0$
outside $K_{m+1}$, is continuous on $X$: it is continuous on the open set $\operatorname{int}K_{m+1}$ and vanishes on the open set $X \setminus \operatorname{supp}\chi_m$, and these two open sets cover $X$.
Let $\mathcal{D}$ be the set of all these functions, for all $m$. Given $f$ with support in $K_m$, choose $u \in \mathcal{D}_m$ with $|f - u| < \varepsilon$ on $K_{m+1}$; then $f = \chi_mf$, so
$|f - \chi_mu| = \chi_m|f - u| < \varepsilon$ on $K_{m+1}$, and both functions vanish outside $K_{m+1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Weak compactness of Radon measures]\label{measure-theory:theorem-weak-compactness}
Let $(\mu_k)$ be a sequence of Radon measures on $X$ with $\sup_k\mu_k(K) < \infty$ for every compact $K \subseteq X$. Then a subsequence converges weakly to a Radon measure.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $(K_m)$ be an exhaustion as in Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-cc-separable}, $C_m = \sup_k\mu_k(K_m)$, and $\mathcal{D} = \{g_1, g_2, \ldots\}$ the countable set of the lemma. For each $i$ the
sequence $\int g_i\,d\mu_k$ is bounded, by $\|g_i\|_\infty\sup_k\mu_k(\operatorname{supp}g_i)$, so it has a convergent subsequence (Theorem~\ref{real-analysis:theorem-bolzano-weierstrass-real}).
Choose a subsequence along which $\int g_1\,d\mu_k$ converges, a further subsequence along which $\int g_2\,d\mu_k$ converges, and so on; the diagonal sequence, the $k$-th term of the $k$-th
subsequence, is a subsequence of each of them from its $i$-th term on. Renaming it $(\mu_k)$, $\int g\,d\mu_k$ converges for every $g \in \mathcal{D}$.

Let $f \in C_c(X)$, with support in some $K_m$, and $\varepsilon > 0$. Choose $g \in \mathcal{D}$ with support in $K_{m+1}$ and $\|f - g\|_\infty < \varepsilon$. Then
\[
\Bigl|\int f\,d\mu_k - \int f\,d\mu_l\Bigr| \leq 2\varepsilon C_{m+1} + \Bigl|\int g\,d\mu_k - \int g\,d\mu_l\Bigr|,
\]
so $\int f\,d\mu_k$ is a Cauchy sequence. Its limit $\Lambda f$ is linear and positive in $f$, so by Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-riesz-markov} it is $\int f\,d\mu$ for a Radon measure $\mu$, and
$\mu_k \rightharpoonup \mu$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{measure-theory:corollary-probability-compact}
Let $X$ be a compact metric space. Every sequence of Borel probability measures on $X$ has a subsequence converging weakly to a Borel probability measure.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
A compact metric space is locally compact and Hausdorff, and it has a countable basis: the balls of radius $1/j$ centred at the points of finite sets of such balls covering $X$. Every Borel measure on
$X$ that is finite is a Radon measure (Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-radon-automatic}), and $\mu_k(X) = 1$ bounds the masses. Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-weak-compactness} gives a
weakly convergent subsequence, and its limit $\mu$ satisfies $\mu(X) = \int 1\,d\mu = \lim\int 1\,d\mu_k = 1$, as $1 \in C_c(X)$ for compact $X$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{measure-theory:remark-weak-compactness}
\begin{enumerate}
\item The hypothesis cannot be dropped: $k\delta_0$ has no weakly convergent subsequence on $\RR$. On non-compact spaces the limit of probability measures may have smaller mass, by
