\begin{lemma}\label{measure-theory:lemma-normal-determinant}
Let $v_1, \ldots, v_{n-1} \in \RR^n$ be linearly independent, $V$ the $n \times (n - 1)$ matrix with these columns, $\nu$ a unit vector orthogonal to all $v_i$, and $w \in \RR^n$. Then
\[
|\det(v_1, \ldots, v_{n-1}, w)| = JV \cdot |\langle w, \nu\rangle| .
\]
\end{lemma}

In words: the volume of a parallelepiped is the $(n-1)$-dimensional volume of its base times its height. Recall that $JV = \sqrt{\det(V^TV)}$ is the $(n-1)$-dimensional volume of the
base (Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-jacobian}).

\begin{proof}
Write $w = u + \langle w, \nu\rangle\nu$ with $u$ in the span of the $v_i$. The determinant is linear in the last column and vanishes when the columns are dependent, so
$\det(V, w) = \langle w, \nu\rangle\det(V, \nu)$. For the matrix $M = (V, \nu)$, $M^TM$ is block diagonal with blocks $V^TV$ and $\langle\nu, \nu\rangle = 1$, because $V^T\nu = 0$. Hence
$(\det M)^2 = \det(M^TM) = \det(V^TV) = (JV)^2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Coarea formula]\label{measure-theory:theorem-coarea}
Let $U \subseteq \RR^n$ be open and $f \colon U \to \RR$ of class $C^1$ with $\nabla f(x) \neq 0$ for all $x \in U$. For every Borel set $A \subseteq U$ the function
$t \mapsto \mathcal{H}^{n-1}(A \cap f^{-1}(t))$ is Lebesgue measurable, and
\[
\int_A|\nabla f|\,d\lambda^n = \int_\RR\mathcal{H}^{n-1}\bigl(A \cap f^{-1}(t)\bigr)\,dt .
\]
More generally, for every Borel function $g \colon U \to [0, \infty]$,
\begin{equation}\label{measure-theory:equation-coarea}
\int_Ug\,|\nabla f|\,d\lambda^n = \int_\RR\Bigl(\int_{f^{-1}(t)}g\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}\Bigr)dt .
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Step 1: local coordinates adapted to $f$.} Let $x_0 \in U$. Some partial derivative $\partial_if(x_0)$ is nonzero. A permutation of the coordinates is an isometry of $\RR^n$, so it preserves
$\lambda^n$ (Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-linear-change}), $\mathcal{H}^{n-1}$ and $|\nabla f|$; so we may assume $\partial_nf(x_0) \neq 0$. The map
$\psi(x) = (x_1, \ldots, x_{n-1}, f(x))$ has $\det D\psi(x_0) = \partial_nf(x_0) \neq 0$. By the inverse function theorem (Theorem~\ref{real-analysis:theorem-inverse-function}) there are open sets
$V \ni x_0$ and $W$ such that $\psi \colon V \to W$ is a $C^1$-diffeomorphism. Let $\phi = \psi^{-1} \colon W \to V$, and write points of $W \subseteq \RR^{n-1} \times \RR$ as $(y, t)$. Since
$\psi(\phi(y, t)) = (y, t)$,
\[
f(\phi(y, t)) = t \qquad\text{for } (y, t) \in W .
\]
For fixed $t$ let $W_t = \{y : (y, t) \in W\}$, open in $\RR^{n-1}$, and $\phi_t = \phi(-, t) \colon W_t \to \RR^n$. It is injective and $C^1$, and $D\phi_t$ is injective, as its columns are the first
$n - 1$ columns of the invertible matrix $D\phi$. Its image is $V \cap f^{-1}(t)$: a point $x \in V$ has $f(x) = t$ if and only if $\psi(x) = (y, t)$ for some $y$.

\emph{Step 2: the Jacobian identity.} We claim $|\det D\phi(y, t)|\,|\nabla f(\phi(y, t))| = J\phi_t(y)$. Differentiating $f(\phi(y, t)) = t$ with the chain rule gives
$\langle\nabla f, \partial_{y_i}\phi\rangle = 0$ for $i < n$ and $\langle\nabla f, \partial_t\phi\rangle = 1$, all evaluated at $\phi(y, t)$ resp.\ $(y, t)$. So $\nu = \nabla f/|\nabla f|$ is a unit vector
orthogonal to the columns $\partial_{y_i}\phi$ of $D\phi_t$, and Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-normal-determinant} with $w = \partial_t\phi$ gives
\[
|\det D\phi| = J\phi_t\cdot|\langle\partial_t\phi, \nu\rangle| = \frac{J\phi_t}{|\nabla f|} .
\]

\emph{Step 3: the formula on $V$.} Let $A \subseteq V$ be Borel, and $h = \mathbf{1}_A \circ \phi$, a Borel function on $W$. By the change of variables formula
(Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-change-of-variables}) for $\phi \colon W \to V$ and Step 2,
\[
\int_A|\nabla f|\,d\lambda^n = \int_Wh(y, t)\,|\nabla f(\phi(y, t))|\,|\det D\phi(y, t)|\,d\lambda^n(y, t) = \int_Wh(y, t)\,J\phi_t(y)\,d\lambda^n(y, t) .
\]
The integrand, extended by $0$ outside $W$, is a nonnegative Borel function on $\RR^{n-1} \times \RR$. By Tonelli's theorem (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-fubini} and
Remark~\ref{measure-theory:remark-lebesgue-product}) the function $t \mapsto \int_{W_t}h(y, t)J\phi_t(y)\,dy$ is measurable and the last integral equals
$\int_\RR\bigl(\int_{W_t}h(y, t)J\phi_t(y)\,dy\bigr)dt$. By the area formula (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-area-formula}) for $\phi_t$ and the Borel set
$B_t = \{y \in W_t : \phi(y, t) \in A\}$, whose image is $A \cap f^{-1}(t)$ by Step 1,
\[
\int_{W_t}h(y, t)J\phi_t(y)\,dy = \int_{B_t}J\phi_t\,d\lambda^{n-1} = \mathcal{H}^{n-1}\bigl(A \cap f^{-1}(t)\bigr).
\]
This proves the theorem for Borel subsets of $V$.

\emph{Step 4: globalisation.} $U$ is a countable union of compact sets, each covered by finitely many of the open sets $V$ of Step 1; so $U = \bigcup_jV_j$ for countably many such sets. Let
$E_j = V_j \setminus \bigcup_{i < j}V_i$, disjoint Borel sets covering $U$. For a Borel set $A \subseteq U$, apply Step 3 to $A \cap E_j \subseteq V_j$ and sum over $j$. On the left,
$\sum_j\int_{A \cap E_j}|\nabla f| = \int_A|\nabla f|$ by monotone convergence. On the right, for every $t$ the sets $A \cap E_j \cap f^{-1}(t)$ are disjoint Borel sets with union
$A \cap f^{-1}(t)$, and $\mathcal{H}^{n-1}$ is a measure on Borel sets (Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-hausdorff}), so
$\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(A \cap f^{-1}(t)) = \sum_j\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(A \cap E_j \cap f^{-1}(t))$, a sum of measurable functions of $t$; integrate with monotone convergence
(Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-monotone-convergence}). Finally, \eqref{measure-theory:equation-coarea} is the first formula for $g = \mathbf{1}_A$; it follows for simple Borel functions by
linearity and for all Borel $g \geq 0$ by monotone convergence, applied on both sides.
\end{proof}

For $f(x) = x_n$ the coarea formula is Fubini's theorem. Its most important special case is integration in polar coordinates.

\begin{corollary}[Polar coordinates]\label{measure-theory:corollary-polar}
Let $S^{n-1} = \{x \in \RR^n : |x| = 1\}$ and $\omega_n = \lambda^n(B(0, 1))$. Then $\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(S^{n-1}) = n\omega_n$, and for every Borel function $g \colon \RR^n \to [0, \infty]$,
\[
\int_{\RR^n}g\,d\lambda^n = \int_0^\infty\Bigl(\int_{|x| = r}g\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}\Bigr)dr = \int_0^\infty r^{n-1}\Bigl(\int_{S^{n-1}}g(r\theta)\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\theta)\Bigr)dr .
\]
In particular, if $g(x) = h(|x|)$ is radial, $\int_{\RR^n}g\,d\lambda^n = n\omega_n\int_0^\infty h(r)r^{n-1}\,dr$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-coarea} to $f(x) = |x|$ on $U = \RR^n \setminus \{0\}$. Here $\nabla f(x) = x/|x|$, so $|\nabla f| = 1$, and the point $0$ is a null set. This gives the
first equality. For the second, $\theta \mapsto r\theta$ is a similarity with ratio $r$ from $S^{n-1}$ onto the sphere of radius $r$, so
$\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\{|x| = r\} \cap G) = r^{n-1}\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(S^{n-1} \cap r^{-1}G)$ for Borel sets $G$ (Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-hausdorff}(3)), which is the second equality for
$g = \mathbf{1}_G$; pass to simple functions and to limits by monotone convergence. For $g = \mathbf{1}_{B(0, 1)}$ we get $\omega_n = \mathcal{H}^{n-1}(S^{n-1})\int_0^1r^{n-1}\,dr = \mathcal{H}^{n-1}(S^{n-1})/n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}\label{measure-theory:example-polar}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Spheres.} $\mathcal{H}^1(S^1) = 2\omega_2 = 2\pi$ and $\mathcal{H}^2(S^2) = 3\omega_3 = 3 \cdot \frac43\pi = 4\pi$, in agreement with Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-area-formula}. The
formula $\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(S^{n-1}) = n\omega_n$ says that the volume of the ball, $\omega_nR^n$, has derivative $n\omega_nR^{n-1}$ in $R$ equal to the area of the sphere of radius $R$: adding a thin shell of
thickness $dR$ adds volume (area)$\cdot dR$.
\item \emph{Integrability of powers.} For $a \in \RR$, $\int_{|x| < 1}|x|^{-a}\,dx = n\omega_n\int_0^1r^{n-1-a}\,dr$ is finite if and only if $a < n$, and $\int_{|x| > 1}|x|^{-a}\,dx = n\omega_n\int_1^\infty r^{n-1-a}\,dr$
is finite if and only if $a > n$. So $|x|^{-a}$ is never integrable on all of $\RR^n$, and the borderline exponent is the dimension, as for $\sum_{k \in \ZZ^n \setminus 0}|k|^{-a}$.
\item \emph{The volume of balls.} By Fubini's theorem and Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-gaussian}, $\int_{\RR^n}e^{-|x|^2}dx = (\int_\RR e^{-t^2}dt)^n = \pi^{n/2}$. By the corollary and the
substitution $u = r^2$ it also equals $n\omega_n\int_0^\infty e^{-r^2}r^{n-1}\,dr = \frac{n\omega_n}2\int_0^\infty e^{-u}u^{n/2 - 1}\,du = \frac{n\omega_n}2\Gamma(\frac n2)$. Hence
$\omega_n = \pi^{n/2}/\Gamma(\frac n2 + 1)$, which solves Exercise~\ref{measure-theory:exercise-ball-volume}: $\omega_1 = 2$, $\omega_2 = \pi$, $\omega_3 = \frac43\pi$, $\omega_4 = \frac{\pi^2}{2}$, and
$\omega_n \to 0$ very fast as $n \to \infty$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{remark}\label{measure-theory:remark-coarea}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Critical points.} For a Lipschitz $f$ the coarea formula holds without excluding critical points \cite[Section 3.4]{evans-gariepy}. On the critical set $Z = \{\nabla f = 0\}$ the left side
vanishes, so the formula contains the statement that $\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(Z \cap f^{-1}(t)) = 0$ for almost every $t$. This is remarkable because the set of critical values $f(Z)$ can have positive
measure: Sard's theorem, which says it is null, needs $f$ of class $C^n$, and Whitney constructed a $C^1$ function on $\RR^2$ that is not constant on a connected arc of critical points
\cite{whitney-1935}; its critical values fill an interval, yet each level set meets $Z$ in an $\mathcal{H}^1$-null set.
\item \emph{Isoperimetry and Sobolev inequalities.} For $u \in C^1_c(\RR^n)$ the coarea formula reads $\int|\nabla u| = \int_\RR\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\{u = t\})\,dt$ (in the general version), and
$\{u = t\}$ is, for almost every $t > 0$, the boundary of $\{u > t\}$. The \emph{isoperimetric inequality} $\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\partial E) \geq n\omega_n^{1/n}\lambda^n(E)^{(n-1)/n}$, with equality for
balls, then gives the \emph{Sobolev inequality} $\|u\|_{n/(n-1)} \leq (n\omega_n^{1/n})^{-1}\|\nabla u\|_1$ with its best constant; conversely, the Sobolev inequality applied to smoothed indicator functions
gives back the isoperimetric inequality. This equivalence between a geometric and an analytic inequality is due to Federer and Fleming and to Maz'ya \cite{federer-fleming-1960}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\section{Densities and rectifiability}\label{measure-theory:section-densities}

By the Lebesgue density theorem (Corollary~\ref{measure-theory:corollary-density}) a measurable set $A \subseteq \RR^n$ fills almost all of every small ball centred at almost every one of its
points. For a set of dimension $s < n$ the analogous question is whether $\mathcal{H}^s(A \cap \overline{B}(x, r))$ is comparable to $\alpha(s)(2r)^s$, the $s$-dimensional ``volume'' of a ball of radius
$r$. For smooth curves and surfaces the answer is yes, with ratio tending to $1$; for the Cantor set the ratio oscillates forever. This difference turns out to be the difference between the sets on which
one can do calculus, the rectifiable ones, and the others. Throughout, $X$ is a metric space and $\overline{B}(x, r) = \{y : d(x, y) \leq r\}$.

\begin{definition}\label{measure-theory:definition-density}
For $A \subseteq X$, $s > 0$ and $x \in X$, the \emph{upper} and \emph{lower $s$-dimensional densities} of $A$ at $x$ are
\[
\Theta^{*s}(A, x) = \limsup_{r \to 0}\frac{\mathcal{H}^s(A \cap \overline{B}(x, r))}{\alpha(s)(2r)^s}, \qquad \Theta^s_*(A, x) = \liminf_{r \to 0}\frac{\mathcal{H}^s(A \cap \overline{B}(x, r))}{\alpha(s)(2r)^s}.
\]
If they are equal, the common value is the \emph{density} $\Theta^s(A, x)$.
\end{definition}

The normalisation is chosen so that flat pieces have density $1$. For $A \subseteq \RR^n$ Lebesgue measurable and $s = n$, $\mathcal{H}^n = \lambda^n$ and $\alpha(n)(2r)^n = \lambda^n(\overline{B}(x, r))$,
so by the density theorem $\Theta^n(A, x) = 1$ for almost every $x \in A$ and $\Theta^n(A, x) = 0$ for almost every $x \notin A$ (spheres are null, so closed and open balls give the same ratios).

\begin{example}\label{measure-theory:example-density-curve}
\emph{Curves have density $1$.} Let $\gamma \colon (a, b) \to \RR^m$ be an injective $C^1$ map with $\gamma^{\prime} \neq 0$ which is a homeomorphism onto its image $\Gamma$. Then
$\Theta^1(\Gamma, x) = 1$ for every $x \in \Gamma$. Let $x = \gamma(t_0)$, $c = |\gamma^{\prime}(t_0)| > 0$, and $0 < \varepsilon < 1$. Choose $\delta > 0$ with $[t_0 - \delta, t_0 + \delta] \subseteq (a, b)$
and $|\gamma^{\prime}(t) - \gamma^{\prime}(t_0)| \leq \varepsilon c$ there. By the mean value inequality, applied to $\gamma(t) - \gamma^{\prime}(t_0)(t - t_0)$,
\[
(1 - \varepsilon)c|t - t_0| \leq |\gamma(t) - x| \leq (1 + \varepsilon)c|t - t_0| \qquad (|t - t_0| \leq \delta),
\]
and $(1 - \varepsilon)c \leq |\gamma^{\prime}(t)| \leq (1 + \varepsilon)c$ there. Since $\gamma^{-1}$ is continuous at $x$, there is $r_0 \in (0, (1 - \varepsilon)c\delta]$ such that
$|\gamma(t) - x| \leq r_0$ implies $|t - t_0| \leq \delta$. For $r < r_0$ the set $I_r = \{t : |\gamma(t) - x| \leq r\}$ therefore satisfies
\[
\bigl[t_0 - \tfrac{r}{(1 + \varepsilon)c}, t_0 + \tfrac{r}{(1 + \varepsilon)c}\bigr] \subseteq I_r \subseteq \bigl[t_0 - \tfrac{r}{(1 - \varepsilon)c}, t_0 + \tfrac{r}{(1 - \varepsilon)c}\bigr].
\]
By the area formula (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-area-formula}), $\mathcal{H}^1(\Gamma \cap \overline{B}(x, r)) = \int_{I_r}|\gamma^{\prime}|$ lies between $2r\frac{1 - \varepsilon}{1 + \varepsilon}$ and
$2r\frac{1 + \varepsilon}{1 - \varepsilon}$. As $\alpha(1) = 1$ and $\varepsilon$ was arbitrary, the ratio tends to $1$. The homeomorphism condition matters: for a curve shaped like the digit $6$, whose end
$\gamma(t)$, $t \to b$, runs into an interior point $x$ from one side, a small ball around $x$ contains the arc of the curve through $x$, of length about $2r$, and the end arc, of length about $r$, and $\Theta^1(\Gamma, x) = \frac32$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}\label{measure-theory:example-density-cantor}
\emph{The Cantor set has no density at $0$.} Let $C$ be the Cantor set, $s = \log 2/\log 3$, and $c = \mathcal{H}^s(C)$, so that $\alpha(s)/2 \leq c \leq \alpha(s)$
(Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-cantor-dimension}). For $k \geq 0$, $C \cap [0, 3^{-k}] = 3^{-k}C$ (the piece $C_w$ for $w = 1\cdots1$ of length $k$, in the notation of
Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-ifs}), so $\mathcal{H}^s(C \cap [0, 3^{-k}]) = 3^{-ks}c = 2^{-k}c$. Moreover $C$ does not meet the gap $(3^{-k-1}, 2 \cdot 3^{-k-1})$, so
$C \cap [0, 2 \cdot 3^{-k-1}]$ is $C \cap [0, 3^{-k-1}]$ plus the single point $2 \cdot 3^{-k-1}$. Hence, with $r = 3^{-k}$ and $r = 2 \cdot 3^{-k-1}$ respectively,
\[
\frac{\mathcal{H}^s(C \cap \overline{B}(0, 3^{-k}))}{\alpha(s)(2 \cdot 3^{-k})^s} = \frac{2^{-k}c}{\alpha(s)2^s2^{-k}} = \frac{c}{\alpha(s)2^s}, \qquad
\frac{\mathcal{H}^s(C \cap \overline{B}(0, 2 \cdot 3^{-k-1}))}{\alpha(s)(4 \cdot 3^{-k-1})^s} = \frac{2^{-k-1}c}{\alpha(s)4^s2^{-k-1}} = \frac{c}{\alpha(s)4^s}.
\]
So $\Theta^{*s}(C, 0) \geq c/(\alpha(s)2^s)$ and $\Theta^s_*(C, 0) \leq c/(\alpha(s)4^s)$, which is smaller by the factor $2^s \approx 1.55$; as $c > 0$, the density does not exist. The same computation
works at the left endpoint $f_w(0)$ of every interval $I_w$: for $r < 3^{-|w|}$ the ball $\overline{B}(f_w(0), r)$ meets $C$ in $f_w(C \cap [0, 3^{|w|}r])$, because $C$ has a gap of length at least
$3^{-|w|}$ to the left of $I_w$ (Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-cantor-dimension}(2)), and $\mathcal{H}^s$ scales by $3^{-|w|s}$ under $f_w$. These endpoints are dense in $C$.
\end{example}

For a general set we cannot expect the density to exist, but the upper density is bounded below almost everywhere, by a short covering argument that uses nothing but the definition of $\mathcal{H}^s$.

\begin{theorem}\label{measure-theory:theorem-upper-density}
Let $A \subseteq X$ with $\mathcal{H}^s(A) < \infty$, $s > 0$. Then $\Theta^{*s}(A, x) \geq 2^{-s}$ for $\mathcal{H}^s$-almost every $x \in A$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $0 < t < 2^{-s}$ and $k \geq 1$ let
\[
A_{t, k} = \bigl\{x \in A : \mathcal{H}^s(A \cap \overline{B}(x, r)) \leq t\,\alpha(s)(2r)^s \text{ for all } 0 < r < 1/k\bigr\}.
\]
A point $x \in A$ with $\Theta^{*s}(A, x) < 2^{-s}$ lies in some $A_{t, k}$ with $t$ rational; so it suffices to show $\mathcal{H}^s(A_{t, k}) = 0$ for all $t$ and $k$, by countable subadditivity.

Fix $t, k$, let $0 < \delta < 1/k$ and $\varepsilon > 0$, and choose a cover $(C_i)$ of $A_{t, k}$ by sets of diameter at most $\delta$ with
$\sum_i\alpha(s)(\operatorname{diam}C_i)^s \leq \mathcal{H}^s_\delta(A_{t, k}) + \varepsilon$. We may discard the $C_i$ that do not meet $A_{t, k}$. For the others pick $x_i \in C_i \cap A_{t, k}$ and let
$d_i = \operatorname{diam}C_i$. If $d_i > 0$, then $C_i \subseteq \overline{B}(x_i, d_i)$ with $d_i < 1/k$, so by the definition of $A_{t, k}$
\[
\mathcal{H}^s(A_{t, k} \cap C_i) \leq \mathcal{H}^s(A \cap \overline{B}(x_i, d_i)) \leq t\,\alpha(s)(2d_i)^s = t2^s\,\alpha(s)d_i^s .
\]
If $d_i = 0$, then $C_i$ is a point and both sides are $0$. Summing, and using countable subadditivity of $\mathcal{H}^s$,
\[
\mathcal{H}^s(A_{t, k}) \leq \sum_i\mathcal{H}^s(A_{t, k} \cap C_i) \leq t2^s\bigl(\mathcal{H}^s_\delta(A_{t, k}) + \varepsilon\bigr) \leq t2^s\bigl(\mathcal{H}^s(A_{t, k}) + \varepsilon\bigr).
\]
Let $\varepsilon \to 0$. Since $\mathcal{H}^s(A_{t, k}) \leq \mathcal{H}^s(A) < \infty$ and $t2^s < 1$, this forces $\mathcal{H}^s(A_{t, k}) = 0$.
\end{proof}

The bound $2^{-s}$ comes from the comparison $C_i \subseteq \overline{B}(x_i, \operatorname{diam}C_i)$, which loses a factor $2$ in the radius. Inequalities in the other direction, bounding the
upper density from above, need a different idea: instead of starting from an arbitrary cover and enclosing its sets in balls, one starts from balls on which the measure is large and extracts disjoint
ones. This is a covering lemma of Vitali type, and it works in every metric space. For a closed ball $B = \overline{B}(x, r)$, given together with its centre and radius, write $\widehat{B} = \overline{B}(x, 5r)$.

\begin{lemma}[$5r$-covering lemma]\label{measure-theory:lemma-five-r-covering}
Let $\mathcal{F}$ be a family of closed balls $\overline{B}(x, r)$, $r > 0$, in a metric space, with $\sup\{r\} = R < \infty$. There is a subfamily $\mathcal{G}$ of pairwise disjoint balls such that every
$B \in \mathcal{F}$ meets some $B^{\prime} \in \mathcal{G}$ whose radius is at least half the radius of $B$; in particular $B \subseteq \widehat{B^{\prime}}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $\mathcal{F}_j$ be the set of balls in $\mathcal{F}$ with radius in $(2^{-j}R, 2^{1-j}R]$, $j \geq 1$. By Zorn's lemma choose a maximal pairwise disjoint subfamily $\mathcal{G}_1$ of $\mathcal{F}_1$ (the union of a chain of
disjoint subfamilies is disjoint), and inductively a maximal pairwise disjoint subfamily $\mathcal{G}_j$ of the balls in $\mathcal{F}_j$ that are disjoint from all balls of
$\mathcal{G}_1 \cup \cdots \cup \mathcal{G}_{j-1}$. Let $\mathcal{G} = \bigcup_j\mathcal{G}_j$; it is pairwise disjoint. Let $B = \overline{B}(x, r) \in \mathcal{F}_j$. Either $B$ meets a ball of some $\mathcal{G}_i$ with $i < j$,
whose radius exceeds $2^{-i}R \geq 2^{1-j}R \geq r$; or it is disjoint from all of them, and then by maximality of $\mathcal{G}_j$ it meets a ball of $\mathcal{G}_j$, whose radius exceeds $2^{-j}R \geq r/2$. In both
cases $B$ meets $B^{\prime} = \overline{B}(x^{\prime}, r^{\prime}) \in \mathcal{G}$ with $r^{\prime} \geq r/2$. If $z \in B \cap B^{\prime}$ and $y \in B$, then
$d(y, x^{\prime}) \leq d(y, x) + d(x, z) + d(z, x^{\prime}) \leq 2r + r^{\prime} \leq 5r^{\prime}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}\label{measure-theory:theorem-density-lower-comparison}
Let $\mu$ be an outer measure on a metric space $X$ for which all Borel sets are $\mu$-measurable (for instance $\mu = \mathcal{H}^s$, by Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-metric-outer-measure}). Let
$A_1, A_2 \subseteq X$, $s > 0$ and $t \geq 0$, and suppose
\[
\Theta^{*s}(\mu, A_2, x) = \limsup_{r \to 0}\frac{\mu(A_2 \cap \overline{B}(x, r))}{\alpha(s)(2r)^s} \geq t \qquad \text{for all } x \in A_1 .
\]
Then $t\,\mathcal{H}^s(A_1) \leq \mu(A_2)$.
\end{theorem}

A Borel measure $\mu$ on $X$ defines such an outer measure, $\mu^*(E) = \inf\{\mu(B) : B \supseteq E \text{ Borel}\}$. The infimum is attained, by the intersection of Borel sets $B_j \supseteq E$ with
$\mu(B_j) \to \mu^*(E)$; and for $B$ Borel, $E \subseteq X$ and a Borel $E^{\prime} \supseteq E$ with $\mu(E^{\prime}) = \mu^*(E)$,
$\mu^*(E \cap B) + \mu^*(E \setminus B) \leq \mu(E^{\prime} \cap B) + \mu(E^{\prime} \setminus B) = \mu^*(E)$, so $B$ is $\mu^*$-measurable. So the theorem applies to Borel measures as well.
This is the form of the theorem in \cite{simon-gmt}, where $A_1 \subseteq A_2$ is assumed; the proof below does not use this inclusion. The sets $A_1$ and $A_2$ need not be measurable. The
important special case is $A_1 = A_2$; the freedom to choose $A_2$ is used to localise, with $A_2 = A \cap U$ for an open set $U \supseteq A_1$. Note the direction: Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-upper-density} and its generalisation Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-density-comparison} below bound $\mu$ from above where the density is small;
this theorem bounds $\mu$ from below where the density is large, and loses no factor $2^s$.

\begin{proof}
We may assume $t > 0$ and $\mu(A_2) < \infty$. Let $0 < t^{\prime} < t$ and $\delta > 0$, and let $\mathcal{F}$ be the family of closed balls $\overline{B}(x, r)$ with $x \in A_1$, $0 < r < \delta$ and
$\mu(A_2 \cap \overline{B}(x, r)) \geq t^{\prime}\alpha(s)(2r)^s$. By hypothesis every $x \in A_1$ is the centre of balls of $\mathcal{F}$ of arbitrarily small radius. Let $\mathcal{G}$ be as in
Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-five-r-covering}.

The balls of $\mathcal{G}$ are disjoint Borel sets, hence $\mu$-measurable, and by the definition of measurability $\mu(A_2) \geq \sum_{B \in \mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\mu(A_2 \cap B)$ for every finite
$\mathcal{G}^{\prime} \subseteq \mathcal{G}$ (split $A_2$ successively along the balls). Each term is at least $t^{\prime}\alpha(s)(2r_B)^s > 0$, so only finitely many balls of $\mathcal{G}$ have $\mu(A_2 \cap B) > 1/m$, for each
$m$; hence $\mathcal{G}$ is countable, say $\mathcal{G} = \{B_1, B_2, \ldots\}$ with radii $r_i$, and
\[
\sum_it^{\prime}\alpha(s)(2r_i)^s \leq \sum_i\mu(A_2 \cap B_i) \leq \mu(A_2) < \infty .
\]

We claim that $A_1 \subseteq \bigcup_{i \leq N}B_i \cup \bigcup_{i > N}\widehat{B_i}$ for every $N$. Let $x \in A_1$ lie outside the closed set $B_1 \cup \cdots \cup B_N$. Some closed ball around $x$ misses this set, so
there is $B \in \mathcal{F}$ centred at $x$ and disjoint from $B_1, \ldots, B_N$. By the lemma $B$ meets some $B_i$ with $B \subseteq \widehat{B_i}$, and necessarily $i > N$; so $x \in \widehat{B_i}$.

The sets $B_i$ and $\widehat{B_i}$ have diameters at most $2r_i < 2\delta$ and $10r_i < 10\delta$. Hence
\[
\mathcal{H}^s_{10\delta}(A_1) \leq \sum_{i \leq N}\alpha(s)(2r_i)^s + \sum_{i > N}\alpha(s)(10r_i)^s \leq \frac{\mu(A_2)}{t^{\prime}} + 5^s\sum_{i > N}\alpha(s)(2r_i)^s .
\]
The last sum is the tail of a convergent series, so letting $N \to \infty$ gives $\mathcal{H}^s_{10\delta}(A_1) \leq \mu(A_2)/t^{\prime}$. Letting $\delta \to 0$ and then $t^{\prime} \to t$ proves the theorem.
\end{proof}

Together with Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-upper-density} this gives the basic density estimates for sets of finite Hausdorff measure, in every metric space.

\begin{theorem}[Upper density theorem]\label{measure-theory:theorem-density-bounds}
Let $A \subseteq X$ be $\mathcal{H}^s$-measurable with $\mathcal{H}^s(A) < \infty$, $s > 0$. Then $2^{-s} \leq \Theta^{*s}(A, x) \leq 1$ for $\mathcal{H}^s$-almost every $x \in A$, and $\Theta^{*s}(A, x) = 0$ for
$\mathcal{H}^s$-almost every $x \in X \setminus A$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The lower bound is Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-upper-density}. Let $\nu(E) = \mathcal{H}^s(A \cap E)$ for Borel $E$; since $A$ and $E$ are $\mathcal{H}^s$-measurable, $\nu$ is a finite Borel measure, so
by Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-closed-approximation} every Borel set $E$ has open neighbourhoods $U$ with $\nu(U) < \nu(E) + \varepsilon$. For $x$ in an open set $U$, small balls around $x$ lie in $U$, so
$\Theta^{*s}(\mathcal{H}^s, A \cap U, x) = \Theta^{*s}(A, x)$.

\emph{Upper bound.} Let $t > 1$ and $B = \{x \in A : \Theta^{*s}(A, x) > t\}$. By Borel regularity (Remark~\ref{measure-theory:remark-hausdorff-conventions}(2)) there is a Borel set $E \supseteq B$ with
$\mathcal{H}^s(E) = \mathcal{H}^s(B)$, so $\nu(E) = \mathcal{H}^s(B)$, as $B \subseteq A \cap E \subseteq E$. For an open $U \supseteq E$ with $\nu(U) < \nu(E) + \varepsilon$,
Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-density-lower-comparison} with $\mu = \mathcal{H}^s$, $A_1 = B$, $A_2 = A \cap U$ gives $t\mathcal{H}^s(B) \leq \mathcal{H}^s(A \cap U) = \nu(U) < \mathcal{H}^s(B) + \varepsilon$. As
$\varepsilon$ is arbitrary and $\mathcal{H}^s(B) < \infty$, $(t - 1)\mathcal{H}^s(B) \leq 0$, so $\mathcal{H}^s(B) = 0$. Taking $t = 1 + 1/j$, $j \geq 1$, gives the upper bound.

\emph{Outside $A$.} Let $t > 0$ and $B = \{x \in X \setminus A : \Theta^{*s}(A, x) > t\}$. Choose a Borel set $A^{\prime} \supseteq A$ with $\mathcal{H}^s(A^{\prime}) = \mathcal{H}^s(A)$; since $A$ is measurable and of finite
measure, $\mathcal{H}^s(A^{\prime} \setminus A) = 0$, so $B \cap A^{\prime}$ is null. The Borel set $X \setminus A^{\prime}$ has $\nu(X \setminus A^{\prime}) = 0$, so it has open neighbourhoods $U$ with $\nu(U) < \varepsilon$, and
the theorem with $A_1 = B \setminus A^{\prime}$, $A_2 = A \cap U$ gives $t\mathcal{H}^s(B \setminus A^{\prime}) \leq \varepsilon$. Hence $B$ is null, and taking $t = 1/j$ gives the last statement.
\end{proof}

The name comes from \cite{simon-gmt}: the theorem is about upper densities only. In $\RR^n$ with $s = n$ it says less than the Lebesgue density theorem, which gives density exactly $1$ on $A$ and $0$
outside; but for non-integral $s$ the density need not exist (Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-density-cantor}), and the upper density is then the right object. For $A$ the Cantor set the theorem
says that $\Theta^{*s}(C, x) \in [2^{-s}, 1]$ at $\mathcal{H}^s$-almost every point of $C$.

So every set of finite $s$-dimensional measure is, at almost all of its points, at least somewhat dense and at most as dense as a flat piece. Whether the density exists, that is, whether the lower
density equals the upper one, is a deep geometric property, and it is related to the following class of sets.

\begin{definition}\label{measure-theory:definition-rectifiable}
Let $k \geq 1$ be an integer. A set $A \subseteq \RR^n$ is \emph{countably $k$-rectifiable} if there are Lipschitz maps $g_j \colon E_j \to \RR^n$, $E_j \subseteq \RR^k$, $j = 1, 2, \ldots$, such that
\[
\mathcal{H}^k\Bigl(A \setminus \bigcup_jg_j(E_j)\Bigr) = 0 .
\]
It is \emph{purely $k$-unrectifiable} if $\mathcal{H}^k(A \cap g(E)) = 0$ for every Lipschitz map $g \colon E \to \RR^n$ with $E \subseteq \RR^k$.
\end{definition}

So a rectifiable set is, up to a null set, a countable union of Lipschitz images of pieces of $\RR^k$, and on such pieces Rademacher's theorem and the area formula give tangent planes and a calculus. A
purely unrectifiable set meets each Lipschitz image of a piece of $\RR^k$ only in a null set.

\begin{proposition}\label{measure-theory:proposition-rectifiable}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Subsets and countable unions of countably $k$-rectifiable sets are countably $k$-rectifiable.
\item A countably $k$-rectifiable set $A$ has $\dim_{\mathcal{H}}A \leq k$, and $A \cap \bigcup_jg_j(E_j)$ has $\sigma$-finite $\mathcal{H}^k$-measure.
\item If $U \subseteq \RR^k$ is open and $g \colon U \to \RR^n$ is of class $C^1$, then $g(U)$ is countably $k$-rectifiable.
\item If $A$ is countably $k$-rectifiable and $B$ is purely $k$-unrectifiable, then $\mathcal{H}^k(A \cap B) = 0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(1) is clear from the definition: a subset can use the same maps, and countably many countable families of maps form a countable family.

(2) Let $L_j$ be a Lipschitz constant of $g_j$. For $m \geq 1$, Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-hausdorff}(3) and Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-hausdorff-lebesgue} give
$\mathcal{H}^k(g_j(E_j \cap [-m, m]^k)) \leq L_j^k\mathcal{H}^k([-m, m]^k) = L_j^k(2m)^k < \infty$. So $\bigcup_jg_j(E_j)$ is a countable union of sets of finite $\mathcal{H}^k$-measure, each of which has
$\mathcal{H}^t$-measure $0$ for $t > k$ (Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-hausdorff}(4)); the remaining part of $A$ is $\mathcal{H}^k$-null, hence also $\mathcal{H}^t$-null for $t > k$.

(3) $U$ is the union of the countably many closed balls $\overline{B} \subseteq U$ with rational centres and radii. On such a ball, which is compact and convex, $\|Dg\|$ is bounded, say by $L$, and the mean
value inequality (Proposition~\ref{real-analysis:proposition-derivative-basic}(4)) shows that $g$ is $L$-Lipschitz on $\overline{B}$.

(4) $A \cap B$ is contained in the union of the $\mathcal{H}^k$-null set $A \setminus \bigcup_jg_j(E_j)$ and the sets $B \cap g_j(E_j)$, which are $\mathcal{H}^k$-null by assumption.
\end{proof}

By (3), images of $C^1$ maps, such as the curves of Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-density-curve}, graphs of $C^1$ or Lipschitz functions, and $k$-dimensional submanifolds of $\RR^n$ (which are
covered by countably many images of charts), are countably $k$-rectifiable. At the other extreme, the four-corner Cantor set $K_4$ of Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-similarity-dimension}(2), which has
$0 < \mathcal{H}^1(K_4) < \infty$, is purely $1$-unrectifiable \cite{falconer-gofs}, \cite{mattila-1995}. The two classes are distinguished by densities and by projections, and these are among the
deepest results of the subject.

\begin{theorem}\label{measure-theory:theorem-rectifiability-criteria}
Let $A \subseteq \RR^n$ be $\mathcal{H}^k$-measurable with $0 < \mathcal{H}^k(A) < \infty$, $k$ an integer with $1 \leq k < n$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item (\emph{Densities}) $A$ is countably $k$-rectifiable if and only if $\Theta^k(A, x) = 1$ for $\mathcal{H}^k$-almost every $x \in A$, and if and only if $\Theta^k(A, x)$ exists and is positive for
$\mathcal{H}^k$-almost every $x \in A$.
\item (\emph{Projections, Besicovitch--Federer}) If $A$ is countably $k$-rectifiable, then its orthogonal projections to almost all $k$-dimensional linear subspaces have positive $\mathcal{H}^k$-measure. If
$A$ is purely $k$-unrectifiable, then its projections to almost all $k$-dimensional linear subspaces are $\mathcal{H}^k$-null.
\end{enumerate}
Here ``almost all'' refers to the rotation-invariant measure on the set of $k$-dimensional subspaces; for $k = 1$, $n = 2$ it means almost all angles. See \cite{mattila-1995}, \cite{federer-gmt}; the last
statement of (1) is Preiss's theorem \cite{preiss-1987}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}\label{measure-theory:remark-densities}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Non-integral dimensions.} If $0 < \mathcal{H}^s(A) < \infty$ and the density $\Theta^s(A, x)$ exists and is positive at almost every point of $A$, then $s$ is an integer (Marstrand, see
\cite{mattila-1995}). So sets of non-integral dimension, such as the Cantor set (Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-density-cantor}) and the self-similar sets of
Section~\ref{measure-theory:section-self-similar}, are always irregular: their densities oscillate.
\item \emph{The four-corner set again.} Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-four-corner-projections} computed two projections of $K_4$: in the direction of the axes it has length $0$, as
Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-rectifiability-criteria}(2) predicts for almost all directions, while in the direction of $(2, 1)$ it is an interval. So the exceptional set of directions in the
Besicovitch--Federer theorem is not empty. For a segment the situation is reversed: its projection has positive length in every direction except the perpendicular one.
\item \emph{Besicovitch and Kakeya sets.} A \emph{Besicovitch set} is a compact subset of $\RR^n$ that contains a segment of length $1$ in every direction. Besicovitch showed that such sets exist in
$\RR^2$ with Lebesgue measure $0$; correspondingly, a needle of length $1$ can be turned around completely inside a region of arbitrarily small area (\emph{Kakeya's needle problem}). Every Besicovitch
set in $\RR^2$ has Hausdorff dimension $2$ (Davies), see \cite{falconer-gofs}. Whether Besicovitch sets in $\RR^n$ must have Hausdorff dimension $n$ is the \emph{Kakeya conjecture}, a central problem
linking geometric measure theory with harmonic analysis and analytic number theory.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\subsection{Densities of measures}\label{measure-theory:subsection-measure-densities}

Nothing in the definition of densities is special to the Hausdorff measure of a set: one can ask how much mass any measure puts in small balls. This point of view, in which the set $A$ is replaced
by the measure $\mathcal{H}^s$ restricted to $A$, is the one used in modern geometric measure theory, and it simplifies the statements. Throughout, $\mu$ is a Borel measure on the metric space $X$ that is
finite on bounded Borel sets.

\begin{definition}\label{measure-theory:definition-measure-density}
For $s > 0$ and $x \in X$, the \emph{upper} and \emph{lower $s$-dimensional densities} of $\mu$ at $x$ are
\[
\Theta^{*s}(\mu, x) = \limsup_{r \to 0}\frac{\mu(\overline{B}(x, r))}{\alpha(s)(2r)^s}, \qquad \Theta^s_*(\mu, x) = \liminf_{r \to 0}\frac{\mu(\overline{B}(x, r))}{\alpha(s)(2r)^s},
\]
and the \emph{density} $\Theta^s(\mu, x)$ is their common value when they agree.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}\label{measure-theory:example-measure-density}
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $A \subseteq X$ is a Borel set with $\mathcal{H}^s(A) < \infty$, then $\mu = \mathcal{H}^s\llcorner A$, $\mu(B) = \mathcal{H}^s(A \cap B)$, is such a measure (Borel sets are
$\mathcal{H}^s$-measurable by Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-metric-outer-measure}), and $\Theta^{*s}(\mu, x) = \Theta^{*s}(A, x)$: the densities of Definition~\ref{measure-theory:definition-density} are those
of this measure.
\item For $f \in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\RR^n)$ with $f \geq 0$ and $\mu = f\lambda$, $\Theta^n(\mu, x) = f(x)$ for almost every $x$, since $\alpha(n)(2r)^n = \lambda(\overline{B}(x, r))$ and the averages of $f$ over balls
converge to $f(x)$ almost everywhere (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-lebesgue-differentiation}; closed and open balls differ by a null sphere). For $s < n$, $\Theta^s(\mu, x) = 0$ wherever $f$ is
bounded near $x$.
\item For a point mass $\mu = \delta_a$, $\Theta^{*s}(\mu, a) = \infty$ for every $s > 0$, and $\Theta^s(\mu, x) = 0$ for $x \neq a$.
\item For the Cantor measure $\mu$ of Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-cantor-dimension}(3) and $s = \log 2/\log 3$, part (4) of that example gives $\mu(\overline{B}(x, r)) \leq 2(2r)^s$ for $2r < 1$, so
$\Theta^{*s}(\mu, x) \leq 2/\alpha(s)$ at every point. At $0$ the density does not exist: $\mu([0, 3^{-k}]) = 2^{-k}$, and $\mu([0, 2 \cdot 3^{-k-1}]) = 2^{-k-1}$ because $\mu$ gives no
mass to the gap $(3^{-k-1}, 2 \cdot 3^{-k-1})$ and none to points (the Cantor function is continuous), so the computation of Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-density-cantor} applies with $c = 1$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

The basic theorem compares $\mu$ with $\mathcal{H}^s$ on the set where the density of $\mu$ is bounded.

\begin{theorem}\label{measure-theory:theorem-density-comparison}
Let $\mu$ be a Borel measure on $X$, finite on bounded Borel sets, let $A \subseteq X$ be a Borel set, $s > 0$ and $0 < t < \infty$. If $\Theta^{*s}(\mu, x) \leq t$ for every $x \in A$, then
\[
\mu(A) \leq 2^st\,\mathcal{H}^s(A).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Measurability.} For fixed $r > 0$ the function $x \mapsto \mu(\overline{B}(x, r))$ is upper semicontinuous: if $x_j \to x$, then $\overline{B}(x_j, r) \subseteq \overline{B}(x, r + \eta)$ for large $j$,
so $\limsup_j\mu(\overline{B}(x_j, r)) \leq \mu(\overline{B}(x, r + \eta))$ for every $\eta > 0$, and $\mu(\overline{B}(x, r + \eta)) \to \mu(\overline{B}(x, r))$ as $\eta \to 0$ by continuity from above, since
$\overline{B}(x, r) = \bigcap_{\eta > 0}\overline{B}(x, r + \eta)$ and these balls have finite measure. So the sets $\{x : \mu(\overline{B}(x, r)) < c\}$ are open and the sets
$\{x : \mu(\overline{B}(x, r)) \leq c\}$ are Borel. The same continuity shows that $r \mapsto \mu(\overline{B}(x, r))$ is right continuous.

\emph{Covering.} Let $t^{\prime} > t$ and, for $k \geq 1$,
\[
A_k = \bigl\{x \in A : \mu(\overline{B}(x, r)) \leq t^{\prime}\alpha(s)(2r)^s \text{ for all } 0 < r < 1/k\bigr\}.
\]
By right continuity it suffices to require the inequality for rational $r$, so $A_k$ is Borel. The sets $A_k$ increase, and their union is $A$, because $\Theta^{*s}(\mu, x) \leq t < t^{\prime}$. Fix $k$,
$0 < \delta < 1/k$ and $\varepsilon > 0$, and assume $\mathcal{H}^s(A_k) < \infty$ (otherwise there is nothing to prove). Choose a cover of $A_k$ by closed sets $C_i$ of diameter $d_i \leq \delta$ with
$\sum_i\alpha(s)d_i^s \leq \mathcal{H}^s_\delta(A_k) + \varepsilon$, discard those not meeting $A_k$, and pick $x_i \in C_i \cap A_k$. If $d_i > 0$, then $C_i \subseteq \overline{B}(x_i, d_i)$ with $d_i < 1/k$, so
$\mu(C_i) \leq t^{\prime}\alpha(s)(2d_i)^s = 2^st^{\prime}\alpha(s)d_i^s$. If $d_i = 0$, then $C_i = \{x_i\}$ and $\mu(C_i) \leq \mu(\overline{B}(x_i, r)) \leq t^{\prime}\alpha(s)(2r)^s$ for all small $r$, so
$\mu(C_i) = 0$. Summing,
\[
\mu(A_k) \leq \sum_i\mu(C_i) \leq 2^st^{\prime}\bigl(\mathcal{H}^s_\delta(A_k) + \varepsilon\bigr) \leq 2^st^{\prime}\bigl(\mathcal{H}^s(A) + \varepsilon\bigr).
\]
Letting $\varepsilon \to 0$, then $k \to \infty$ (continuity from below), then $t^{\prime} \to t$ gives the claim.
\end{proof}

For $\mu = \mathcal{H}^s\llcorner A$ the argument is the proof of Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-upper-density}: on the set where $\Theta^{*s}(A, \cdot) \leq t$ with $t < 2^{-s}$ the theorem
gives $\mathcal{H}^s \leq 2^st\mathcal{H}^s$, which forces measure zero. The hypothesis $t > 0$ cannot be dropped: Lebesgue measure on $\RR$ has $\Theta^{1/2}(\lambda, x) = 0$ everywhere, while
$\lambda(\RR) > 0$ and $\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\RR) = \infty$.

\begin{corollary}\label{measure-theory:corollary-density-consequences}
Let $\mu$ be as in the theorem and $s > 0$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item (\emph{Absolute continuity}) If $\Theta^{*s}(\mu, x) < \infty$ for every $x$ in a Borel set $A$, then every Borel set $E \subseteq A$ with $\mathcal{H}^s(E) = 0$ has $\mu(E) = 0$.
\item (\emph{Mass distribution principle}) If $A$ is a Borel set and there are $c, r_0 > 0$ with $\mu(\overline{B}(x, r)) \leq cr^s$ for all $x \in A$ and $0 < r < r_0$, then
$\mathcal{H}^s(A) \geq \alpha(s)\mu(A)/c$. In particular, if $\mu(A) > 0$ then $\dim_{\mathcal{H}}A \geq s$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
(1) For integers $m, k \geq 1$ let $E_{m,k}$ be the set of $x \in E$ with $\mu(\overline{B}(x, r)) \leq m\alpha(s)(2r)^s$ for $0 < r < 1/k$, a Borel set as in the proof above, on which
$\Theta^{*s}(\mu, \cdot) \leq m$. By the theorem $\mu(E_{m,k}) \leq 2^sm\mathcal{H}^s(E_{m,k}) = 0$, and $E = \bigcup_{m,k}E_{m,k}$ because the upper density is finite on $E$.
(2) On $A$, $\Theta^{*s}(\mu, x) \leq c/(2^s\alpha(s))$, so the theorem gives $\mu(A) \leq (c/\alpha(s))\mathcal{H}^s(A)$. If $\mu(A) > 0$, then $\mathcal{H}^s(A) > 0$, so $\dim_{\mathcal{H}}A \geq s$.
\end{proof}

For the Cantor measure, (2) with $c = 2^{1+s}$ gives $\mathcal{H}^s(C) \geq \alpha(s)2^{-1-s}$, a little weaker than the bound $\alpha(s)/2$ of Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-cantor-dimension}, which
estimated $\mu$ on arbitrary small sets rather than on balls. Statement (1) says that a measure with finite upper $s$-density cannot charge $\mathcal{H}^s$-null sets; for instance a measure with finite
upper $1$-density everywhere on $\RR^2$ gives no mass to the Cantor set $C \times \{0\}$, of dimension $\log 2/\log 3 < 1$.

\begin{remark}\label{measure-theory:remark-measure-densities}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The reverse inequality.} Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-density-lower-comparison} is the reverse of the theorem above: for $\mu$ as in the theorem, a Borel set $A$ and an open
$U \supseteq A$ with $t_1 \leq \Theta^{*s}(\mu, x) \leq t_2$ on $A$, they give $t_1\mathcal{H}^s(A) \leq \mu(U)$ (as $\mu(U \cap \overline{B}(x, r)) = \mu(\overline{B}(x, r))$ for $x \in A$ and small $r$)
and $\mu(A) \leq 2^st_2\mathcal{H}^s(A)$; taking the infimum over $U$, which is $\mu(A)$ by outer regularity (Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-closed-approximation}, applied on bounded pieces),
$t_1\mathcal{H}^s(A) \leq \mu(A) \leq 2^st_2\mathcal{H}^s(A)$. The factor $2^s$ on the right is lost because the proof encloses arbitrary sets of a cover in balls; the left inequality is proved with
the $5r$-covering lemma, which works with balls from the start. For $\mu = \mathcal{H}^s\llcorner A$ the two inequalities are the two halves of the upper density theorem
(Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-density-bounds}).
\item \emph{Differentiation of one measure by another.} For two Radon measures $\mu$, $\nu$ on $\RR^n$, the limit $\lim_{r \to 0}\nu(\overline{B}(x, r))/\mu(\overline{B}(x, r))$ exists for $\mu$-almost every $x$
and is the Radon--Nikodym derivative of the absolutely continuous part of $\nu$ with respect to $\mu$ \cite[Chapter 1]{evans-gariepy}. For $\mu = \lambda$ this is
Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-differentiation-measures}. For a general $\mu$ the Vitali covering lemma is useless, since $\mu(B(x, 3r))$ need not be comparable to $\mu(B(x, r))$, and it is
replaced by the \emph{Besicovitch covering theorem}, which uses the geometry of $\RR^n$: if every point of a bounded set $A$ is the centre of some ball of a family of closed balls with bounded radii,
one can extract a subfamily that still covers $A$ and in which no point lies in more than $N(n)$ balls, a number depending only on $n$. In a general metric space the Vitali argument survives
when $\mu$ is doubling, $\mu(\overline{B}(x, 2r)) \leq C\mu(\overline{B}(x, r))$; see Subsection~\ref{measure-theory:subsection-doubling}.
\item \emph{Rectifiable measures.} The rectifiability criteria have their natural form for measures. A Radon measure $\mu$ on $\RR^n$ is \emph{$k$-rectifiable} if $\mu = \theta\mathcal{H}^k\llcorner E$
with $E$ countably $k$-rectifiable and $\theta$ a positive Borel function. \emph{Preiss's theorem} says that $\mu$ is $k$-rectifiable if and only if $0 < \Theta^k(\mu, x) < \infty$ for $\mu$-almost every
$x$ \cite{preiss-1987}, and \emph{Marstrand's theorem} says that if $0 < \Theta^s(\mu, x) < \infty$ on a set of positive $\mu$-measure, then $s$ is an integer \cite{mattila-1995}. Preiss's proof
studies the \emph{tangent measures} of $\mu$ at $x$, the weak limits (Section~\ref{measure-theory:section-weak-convergence}) of the rescaled measures $A \mapsto \mu(x + rA)/r^k$ as $r \to 0$; existence
of the density forces them to be flat.
\item \emph{Frostman's lemma.} The mass distribution principle has a converse: if $A \subseteq \RR^n$ is a Borel set with $\mathcal{H}^s(A) > 0$, there is a nonzero finite Borel measure $\mu$ supported on a
compact subset of $A$ with $\mu(\overline{B}(x, r)) \leq r^s$ for all $x$ and $r$ \cite{mattila-1995}. So $\dim_{\mathcal{H}}A$ is the supremum of the $s$ for which $A$ carries a nonzero measure with
$\mu(\overline{B}(x, r)) \leq r^s$: Hausdorff dimension can be computed with measures instead of covers.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\subsection{Differentiation with respect to doubling measures}\label{measure-theory:subsection-doubling}

Densities measure a measure $\mu$ against the function $r \mapsto \alpha(s)(2r)^s$. Measuring it against another measure $\nu$ instead, through the ratios $\mu(\overline{B}(x, r))/\nu(\overline{B}(x, r))$,
leads to the general form of the Radon--Nikodym theorem in which the derivative $d\mu/d\nu$ is computed as a limit, as in Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-differentiation-measures} for
$\nu = \lambda$. The proof there used $\lambda(3B) = 3^n\lambda(B)$, and the $5r$-covering lemma (Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-five-r-covering}) shows that a weak form of this property is all one
needs, in any metric space. Throughout, $X$ is a nonempty metric space, and for a Borel measure $\nu$ on $X$ and any $A \subseteq X$ we write $\nu^*(A) = \inf\{\nu(E) : E \supseteq A \text{ Borel}\}$.
This outer measure is countably subadditive (take a union of Borel supersets), and a set with $\nu^*(A) = 0$ lies in a Borel set of measure $0$.

\begin{definition}\label{measure-theory:definition-doubling}
A Borel measure $\nu$ on $X$ is \emph{doubling} if $0 < \nu(\overline{B}(x, r)) < \infty$ for all $x \in X$ and $r > 0$, and there is a constant $C$ with
\[
\nu(\overline{B}(x, 2r)) \leq C\,\nu(\overline{B}(x, r)) \qquad \text{for all } x \in X,\ r > 0 .
\]
\end{definition}

Iterating, $\nu(\overline{B}(x, 2^kr)) \leq C^k\nu(\overline{B}(x, r))$; in particular, for the enlarged ball $\widehat{B} = \overline{B}(x, 5r)$ of $B = \overline{B}(x, r)$,
\begin{equation}\label{measure-theory:equation-doubling-five}
\nu(\widehat{B}) \leq \nu(\overline{B}(x, 8r)) \leq C^3\nu(B) .
\end{equation}
A doubling measure is finite on bounded sets, since every bounded set lies in a closed ball, and hence $\sigma$-finite: $X = \bigcup_k\overline{B}(x_0, k)$ for any $x_0 \in X$.

\begin{example}\label{measure-theory:example-doubling}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Lebesgue measure on $\RR^n$ is doubling with $C = 2^n$, since $\lambda(\overline{B}(x, r)) = \alpha(n)(2r)^n$.
\item If $w$ is a Borel function on $\RR^n$ with $0 < m \leq w \leq M$, then $\nu = w\lambda$ is doubling with $C = 2^nM/m$:
$\nu(\overline{B}(x, 2r)) \leq M\lambda(\overline{B}(x, 2r)) = 2^nM\lambda(\overline{B}(x, r)) \leq 2^n(M/m)\nu(\overline{B}(x, r))$.
\item The Gaussian measure $\gamma = e^{-y^2/2}\lambda$ on $\RR$ is not doubling. For $x > 2$ the density $e^{-y^2/2}$ is decreasing on $[x - 2, x + 2]$, so
\[
\frac{\gamma([x - 2, x + 2])}{\gamma([x - 1, x + 1])} \geq \frac{\frac12e^{-(x - 3/2)^2/2}}{2e^{-(x - 1)^2/2}} = \frac14e^{(x - 5/4)/2},
\]
estimating the numerator by the integral over $[x - 2, x - \frac32]$; the right side is unbounded as $x \to \infty$. Far out, the measure of a ball is concentrated near its left end.
\item A point mass $\delta_a$ on $\RR$ is not doubling, since balls missing $a$ have measure $0$; but $\delta_a$ is doubling on the one-point metric space $\{a\}$. Doubling is a property of the measure
together with the metric space it lives on.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

We need one approximation lemma, which replaces the role of $C_c(\RR^n)$ in the Lebesgue differentiation theorem.

\begin{lemma}\label{measure-theory:lemma-lipschitz-dense}
Let $\nu$ be a finite Borel measure on a metric space $X$ and $1 \leq p < \infty$. The bounded Lipschitz functions are dense in $L^p(\nu)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-measurable-functions}(4) and dominated convergence, simple functions $\sum_ia_i\mathbf{1}_{E_i}$ with $E_i$ Borel are dense, so it suffices to approximate
$\mathbf{1}_E$ for a Borel set $E$. Let $\varepsilon > 0$. By Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-closed-approximation} there is a closed set $F \subseteq E$ with $\nu(E \setminus F) < \varepsilon$, so
$\|\mathbf{1}_E - \mathbf{1}_F\|_p^p < \varepsilon$. If $F = \emptyset$, then $\|\mathbf{1}_E\|_p^p < \varepsilon$ and $0$ approximates. Otherwise let $g_k(x) = \max(0, 1 - k\,d(x, F))$. It is Lipschitz with constant $k$, takes values in $[0, 1]$,
equals $1$ on $F$, and $g_k(x) \to 0$ for $x \notin F$ because $d(x, F) > 0$ for such $x$, $F$ being closed. So $g_k \to \mathbf{1}_F$ pointwise with $|g_k - \mathbf{1}_F| \leq 1$, and as $\nu$ is finite,
dominated convergence gives $\|g_k - \mathbf{1}_F\|_p \to 0$.
\end{proof}

From now on $\nu$ is a doubling measure on $X$ with constant $C$.

\begin{theorem}[Maximal inequality for doubling measures]\label{measure-theory:theorem-doubling-maximal}
Let $f \in L^1(\nu)$ and $t > 0$, and let $A$ be the set of $x \in X$ such that
\[
\frac{1}{\nu(\overline{B}(x, r))}\int_{\overline{B}(x, r)}|f|\,d\nu > t \qquad \text{for some } 0 < r \leq 1 .
\]
Then $\nu^*(A) \leq C^3\|f\|_1/t$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $\mathcal{F}$ be the family of balls $B = \overline{B}(x, r)$ with $x \in A$, $0 < r \leq 1$ and $\int_B|f|\,d\nu > t\nu(B)$; every point of $A$ is the centre of a ball of $\mathcal{F}$. Let
$\mathcal{G}$ be the disjoint subfamily of Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-five-r-covering}. For finitely many balls of $\mathcal{G}$ the integrals $\int_B|f|\,d\nu$ add up to at most $\|f\|_1$, and each of them is
$> t\nu(B) > 0$; so for each $m$ only finitely many exceed $1/m$, and $\mathcal{G} = \{B_1, B_2, \ldots\}$ is countable. Every ball of $\mathcal{F}$ lies in some $\widehat{B_i}$, so
$A \subseteq \bigcup_i\widehat{B_i}$, and by \eqref{measure-theory:equation-doubling-five}
\[
\nu^*(A) \leq \sum_i\nu(\widehat{B_i}) \leq C^3\sum_i\nu(B_i) < \frac{C^3}{t}\sum_i\int_{B_i}|f|\,d\nu \leq \frac{C^3}{t}\|f\|_1 .
\]
(If $A$ is empty there is nothing to prove.)
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Lebesgue differentiation theorem for doubling measures]\label{measure-theory:theorem-doubling-lebesgue}
Let $f \colon X \to \RR$ be a Borel function with $\int_B|f|\,d\nu < \infty$ for every closed ball $B$. For $\nu$-almost every $x \in X$,
\[
\lim_{r \to 0}\frac{1}{\nu(\overline{B}(x, r))}\int_{\overline{B}(x, r)}|f(y) - f(x)|\,d\nu(y) = 0,
\]
and in particular the averages of $f$ over $\overline{B}(x, r)$ tend to $f(x)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $\langle g \rangle_{x, r}$ for the average of a function $g$ over $\overline{B}(x, r)$ with respect to $\nu$, and $\Omega(x) = \limsup_{r \to 0}\langle|f - f(x)|\rangle_{x, r}$. Fix $x_0 \in X$, an integer $k \geq 1$,
$t > 0$ and $\varepsilon > 0$, and let $D = \overline{B}(x_0, k + 1)$. The measure $E \mapsto \nu(E \cap D)$ is finite, so by Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-lipschitz-dense} there is a bounded continuous $g$ with
$\int_D|f - g|\,d\nu < \varepsilon$. Let $h = (f - g)\mathbf{1}_D$, so $\|h\|_1 < \varepsilon$.

Let $x \in B(x_0, k)$ and $0 < r \leq 1$. Then $\overline{B}(x, r) \subseteq D$, and for $y \in \overline{B}(x, r)$
\[
|f(y) - f(x)| \leq |h(y)| + |h(x)| + |g(y) - g(x)| .
\]
Averaging over $y$ gives $\langle|f - f(x)|\rangle_{x, r} \leq \langle|h|\rangle_{x, r} + |h(x)| + \sup_{\overline{B}(x, r)}|g - g(x)|$, and the last term tends to $0$ as $r \to 0$ because $g$ is continuous. So, if
$\Omega(x) > 2t$, then either $\langle|h|\rangle_{x, r} > t$ for some $r \leq 1$, or $|h(x)| > t$. By Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-doubling-maximal} and Markov's inequality
(Corollary~\ref{measure-theory:corollary-integral-properties}(4)),
\[
\nu^*\bigl(\{x \in B(x_0, k) : \Omega(x) > 2t\}\bigr) \leq \frac{C^3\varepsilon}{t} + \frac{\varepsilon}{t} .
\]
As $\varepsilon$ is arbitrary, this set has outer measure $0$. The set where $\Omega > 0$ is the union of these sets over $k \geq 1$ and $t = 1/j$, $j \geq 1$, so it has outer measure $0$ and lies in a
$\nu$-null Borel set. Finally $|\langle f \rangle_{x, r} - f(x)| \leq \langle|f - f(x)|\rangle_{x, r}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Differentiation of measures with respect to a doubling measure]\label{measure-theory:theorem-doubling-differentiation}
Let $\mu$ be a Borel measure on $X$ that is finite on bounded Borel sets, and let $\mu = h\nu + \mu_s$ be its Lebesgue decomposition with respect to $\nu$
(Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-lebesgue-decomposition}), with $h \geq 0$ Borel and $\mu_s \perp \nu$. Then for $\nu$-almost every $x$
\[
\lim_{r \to 0}\frac{\mu(\overline{B}(x, r))}{\nu(\overline{B}(x, r))} = h(x) .
\]
In particular, if $\mu \ll \nu$, the Radon--Nikodym derivative is $\frac{d\mu}{d\nu}(x) = \lim_{r \to 0}\mu(\overline{B}(x, r))/\nu(\overline{B}(x, r))$ for $\nu$-almost every $x$; and $\mu \perp \nu$ if and only
if this limit is $0$ $\nu$-almost everywhere.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Both measures are $\sigma$-finite, $X$ being the union of the balls $\overline{B}(x_0, k)$, so the decomposition exists. For every closed ball $B$, $\int_Bh\,d\nu \leq \mu(B) < \infty$, so by
Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-doubling-lebesgue} the averages of $h$ over $\overline{B}(x, r)$ tend to $h(x)$ for almost every $x$. Since
\[
\frac{\mu(\overline{B}(x, r))}{\nu(\overline{B}(x, r))} = \frac{1}{\nu(\overline{B}(x, r))}\int_{\overline{B}(x, r)}h\,d\nu + \frac{\mu_s(\overline{B}(x, r))}{\nu(\overline{B}(x, r))},
\]
it remains to show that the last ratio tends to $0$ for almost every $x$.

Let $S$ be a Borel set with $\nu(S) = 0$ and $\mu_s(X \setminus S) = 0$. Fix $x_0 \in X$, $k \geq 1$, $t > 0$ and $\varepsilon > 0$, and let $A$ be the set of $x \in B(x_0, k) \setminus S$ with
$\limsup_{r \to 0}\mu_s(\overline{B}(x, r))/\nu(\overline{B}(x, r)) > t$. The measure $E \mapsto \mu_s(E \cap B(x_0, k + 1))$ is finite and vanishes on the Borel set $B(x_0, k) \setminus S$, so by
Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-closed-approximation} there is an open $U^{\prime} \supseteq B(x_0, k) \setminus S$ with $\mu_s(U^{\prime} \cap B(x_0, k + 1)) < \varepsilon$. The set $U = U^{\prime} \cap B(x_0, k + 1)$ is
open, contains $A$, and has $\mu_s(U) < \varepsilon$.

Let $\mathcal{F}$ be the family of balls $B = \overline{B}(x, r)$ with $x \in A$, $r \leq 1$, $B \subseteq U$ and $\mu_s(B) > t\nu(B)$. Each $x \in A$ is the centre of such balls, because
$\overline{B}(x, r) \subseteq U$ for small $r$. Let $\mathcal{G}$ be the disjoint subfamily of Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-five-r-covering}. Its balls lie in the bounded set $U$, which has finite
$\nu$-measure, and have positive $\nu$-measure, so $\mathcal{G} = \{B_1, B_2, \ldots\}$ is countable, as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-doubling-maximal}. As there,
$A \subseteq \bigcup_i\widehat{B_i}$ and
\[
\nu^*(A) \leq C^3\sum_i\nu(B_i) < \frac{C^3}{t}\sum_i\mu_s(B_i) \leq \frac{C^3}{t}\mu_s(U) < \frac{C^3\varepsilon}{t} .
\]
So $\nu^*(A) = 0$. Taking the union over $k$ and $t = 1/j$, and adding the null set $S$, the ratio $\mu_s(\overline{B}(x, r))/\nu(\overline{B}(x, r))$ tends to $0$ outside a $\nu$-null set.

If $\mu \ll \nu$, then $\mu_s = 0$ by uniqueness of the decomposition, and $h = d\mu/d\nu$ is the Radon--Nikodym derivative (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-radon-nikodym}). If $\mu \perp \nu$, then
$h\nu = 0$ by uniqueness, so $h = 0$ almost everywhere; conversely, if the limit is $0$ almost everywhere, then $h = 0$ almost everywhere and $\mu = \mu_s$.
\end{proof}

So the Radon--Nikodym derivative, obtained abstractly in Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-radon-nikodym}, is a limit of ratios of measures of balls whenever the reference measure is doubling. For
$f \in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ and $\mu = f\nu$ the theorem is the Lebesgue differentiation theorem again; for $\mu = \nu\llcorner E$, $E$ Borel, it is a density theorem:
$\nu(E \cap \overline{B}(x, r))/\nu(\overline{B}(x, r))$ tends to $1$ at almost every point of $E$ and to $0$ at almost every point outside $E$.

\begin{remark}\label{measure-theory:remark-doubling}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Densities as derivatives.} The density $\Theta^s(\mu, x)$ is the derivative of $\mu$ with respect to the function $r \mapsto \alpha(s)(2r)^s$ in place of a measure $\nu$. Since this function
is not the measure of the balls of a fixed measure, there is no Lebesgue decomposition to recover, and one only gets the inequalities of Theorems~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-density-lower-comparison}
and~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-density-comparison}. The proofs are parallel: the singular part was handled above exactly as in Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-density-lower-comparison}, with
$\nu(B)$ in place of $\alpha(s)(2r)^s$ for $B = \overline{B}(x, r)$, and with \eqref{measure-theory:equation-doubling-five} in place of $\alpha(s)(10r)^s = 5^s\alpha(s)(2r)^s$.
\item \emph{Beyond doubling.} Doubling is sufficient, not necessary. On $\RR^n$ every Radon measure can serve as $\nu$, by the Besicovitch covering theorem
(Remark~\ref{measure-theory:remark-besicovitch} and Remark~\ref{measure-theory:remark-measure-densities}(2)); so the non-doubling Gaussian measure of Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-doubling}(3)
still differentiates. In general metric spaces some covering property is needed; spaces with a doubling measure, the \emph{spaces of homogeneous type}, are the standard setting for analysis on metric
spaces \cite[Chapter 1]{heinonen-2001}.
\item \emph{Ahlfors regularity.} A measure $\nu$ with $c^{-1}r^s \leq \nu(\overline{B}(x, r)) \leq cr^s$ for all $x \in X$ and $0 < r \leq \operatorname{diam}X$ (so $c \geq 1$) is doubling with
$C = 2^sc^2$: if $2r \leq \operatorname{diam}X$, then $\nu(\overline{B}(x, 2r)) \leq c2^sr^s \leq 2^sc^2\nu(\overline{B}(x, r))$; if $r \leq \operatorname{diam}X < 2r$, then
$\nu(\overline{B}(x, 2r)) = \nu(\overline{B}(x, \operatorname{diam}X)) \leq c(2r)^s \leq 2^sc^2\nu(\overline{B}(x, r))$; and if $r > \operatorname{diam}X$, both balls are $X$. For such a measure, $\nu$ and
$\mathcal{H}^s$ are comparable on Borel sets $A$: $\nu(A) \leq (c/\alpha(s))\mathcal{H}^s(A)$ by Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-density-comparison}, and
$\mathcal{H}^s(A) \leq 2^sc\,\alpha(s)\nu(U)$ for every open $U \supseteq A$ by Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-density-lower-comparison} with $A_1 = A$, $A_2 = U$, which gives
$\mathcal{H}^s(A) \leq 2^sc\,\alpha(s)\nu(A)$ by outer regularity (Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-closed-approximation}, applied on bounded pieces). The Cantor measure on the Cantor set $C$ is of this
kind with $s = \log 2/\log 3$: Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-measure-density}(4) gives the upper bound, and for $x \in C$ and $3^{-k} \leq r < 3^{1-k}$ the ball $\overline{B}(x, r)$ contains the
interval of the $k$-th stage of the construction that contains $x$, of measure $2^{-k} = (3^{-k})^s \geq 3^{-s}r^s$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-doubling-totally-bounded}
Let $\nu$ be a doubling measure on $X$ with constant $C$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Let $0 < \varepsilon \leq R$ and let $m \geq 0$ be an integer with $2^m\varepsilon \geq 2R$. Show that a set of points of $\overline{B}(x, R)$ with pairwise distances $> 2\varepsilon$ has at most $C^{m+1}$
elements.
\item Deduce that closed balls in $X$ are totally bounded, that $X$ is separable, and that if $X$ is complete, closed balls are compact.
\item Deduce that the Hilbert space $\ell^2$ of square-summable real sequences carries no doubling measure.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
(1) Let $y_1, \ldots, y_N \in \overline{B}(x, R)$ with $d(y_i, y_j) > 2\varepsilon$ for $i \neq j$. The balls $\overline{B}(y_i, \varepsilon)$ are pairwise disjoint and lie in $\overline{B}(x, R + \varepsilon) \subseteq \overline{B}(x, 2R)$.
Moreover $\overline{B}(x, R) \subseteq \overline{B}(y_i, 2R)$, so
$\nu(\overline{B}(x, R)) \leq \nu(\overline{B}(y_i, 2R)) \leq C^m\nu(\overline{B}(y_i, 2^{-m}2R)) \leq C^m\nu(\overline{B}(y_i, \varepsilon))$. Summing over $i$,
\[
NC^{-m}\nu(\overline{B}(x, R)) \leq \sum_i\nu(\overline{B}(y_i, \varepsilon)) \leq \nu(\overline{B}(x, 2R)) \leq C\nu(\overline{B}(x, R)),
\]
and dividing by $\nu(\overline{B}(x, R)) > 0$ gives $N \leq C^{m+1}$.

(2) Given $\varepsilon \leq R$, choose a set $\{y_1, \ldots, y_N\} \subseteq \overline{B}(x, R)$ with pairwise distances $> 2\varepsilon$ and with $N$ as large as possible, which is possible by (1). Every
$y \in \overline{B}(x, R)$ has $d(y, y_i) \leq 2\varepsilon$ for some $i$, since otherwise $y$ could be added. So finitely many open balls of radius $3\varepsilon$ cover $\overline{B}(x, R)$; for
$\varepsilon > R$ the single ball $B(x, 3\varepsilon)$ does. Hence $\overline{B}(x, R)$ is totally bounded. A totally bounded metric space is separable (the centres of finite $1/j$-nets, $j \geq 1$, form a
dense countable set), and $X = \bigcup_k\overline{B}(x_0, k)$ is a countable union of such spaces, so $X$ is separable. A closed subset of a complete space is complete, so if $X$ is complete, closed balls
are compact by Theorem~\ref{general-topology:theorem-compact-metric}.

(3) The unit vectors $e_1, e_2, \ldots$ lie in the closed unit ball of $\ell^2$ and have pairwise distances $\|e_i - e_j\| = \sqrt2$. So the closed unit ball is not totally bounded: no finitely many balls of
radius $\frac12$ cover it, since each such ball contains at most one $e_i$. By (2), there is no doubling measure on $\ell^2$. Doubling measures live on spaces that are ``finite-dimensional'' in a metric sense.
\end{solution}

\section{Exercises}\label{measure-theory:section-exercises}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-cantor}
Show that the Cantor set, obtained from $[0, 1]$ by repeatedly removing open middle thirds, is compact, uncountable and of Lebesgue measure zero.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
It is an intersection of closed subsets of $[0, 1]$, hence compact. After $k$ steps it is covered by $2^k$ intervals of length $3^{-k}$, so its measure is at most $(2/3)^k$. Its points are the numbers
$\sum_k a_k3^{-k}$ with $a_k \in \{0, 2\}$, and distinct sequences give distinct numbers, so it is in bijection with $\{0, 1\}^{\NN}$, which is uncountable (Theorem~\ref{sets:theorem-cantor}).
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-borel-cantelli}
(Borel--Cantelli.) If $\sum_k\mu(A_k) < \infty$, show that almost every point lies in only finitely many $A_k$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
By monotone convergence $\int\sum_k\mathbf{1}_{A_k} = \sum_k\mu(A_k) < \infty$, so $\sum_k\mathbf{1}_{A_k} < \infty$ a.e.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-no-half-density}
Show that there is no Lebesgue measurable $A \subseteq \RR$ with $\lambda(A \cap I) = \frac12\lambda(I)$ for every bounded interval $I$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
For such $A$, $\lambda(A \cap B(x, r))/\lambda(B(x, r)) = \frac12$ for all $x$ and $r$. By Corollary~\ref{measure-theory:corollary-density} this ratio tends to $1$ at
almost every point of $A$ and to $0$ at almost every point outside $A$; so $A$ and $\RR \setminus A$ are both null, which is impossible.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-maximal-not-integrable}
Show that $Mf \notin L^1(\RR^n)$ for every $f \in L^1(\RR^n)$ that is not zero almost everywhere; so the maximal inequality cannot be improved to
$\|Mf\|_1 \leq C\|f\|_1$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
Choose $R$ with $a = \int_{B(0, R)}|f| > 0$. For $\|x\| \geq R$, the ball $B(x, 2\|x\|)$ contains $B(0, R)$, so
$Mf(x) \geq a/\lambda(B(x, 2\|x\|)) = a/(2^n\|x\|^n\lambda(B(0, 1)))$. The function $\|x\|^{-n}$ is not integrable on $\{\|x\| \geq R\}$: the shells
$2^kR \leq \|x\| < 2^{k+1}R$ have measure $(2^n - 1)2^{kn}R^n\lambda(B(0, 1))$, and on each of them $\|x\|^{-n} \geq 2^{-(k+1)n}R^{-n}$, so each contributes at least the
positive constant $(1 - 2^{-n})\lambda(B(0, 1))$ to the integral.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-lp-inclusions}
Show that $L^q(\mu) \subseteq L^p(\mu)$ for $p \leq q$ if $\mu(X) < \infty$, and that there is no inclusion in either direction for Lebesgue measure on $\RR$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
By H\"older with exponents $q/p$ and its conjugate, $\int|f|^p \leq (\int|f|^q)^{p/q}\mu(X)^{1 - p/q}$. On $\RR$, $|x|^{-1/2}\mathbf{1}_{[-1,1]}$ is in $L^1$ but not $L^2$, and $(1 + |x|)^{-1}$ is in $L^2$ but not $L^1$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-convolution}
For $f \in L^1(\RR^n)$ and $g \in L^p(\RR^n)$, show using Fubini's theorem that $(f * g)(x) = \int f(x - y)g(y)\,dy$ is defined a.e. and $\|f * g\|_p \leq \|f\|_1\|g\|_p$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-ball-volume}
Show that the volume of the unit ball in $\RR^n$ is $\omega_n = \pi^{n/2}/\Gamma(\frac n2 + 1)$, where $\Gamma(s) = \int_0^\infty t^{s-1}e^{-t}\,dt$, by computing $\int_{\RR^n}e^{-\|x\|^2}$ in two ways.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-middle-half-cantor}
Let $K \subseteq [0, 1]$ be the set obtained by removing the open middle half $(\frac14, \frac34)$ of $[0, 1]$, then the open middle halves of the two remaining intervals, and so on; equivalently, the
numbers $\sum_{k \geq 1}a_k4^{-k}$ with all $a_k \in \{0, 3\}$. Show that $\dim_{\mathcal{H}}K = \frac12$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
Follow Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-cantor-dimension} with $3$ replaced by $4$ and the digits $\{0, 2\}$ by $\{0, 3\}$; now $s = \log 2/\log 4 = \frac12$, so $4^s = 2$. The $2^k$ intervals of
generation $k$ have length $4^{-k}$, which gives $\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(K) \leq \alpha(\frac12)2^k4^{-k/2} = \alpha(\frac12)$. Two of them first differing at position $j \leq k$ have left ends differing by at least
$3 \cdot 4^{-j} - \sum_{j < i \leq k}3 \cdot 4^{-i} \geq 2 \cdot 4^{-j}$, so they are at distance at least $4^{-k}$. The function $F(\sum_ka_k4^{-k}) = \sum_k(a_k/3)2^{-k}$ on $K$, extended to
$[0, 1]$ as in Counterexample~\ref{measure-theory:counterexample-cantor-function}, is continuous and nondecreasing, and its measure $\mu_F$ (Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-stieltjes}) gives mass
$2^{-k}$ to each interval of generation $k$ and mass $0$ to $[0, 1] \setminus K$. A closed set of diameter $d$ with $4^{-k-1} \leq d < 4^{-k}$ meets at most one interval of generation $k$, so its
$\mu_F$-measure is at most $2^{-k} = 2 \cdot 4^{-(k+1)/2} \leq 2d^{1/2}$, and as in the example $\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(K) \geq \alpha(\frac12)/2 > 0$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-lipschitz-graph}
Let $h \colon \RR^n \to \RR$ be Lipschitz with constant $L$ and $U \subseteq \RR^n$ a bounded open set. Show that the graph $\Gamma = \{(x, h(x)) : x \in U\}$ satisfies
$\lambda^n(U) \leq \mathcal{H}^n(\Gamma) \leq (1 + L^2)^{n/2}\lambda^n(U)$, so that $\dim_{\mathcal{H}}\Gamma = n$, even though $h$ need not be differentiable everywhere.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
The map $g(x) = (x, h(x))$ satisfies $|g(x) - g(y)|^2 = |x - y|^2 + |h(x) - h(y)|^2 \leq (1 + L^2)|x - y|^2$, so it is $\sqrt{1 + L^2}$-Lipschitz, and its inverse, the projection
$\Gamma \to U$, is $1$-Lipschitz. By Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-hausdorff}(3) and Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-hausdorff-lebesgue},
$\lambda^n(U) = \mathcal{H}^n(U) \leq \mathcal{H}^n(\Gamma) \leq (1 + L^2)^{n/2}\mathcal{H}^n(U)$. Both bounds are positive and finite, so $\mathcal{H}^s(\Gamma)$ is $\infty$ for $s < n$ and $0$ for
$s > n$ by Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-hausdorff}(4). (By the general area formula of Remark~\ref{measure-theory:remark-area-coarea} and Rademacher's theorem,
$\mathcal{H}^n(\Gamma) = \int_U\sqrt{1 + |\nabla h|^2}$.)
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-torus-area}
For $R > r > 0$ let $x(u, v) = ((R + r\cos u)\cos v, (R + r\cos u)\sin v, r\sin u)$. Show that $x$ is injective with injective differential on $(0, 2\pi)^2$, that $Jx = r(R + r\cos u)$, and that
the torus $T = x(\RR^2)$ has $\mathcal{H}^2(T) = 4\pi^2Rr$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
$x_u = (-r\sin u\cos v, -r\sin u\sin v, r\cos u)$ and $x_v = (-(R + r\cos u)\sin v, (R + r\cos u)\cos v, 0)$ are orthogonal, of lengths $r$ and $R + r\cos u > 0$, so
$Jx = |x_u \times x_v| = r(R + r\cos u)$ and $Dx$ is injective. The point $x(u, v)$ determines $v$ modulo $2\pi$ (its projection to the plane $z = 0$ has argument $v$) and then $u$ modulo
$2\pi$, so $x$ is injective on $(0, 2\pi)^2$. Its image is $T$ minus the two circles $u = 0$ and $v = 0$, which have finite $\mathcal{H}^1$-measure and are therefore $\mathcal{H}^2$-null. By the area
formula, $\mathcal{H}^2(T) = \int_0^{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}r(R + r\cos u)\,du\,dv = 4\pi^2Rr$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-ae-measurable}
Let $(X, \mathcal{A}, \mu)$ be a complete measure space, $f$ measurable and $g = f$ almost everywhere. Show that $g$ is measurable. Deduce that for Lebesgue measure, changing a measurable function on a null set
gives a measurable function, so that ``measurable function defined almost everywhere'' makes sense.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
Let $N \in \mathcal{A}$ be a null set with $f = g$ outside $N$. For $a \in \RR$, $\{g > a\} = (\{f > a\} \setminus N) \cup (\{g > a\} \cap N)$. The first set is measurable, and the second is a subset of
the null set $N$, hence measurable by completeness. So $g$ is measurable (Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-measurable-functions}(1)). Lebesgue measure is complete
(Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-lebesgue-measure}).
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-inner-measure}
For $A \subseteq [0, 1]$ define the \emph{inner measure} $\lambda_*(A) = \sup\{\lambda(K) : K \subseteq A \text{ compact}\}$, approximating $A$ from inside instead of from outside. Show that
$\lambda_*(A) = 1 - \lambda^*([0, 1] \setminus A)$, that $\lambda_*(A) \leq \lambda^*(A)$, and that $A$ is Lebesgue measurable if and only if $\lambda_*(A) = \lambda^*(A)$. (This was Lebesgue's original
definition of measurability.)
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
Compact subsets $K \subseteq A$ correspond to relatively open sets $V = [0, 1] \setminus K \supseteq [0, 1] \setminus A$, with $\lambda(K) = 1 - \lambda(V)$; and $\lambda^*(B)$ is the infimum of $\lambda(U \cap [0, 1])$
over open $U \supseteq B$ for $B \subseteq [0, 1]$ (enlarge the covering boxes, as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-lebesgue-measure}). This gives the formula. If $K \subseteq A \subseteq U$
then $\lambda(K) \leq \lambda^*(A) \leq \lambda(U)$, so $\lambda_* \leq \lambda^*$. If $A$ is measurable, equality is inner regularity. Conversely, if $\lambda_*(A) = \lambda^*(A)$, choose compact $K_n \subseteq A$
and open $U_n \supseteq A$ with $\lambda(K_n) \to \lambda_*(A)$ and $\lambda(U_n) \to \lambda^*(A)$; then $F = \bigcup K_n \subseteq A \subseteq G = \bigcap U_n$ are Borel sets with
$\lambda(G \setminus F) = \lambda(G) - \lambda(F) = 0$, so $A = F \cup (A \setminus F)$ with $A \setminus F \subseteq G \setminus F$ null, and $A$ is measurable by completeness.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-riesz-circle}
Let $\sigma$ be the measure on $\RR^2$ of Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-positive-functionals}(3), and $\varphi(t) = (\cos t, \sin t)$. Show that $\sigma(E) = \lambda(\{t \in [0, 2\pi] : \varphi(t) \in E\})$
for every Borel set $E$, and deduce that an arc $\{\varphi(t) : \alpha < t < \beta\}$ with $0 \leq \alpha < \beta \leq 2\pi$ has measure $\beta - \alpha$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
The right side defines a finite Borel measure $\nu$, the push-forward of $\lambda$ restricted to $[0, 2\pi]$, since preimages under the continuous $\varphi$ of Borel sets are Borel. For $f = \mathbf{1}_E$,
$\int f\,d\nu = \int_{[0, 2\pi]}f \circ \varphi\,d\lambda$ by definition; by linearity this holds for simple functions, by monotone convergence for measurable $f \geq 0$, and then for $f \in C_c(\RR^2)$. By
Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-riemann-lebesgue}, $\int_{[0, 2\pi]}f \circ \varphi\,d\lambda = \Lambda f$. So $\nu$ represents $\Lambda$, and $\nu = \sigma$ by the uniqueness in
Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-riesz-markov}. Since $\varphi$ is injective on $[0, 2\pi)$ and $\varphi(2\pi) = \varphi(0)$, the set of $t \in [0, 2\pi]$ with $\varphi(t)$ in the arc is $(\alpha, \beta)$, of
measure $\beta - \alpha$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-weak-mass}
Let $\mu_k \rightharpoonup \mu$ be Radon measures on $X$ (locally compact Hausdorff with a countable basis).
\begin{enumerate}
\item Show that $\mu(X) \leq \liminf_k\mu_k(X)$, and that $\mu_k(X) \to \mu(X)$ if $X$ is compact.
\item Suppose that the $\mu_k$ and $\mu$ are probability measures. Show that $\int f\,d\mu_k \to \int f\,d\mu$ for every bounded continuous $f \colon X \to \RR$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
(1) The first statement is Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-weak-convergence-sets}(1) with $U = X$; if $X$ is compact, $1 \in C_c(X)$. (2) Let $\varepsilon > 0$. By inner regularity choose a
compact $K$ with $\mu(K) > 1 - \varepsilon$, and $\phi \in C_c(X)$ with $0 \leq \phi \leq 1$ and $\phi = 1$ on $K$. Then $\int\phi\,d\mu_k \to \int\phi\,d\mu > 1 - \varepsilon$, so
$\int(1 - \phi)\,d\mu_k < 2\varepsilon$ for $k$ large, while $\int(1 - \phi)\,d\mu < \varepsilon$. As $f\phi \in C_c(X)$,
\[
\Bigl|\int f\,d\mu_k - \int f\,d\mu\Bigr| \leq \Bigl|\int f\phi\,d\mu_k - \int f\phi\,d\mu\Bigr| + \|f\|_\infty\Bigl(\int(1 - \phi)\,d\mu_k + \int(1 - \phi)\,d\mu\Bigr),
\]
whose $\limsup$ is at most $3\varepsilon\|f\|_\infty$. So when no mass is lost, testing against $C_c(X)$ and against bounded continuous functions give the same notion of convergence.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-staircase}
\emph{Length is not continuous.} For $k \geq 1$ let $\gamma_k(x) = (x, \frac1k\sin kx)$ for $x \in [0, 2\pi]$, and let $\mu_k$ be the length measure of $\gamma_k$, the Radon measure on $\RR^2$ with
\[
\int f\,d\mu_k = \int_0^{2\pi}f(\gamma_k(x))\,|\gamma_k^{\prime}(x)|\,dx = \int_0^{2\pi}f\bigl(x, \tfrac1k\sin kx\bigr)\sqrt{1 + \cos^2kx}\,dx .
\]
Show that $\gamma_k \to \gamma_0$, $\gamma_0(x) = (x, 0)$, uniformly, that $\mu_k \rightharpoonup c\mu_0$ with $c = \frac1{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}\sqrt{1 + \cos^2t}\,dt$, where $\mu_0$ is the length measure of the
segment, and that $c > 1$. So the curves $\gamma_k$ have length $2\pi c$, which does not converge to the length $2\pi$ of the limit curve.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
$|\gamma_k(x) - \gamma_0(x)| \leq 1/k$. Let $f \in C_c(\RR^2)$, with modulus of uniform continuity $\omega$. Then $|f(\gamma_k(x)) - f(x, 0)| \leq \omega(1/k)$, so $\int f\,d\mu_k$ differs from
$\int_0^{2\pi}f(x, 0)g(kx)\,dx$, where $g(t) = \sqrt{1 + \cos^2t}$, by at most $2\pi\sqrt2\,\omega(1/k)$. The argument of Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-weak-convergence}(5), applied on $[0, 2\pi]$ to the
continuous function $x \mapsto f(x, 0)$ (only its continuity on $[0, 2\pi]$ was used), shows that $\int_0^{2\pi}f(x, 0)g(kx)\,dx \to \bar g\int_0^{2\pi}f(x, 0)\,dx = c\int f\,d\mu_0$. Since $g \geq 1$, with
equality only where $\cos t = 0$, and $g$ is continuous, $c > 1$. The length of $\gamma_k$ is $\int_0^{2\pi}g(kx)\,dx = \frac1k\int_0^{2\pi k}g = 2\pi c$, as the interval consists of $k$ periods. In
general length is only \emph{lower semicontinuous} under uniform convergence of curves: the limit can be shorter, never longer.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-ball-semicontinuous}
Let $\mu$ be a Radon measure on $\RR^n$ and $r > 0$. Show that $x \mapsto \mu(B(x, r))$ is lower semicontinuous and $x \mapsto \mu(\overline{B}(x, r))$ is upper semicontinuous, and that for fixed $x$ the
sphere $\{y : \|y - x\| = r\}$ has $\mu$-measure $0$ for all but countably many $r$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
Let $x_k \to x$. If $\|y - x\| < r$, then $\|y - x_k\| < r$ for $k$ large, so $\mathbf{1}_{B(x, r)} \leq \liminf_k\mathbf{1}_{B(x_k, r)}$, and Fatou's lemma
(Corollary~\ref{measure-theory:corollary-integral-properties}) gives $\mu(B(x, r)) \leq \liminf_k\mu(B(x_k, r))$. If $\|y - x_k\| \leq r$ for infinitely many $k$, then $\|y - x\| \leq r$; so
$\limsup_k\mathbf{1}_{\overline{B}(x_k, r)} \leq \mathbf{1}_{\overline{B}(x, r)}$, and Fatou's lemma applied to the non-negative functions $\mathbf{1}_{\overline{B}(x, r + 1)} - \mathbf{1}_{\overline{B}(x_k, r)}$
($k$ large), whose integrals are finite, gives $\limsup_k\mu(\overline{B}(x_k, r)) \leq \mu(\overline{B}(x, r))$. The spheres of different radii are disjoint; those of radius less than $R$ with measure at
least $1/m$ number at most $m\mu(B(x, R))$, so only countably many spheres have positive measure.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-box-cantor}
Show directly from Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-cantor-dimension}(2), without Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-moran}, that the Cantor set has box-counting dimension $\log 2/\log 3$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
Let $3^{-k-1} \leq \delta < 3^{-k}$. The $2^{k+1}$ intervals of generation $k + 1$ have length $3^{-k-1} \leq \delta$ and cover $C$, so $N_\delta(C) \leq 2^{k+1}$. A set of diameter at most $\delta < 3^{-k}$
meets at most one of the $2^k$ intervals of generation $k$, and each of them contains points of $C$, so $N_\delta(C) \geq 2^k$. Since $k\log 3 < -\log\delta \leq (k + 1)\log 3$,
\[
\frac{k\log 2}{(k + 1)\log 3} \leq \frac{\log N_\delta(C)}{-\log\delta} \leq \frac{(k + 1)\log 2}{k\log 3} \qquad (k \geq 1),
\]
and both bounds tend to $\log 2/\log 3$ as $\delta \to 0$, that is, as $k \to \infty$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-cantor-sum}
Let $C$ be the Cantor set. Show that $C + C = \{x + y : x, y \in C\}$ is the interval $[0, 2]$. (Hint: $C \times C$ is the attractor of
Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-similarity-dimension}(3); project it as in Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-four-corner-projections}.) So the set $C \times C$, of dimension $\log 4/\log 3 < 2$, has a
projection of positive length, while its projections to the axes are copies of $C$, of length $0$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
Let $\pi(x, y) = x + y$ and $f_{a, b}(x, y) = (x/3 + a, y/3 + b)$ for $a, b \in \{0, \frac23\}$. Then $\pi \circ f_{a, b} = g_{a + b} \circ \pi$ with $g_c(t) = t/3 + c$, and $a + b$ takes the values
$0, \frac23, \frac43$. Since $C \times C = \bigcup_{a, b}f_{a, b}(C \times C)$, its image $\pi(C \times C) = C + C$ is a nonempty compact set with $C + C = g_0(C + C) \cup g_{2/3}(C + C) \cup g_{4/3}(C + C)$,
that is, the attractor of $\{g_0, g_{2/3}, g_{4/3}\}$. The interval $[0, 2]$ is also invariant: $g_0([0, 2]) \cup g_{2/3}([0, 2]) \cup g_{4/3}([0, 2]) = [0, \frac23] \cup [\frac23, \frac43] \cup [\frac43, 2]$. By
the uniqueness in Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-hutchinson}, $C + C = [0, 2]$. (More generally, by Marstrand's projection theorem a Borel set $A \subseteq \RR^2$ with $\dim_{\mathcal{H}}A > 1$ has
projections of positive length in almost every direction \cite{mattila-1995}.)
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-prescribed-dimension}
Show that for every $s \in [0, 1]$ there is a compact set $K \subseteq [0, 1]$ with $\dim_{\mathcal{H}}K = s$ and $0 < \mathcal{H}^s(K) < \infty$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
For $s = 0$ take a point, for $s = 1$ the interval $[0, 1]$ (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-hausdorff-lebesgue}). For $0 < s < 1$ let $\rho = 2^{-1/s}$, so $0 < \rho < \frac12$, and consider
$f_1(x) = \rho x$, $f_2(x) = \rho x + 1 - \rho$. Since $f_1([0, 1]) \cup f_2([0, 1]) = [0, \rho] \cup [1 - \rho, 1] \subseteq [0, 1]$, the attractor $K$ lies in $[0, 1]$
(Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-hutchinson}(1)), and the pieces $f_1(K) \subseteq [0, \rho]$ and $f_2(K) \subseteq [1 - \rho, 1]$ are disjoint because $\rho < 1 - \rho$. The similarity dimension solves
$2\rho^t = 1$, so it is $s$, and Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-moran} applies.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-natural-measure}
