Let $K$ be the attractor of an iterated function system satisfying the strong separation condition, with similarity dimension $s$, and let $\mu$ be the measure constructed in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-moran}.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Show that $\mathcal{H}^s(K_w) = r_w^s\mathcal{H}^s(K)$ for every word $w$, and explain why this, together with $0 < \mathcal{H}^s(K) < \infty$, forces \eqref{measure-theory:equation-moran}.
\item Show that two Borel probability measures on $K$ which agree on all the sets $K_w$ are equal. Deduce that $\mu(B) = \mathcal{H}^s(B)/\mathcal{H}^s(K)$ for every Borel set $B \subseteq K$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
(1) $f_w$ is a similarity with ratio $r_w$, so $\mathcal{H}^s(f_w(K)) = r_w^s\mathcal{H}^s(K)$. The sets $K_1, \ldots, K_N$ are disjoint compact sets with union $K$, so
$\mathcal{H}^s(K) = \sum_i\mathcal{H}^s(K_i) = \bigl(\sum_ir_i^s\bigr)\mathcal{H}^s(K)$, and dividing by $\mathcal{H}^s(K) \in (0, \infty)$ gives $\sum_ir_i^s = 1$. This is the heuristic of the introduction
to Section~\ref{measure-theory:section-self-similar}.

(2) Let $\mathcal{P}$ consist of $\emptyset$ and the sets $K_w$, including $K = K_\varnothing$. Two of the $K_w$ are either nested (if one word begins with the other, by
Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-hutchinson}(2)) or disjoint (if the words first differ at some position, by Step 1 of the proof of Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-moran}), so $\mathcal{P}$ is
closed under finite intersections. Every relatively open $G \subseteq K$ is a countable union of sets $K_w \subseteq G$: if $x \in G$ has address $\omega$, then $x \in K_{\omega|k}$ and
$\operatorname{diam}K_{\omega|k} \leq r^k\operatorname{diam}K$, so $K_{\omega|k} \subseteq G$ for $k$ large; and there are only countably many words. So $\sigma(\mathcal{P})$ contains the open subsets of
$K$, hence all Borel subsets, and Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-dynkin} shows that two probability measures agreeing on $\mathcal{P}$ agree on all Borel sets. Finally,
$B \mapsto \mathcal{H}^s(B)/\mathcal{H}^s(K)$ is a Borel probability measure on $K$ (Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-hausdorff}(1) and Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-moran}) which by (1)
gives the value $r_w^s = \mu(K_w)$ to every $K_w$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-sierpinski}
Let $S$ be the Sierpi\'nski gasket of a triangle $T$ with perimeter $P$ (Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-ifs}(3)). Show:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\lambda^2(S) = 0$;
\item two distinct triangles $f_v(T)$, $f_w(T)$ with $|v| = |w|$ meet in at most one point;
\item $S$ contains the boundary $\partial T$, and more generally the boundaries of all triangles $f_w(T)$;
\item $\mathcal{H}^1(S) = \infty$, so that $1 \leq \dim_{\mathcal{H}}S \leq \log 3/\log 2$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
(1) $F^k(T)$ is the union of the $3^k$ triangles $f_w(T)$, $|w| = k$, which are images of $T$ under similarities of ratio $2^{-k}$, so of area $4^{-k}\lambda^2(T)$ (Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-hausdorff}(3)
and Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-hausdorff-lebesgue}). Hence $\lambda^2(S) \leq \lambda^2(F^k(T)) \leq (\frac34)^k\lambda^2(T) \to 0$.

(2) For $i \neq j$ the corner triangles $f_i(T)$ and $f_j(T)$ meet only in the midpoint of the side $p_ip_j$. If $v \neq w$ first differ at position $j$ and $u$ is their common beginning of length
$j - 1$, then $f_v(T) \subseteq f_u(f_{v_j}(T))$ and $f_w(T) \subseteq f_u(f_{w_j}(T))$, and these meet in the image under the injective map $f_u$ of a single point.

(3) The side $[p_i, p_j]$ is the union of $[p_i, m]$ and $[m, p_j]$, $m$ its midpoint, which are sides of $f_i(T)$ and $f_j(T)$. So $\partial T \subseteq F(\partial T)$, and inductively
$\partial T \subseteq F(\partial T) \subseteq F^2(\partial T) \subseteq \cdots$, since $F$ preserves inclusions. As $F^k(\partial T) \subseteq F^k(T)$, $\partial T \subseteq \bigcap_kF^k(T) = S$
(Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-hutchinson}(1)). Then $f_w(\partial T) \subseteq f_w(S) \subseteq S$.

(4) A segment of length $\ell$ is isometric to an interval, so it has $\mathcal{H}^1$-measure $\ell$ (Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-hausdorff-lebesgue}). By (3), $S$ contains the boundaries of the $3^k$
triangles of generation $k$, each the union of three segments of total length $2^{-k}P$. By (2) these $3^{k+1}$ segments meet each other only in finitely many points, which are $\mathcal{H}^1$-null; so,
$\mathcal{H}^1$ being a measure on Borel sets, $\mathcal{H}^1(S) \geq 3^k2^{-k}P = (\frac32)^kP$ for every $k$. Hence $\mathcal{H}^1(S) = \infty$ and $\dim_{\mathcal{H}}S \geq 1$; the upper bound is
Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-similarity-upper}, with $3 \cdot 2^{-s} = 1$. (By Remark~\ref{measure-theory:remark-open-set-condition}, $\dim_{\mathcal{H}}S = \log 3/\log 2$.)
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-holder-graph}
Let $h \colon [0, 1] \to \RR$ satisfy $|h(x) - h(y)| \leq c|x - y|^\alpha$ for some $c > 0$ and $0 < \alpha \leq 1$ (a \emph{H\"older continuous} function), and let
$G = \{(x, h(x)) : x \in [0, 1]\}$ be its graph. Show that $1 \leq \dim_{\mathcal{H}}G \leq \overline{\dim}_BG \leq 2 - \alpha$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
Projection to the first coordinate is $1$-Lipschitz and maps $G$ onto $[0, 1]$, so $\mathcal{H}^1(G) \geq 1$ and $\dim_{\mathcal{H}}G \geq 1$. For $m \geq 1$ divide $[0, 1]$ into $m$ intervals $I_j$ of
length $\frac1m$. On $I_j$ the values of $h$ lie in an interval of length at most $cm^{-\alpha}$, which is covered by at most $cm^{1-\alpha} + 1$ intervals of length $\frac1m$; so the part of $G$ over
$I_j$ is covered by at most $cm^{1-\alpha} + 1$ squares of side $\frac1m$, and $G$ by at most $m(cm^{1-\alpha} + 1) \leq (c + 1)m^{2-\alpha}$ of them. The squares have diameter $\sqrt2/m$. Given
$0 < \delta < \sqrt2$, choose $m$ with $\sqrt2/(m + 1) \leq \delta < \sqrt2/m$; then $N_\delta(G) \leq (c + 1)(m + 1)^{2-\alpha}$ and $-\log\delta > \log(m/\sqrt2)$, so
\[
\frac{\log N_\delta(G)}{-\log\delta} \leq \frac{\log(c + 1) + (2 - \alpha)\log(m + 1)}{\log m - \log\sqrt2} \to 2 - \alpha \qquad (\delta \to 0).
\]
The middle inequality is Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-dimension-properties}(3). For $\alpha = 1$, a Lipschitz function, the graph has dimension $1$, in accordance with
Exercise~\ref{measure-theory:exercise-lipschitz-graph}; rough functions can have graphs of dimension close to $2$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-ball-derivative}
Let $g \colon \RR^n \to \RR$ be continuous, $n \geq 2$, and $\Phi(R) = \int_{B(0, R)}g\,d\lambda^n$. Show that $\Phi$ is differentiable on $(0, \infty)$ with
$\Phi^{\prime}(R) = \int_{|x| = R}g\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}$, and that the averages $\frac1{n\omega_nR^{n-1}}\int_{|x| = R}g\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}$ tend to $g(0)$ as $R \to 0$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
Apply Corollary~\ref{measure-theory:corollary-polar} to the positive and negative parts of $g\mathbf{1}_{B(0, R)}$, which are bounded Borel functions vanishing outside a bounded set; subtracting,
$\Phi(R) = \int_0^R\eta(r)\,dr$ with $\eta(r) = r^{n-1}\int_{S^{n-1}}g(r\theta)\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\theta)$. As $r \to r_0$, $g(r\theta) \to g(r_0\theta)$ uniformly in $\theta \in S^{n-1}$, by uniform
continuity of $g$ on a compact ball, and $\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(S^{n-1}) = n\omega_n < \infty$; so $\eta$ is continuous on $[0, \infty)$, and $\Phi^{\prime}(R) = \eta(R)$ by the fundamental theorem of calculus
(Theorem~\ref{real-analysis:theorem-ftc}). By the scaling in the proof of Corollary~\ref{measure-theory:corollary-polar}, $\eta(R) = \int_{|x| = R}g\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}$. Finally
$\eta(R)/(n\omega_nR^{n-1}) - g(0) = \frac{1}{n\omega_n}\int_{S^{n-1}}(g(R\theta) - g(0))\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\theta)$, which is at most $\sup_{|x| = R}|g(x) - g(0)| \to 0$ in absolute value.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-dirichlet-approximation}
(\emph{Dirichlet's approximation theorem.}) Let $x \in \RR$ and $N \geq 1$. Using the pigeonhole principle for the $N + 1$ numbers $kx - \lfloor kx\rfloor \in [0, 1)$, $k = 0, \ldots, N$, show that there
are integers $p, q$ with $1 \leq q \leq N$ and $|qx - p| < 1/N$. Deduce that an irrational $x$ has infinitely many approximations $p/q$ with $|x - p/q| < 1/q^2$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
Divide $[0, 1)$ into the $N$ intervals $[\frac{i}{N}, \frac{i+1}{N})$. Two of the $N + 1$ numbers, for $k < l$ say, lie in the same interval, so $|(l - k)x - (\lfloor lx\rfloor - \lfloor kx\rfloor)| < 1/N$; take
$q = l - k$ and $p = \lfloor lx\rfloor - \lfloor kx\rfloor$. Then $|x - p/q| < 1/(qN) \leq 1/q^2$, and also $|x - p/q| < 1/N$. Writing $p/q$ in lowest terms $p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}$ with $q^{\prime} \leq q$ keeps
$|x - p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}| < 1/q^2 \leq 1/q^{\prime 2}$. If $x$ is irrational, no fraction equals $x$, so a finite set of fractions has positive minimal distance from $x$; since letting $N \to \infty$ produces
fractions arbitrarily close to $x$, we obtain infinitely many distinct ones.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-decimal-blocks}
For $x \in [0, 1)$ let $e_k(x) = \lfloor 10^kx\rfloor - 10\lfloor 10^{k-1}x\rfloor$ be its decimal digits. Show that for almost every $x$:
\begin{enumerate}
\item every finite block of decimal digits occurs infinitely often among the $e_k(x)$;
\item the frequency $\frac1n\#\{k \leq n : e_k(x) = 7\}$ tends to $\frac1{10}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
The analogue of Lemma~\ref{measure-theory:lemma-binary-digits} holds with the same proof: prescribing the digits at the positions of a finite set $S$ defines a set of measure $10^{-|S|}$, a union of
decimal intervals of length $10^{-K}$, $K = \max S$, and events determined by disjoint sets of positions are independent, with the product formula for integrals. (1) is then proved as in
Example~\ref{measure-theory:example-infinite-monkey}, with probability $10^{-m}$ for a block of length $m$. For (2) let $\rho_k = \mathbf{1}_{\{e_k = 7\}} - \frac1{10}$, so $\int\rho_k = 0$ and $|\rho_k| \leq 1$, and
$S_n = \rho_1 + \cdots + \rho_n$. In $\int S_n^4 = \sum\int\rho_i\rho_j\rho_k\rho_l$, a term in which some index occurs exactly once vanishes by the product formula. The remaining terms have all indices equal
($n$ terms) or two values each occurring twice ($3n(n - 1)$ terms), and each is at most $1$ in absolute value; so $\int S_n^4 \leq 3n^2$, and Step 2 of the proof of
Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-borel-normal} applies verbatim.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-total-variation-point}
Let $L(F) = F_1(0) + 2F_2(0)$ for $F = (F_1, F_2) \in C_c(\RR, \RR^2)$. Find the measure $\mu$ and the field $\nu$ of Theorem~\ref{measure-theory:theorem-riesz-vector}, and check
\eqref{measure-theory:equation-total-variation} directly.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
$L(F) = \langle F(0), (1, 2)\rangle$, so $L(F) = \int\langle F, \nu\rangle\,d\mu$ with $\mu = \sqrt5\,\delta_0$ and $\nu(0) = (1, 2)/\sqrt5$, a unit vector. For an open $V \ni 0$ and $|F| \leq 1$ with
support in $V$, the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality gives $L(F) \leq |(1, 2)| = \sqrt5$, with equality when $F(0) = (1, 2)/\sqrt5$, which is possible for $F = \psi\,(1, 2)/\sqrt5$ with $\psi \prec V$, $\psi(0) = 1$; for
$0 \notin V$ the supremum is $0$. So the total variation is $\sqrt5\,\delta_0$, not $\delta_0 + 2\delta_0 = 3\delta_0$: the components cannot be estimated separately, as $|F| \leq 1$ couples them.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-steinmetz}
Show that the intersection of the two solid cylinders $\{x^2 + y^2 \leq R^2\}$ and $\{x^2 + z^2 \leq R^2\}$ in $\RR^3$ (the \emph{Steinmetz solid}) has volume $\frac{16}{3}R^3$, using Cavalieri's principle with
slices perpendicular to the $x$-axis.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
For $|x| \leq R$ the slice is $\{(y, z) : |y| \leq \sqrt{R^2 - x^2}, |z| \leq \sqrt{R^2 - x^2}\}$, a square of side $2\sqrt{R^2 - x^2}$, and it is empty for $|x| > R$. By
Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-cavalieri} the volume is $\int_{-R}^R4(R^2 - x^2)\,dx = 4\bigl(2R^3 - \frac23R^3\bigr) = \frac{16}{3}R^3$. (Archimedes knew this too; compare with the ball inscribed in the
solid, whose slices are the inscribed discs, of area $\frac\pi4$ times that of the squares, and whose volume is $\frac\pi4 \cdot \frac{16}{3}R^3 = \frac43\pi R^3$.)
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-pappus-cone}
Use the Pappus--Guldin theorems to compute the volume and the lateral area of the cone of revolution with base radius $R$ and height $h$, obtained by rotating the triangle with vertices $(0, 0)$, $(R, 0)$,
$(0, h)$ of the $(r, z)$-plane around the $z$-axis. (The centroid of a triangle is the average of its vertices.)
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
The triangle has area $\frac12Rh$ and centroid at distance $\bar r = \frac13(0 + R + 0) = \frac R3$ from the axis, so the volume is $2\pi\frac R3 \cdot \frac12Rh = \frac13\pi R^2h$, by
Corollary~\ref{measure-theory:corollary-pappus-volume}. The lateral surface comes from the segment from $(R, 0)$ to $(0, h)$, of length $\sqrt{R^2 + h^2}$, whose centroid is its midpoint, at distance $\frac R2$; by
Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-pappus-area} the area is $2\pi\frac R2\sqrt{R^2 + h^2} = \pi R\sqrt{R^2 + h^2}$. (That the centroid of a triangle is the average of its vertices follows by applying an affine map
taking it to the triangle $(0, 0)$, $(1, 0)$, $(0, 1)$, which changes all areas by the same factor, and computing $\int_0^1\int_0^{1-r}r\,dz\,dr = \frac16 = \frac13 \cdot \frac12$.)
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-shells}
Compute the volume of the solid obtained by rotating the region $\{(r, z) : 0 < r \leq \pi, 0 \leq z \leq \sin r\}$ around the $z$-axis, using shells, and check the result with the Pappus--Guldin theorem.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
By Proposition~\ref{measure-theory:proposition-revolution-volume}(2), with $a \to 0$ by monotone convergence, the volume is $2\pi\int_0^\pi r\sin r\,dr = 2\pi\bigl[\sin r - r\cos r\bigr]_0^\pi = 2\pi^2$. The region has area
$\int_0^\pi\sin r\,dr = 2$, and by symmetry about $r = \frac\pi2$ its centroid has $\bar r = \frac\pi2$; indeed $2\pi \cdot \frac\pi2 \cdot 2 = 2\pi^2$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}\label{measure-theory:exercise-paraboloid}
Show that the solid paraboloid $\{(x, y, z) : x^2 + y^2 \leq z \leq h\}$ has half the volume of the circumscribed cylinder $\{x^2 + y^2 \leq h, 0 \leq z \leq h\}$, a result of Archimedes.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
By the disc method with $f(z) = \sqrt z$ on $[0, h]$, the volume is $\pi\int_0^hz\,dz = \frac12\pi h^2$, and the cylinder of radius $\sqrt h$ and height $h$ has volume $\pi h \cdot h = \pi h^2$.
\end{solution}
